Train / Test Split for Time-Series

Time-series splits cannot be randomized. You must always use the most recent data as the test set — using future data to train a model and testing on the past is data leakage and produces artificially good metrics.

# Use last 20% of data as test set
split_idx = int(len(prices) * 0.80)
train = prices.iloc[:split_idx].copy()
test  = prices.iloc[split_idx:].copy()

print(f"Train: {len(train)} days ({train.index[0].date()} → {train.index[-1].date()})")
print(f"Test:  {len(test)} days ({test.index[0].date()} → {test.index[-1].date()})")

# Visualize the split
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 5))
plt.plot(train.index, train['price'], color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Train')
plt.plot(test.index,  test['price'],  color='tomato',    lw=1.5, label='Test')
plt.axvline(x=train.index[-1], color='gray', linestyle='--', lw=1, label='Split')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — Train/Test Split (80/20)')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('02_train_test_split.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

5.3 Naïve Forecasting AN300 AN306

Naïve forecasting is the simplest possible model and the essential baseline. If your sophisticated model cannot beat the naïve forecast, it is not adding value.

The Naïve Forecast

ŷ_{t+1} = y_t

The prediction for the next period is simply the last observed value. The model assumes the series will stay exactly where it is.

💡 Naïve Forecast in Action — 5 Days of Stock Prices

Apple's close prices for 5 days: Mon $182, Tue $185, Wed $180, Thu $183, Fri $186. The naïve model's predictions:

DayActual PriceNaïve Prediction (= yesterday's close)Error
Mon$182——
Tue$185$182 (Mon's close)−$3
Wed$180$185 (Tue's close)+$5
Thu$183$180 (Wed's close)−$3
Fri$186$183 (Thu's close)−$3

Notice: the naïve model never tries to detect patterns. It just copies yesterday's price every single time. For stocks that follow a random walk (tomorrow's price = today's price + random noise), this is surprisingly hard to beat — any sophisticated model that claims to do better needs to prove it on the test set.

ℹ Why Use Naïve as a Baseline?
For many financial assets, the Naïve model is surprisingly hard to beat in one-step-ahead forecasts because stock prices exhibit random walk behavior. Any model you build should be evaluated by asking: does this beat Naïve? If not, you've over-engineered.

Implementation

# Naïve forecast: each prediction = last observed value
# In practice: test predictions shift the training series by 1

# One-step-ahead naïve on the test period:
# forecast for test[0] = train[-1]; for test[1] = test[0]; etc.
# The easiest implementation: use the entire series shifted by 1

naive_pred = prices['price'].shift(1).dropna()

# Align with test set
naive_test = naive_pred.loc[test.index]

# Plot
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 5))
plt.plot(train.index[-100:], train['price'].iloc[-100:], color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Train (last 100 days)')
plt.plot(test.index,  test['price'],  color='tomato',    lw=1.5, label='Actual (Test)')
plt.plot(test.index,  naive_test,     color='gold',      lw=1.5, linestyle='--', label='Naïve Forecast')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — Naïve Forecast vs. Actual')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()

Seasonal Naïve

A variant: predict using the same period from the previous cycle. For monthly data with annual seasonality, the forecast for January 2025 = January 2024's value:

ŷ_{t+1} = y_{t − m + 1}

Where m = seasonal period (m=12 for monthly, m=5 for weekly trading days).

5.4 Simple Moving Average (SMA) AN300 AN306

A Simple Moving Average (SMA) smooths out short-term noise by averaging the most recent k observations. It tracks the trend without being distracted by day-to-day volatility.

The SMA Formula

SMA_t = (y_t + y_{t-1} + … + y_{t-k+1}) / k

Each period, the window slides forward by one observation — the oldest value drops off and the newest is added.

💡 SMA Sliding Window — Step by Step With Numbers

Using the same 5 Apple prices (Mon $182, Tue $185, Wed $180, Thu $183, Fri $186) with k=3 (3-day window):

DayPriceSMA(3) CalculationSMA(3) Value
Mon$182—NaN (not enough data yet)
Tue$185—NaN (need 3 days)
Wed$180(182 + 185 + 180) / 3$182.33
Thu$183(185 + 180 + 183) / 3$182.67 ← Mon's $182 dropped off, Thu's $183 added
Fri$186(180 + 183 + 186) / 3$183.00 ← Tue's $185 dropped off, Fri's $186 added

The SMA used as a one-step-ahead forecast: to predict Friday's price, you use Thursday's SMA(3) = $182.67 (computed from Mon–Wed data, shifted forward by one day). The actual Friday price is $186, so the error = $3.33. The SMA smoothed out the Wed dip ($180) but it's always slightly behind — it can't react until the new price is already in.

Choosing k (Window Size)

  • Small k (e.g., k=5): Reacts quickly to changes; still noisy; good for short-term signals
  • Large k (e.g., k=50 or k=200): Very smooth; slow to react; reveals long-run trend
  • Common choices in finance: 5-day (1 trading week), 20-day (1 trading month), 50-day, 200-day
⚠ The SMA Lag Problem
An SMA always lags the actual series — it cannot react until the data has already changed. This means it will never catch turning points quickly. The larger k, the more lag. This is SMA's fundamental limitation for forecasting (vs. its use in signal analysis).

Implementation

# Compute multiple SMAs
prices['SMA_5']   = prices['price'].rolling(window=5).mean()
prices['SMA_20']  = prices['price'].rolling(window=20).mean()
prices['SMA_50']  = prices['price'].rolling(window=50).mean()
prices['SMA_200'] = prices['price'].rolling(window=200).mean()

# Plot all SMAs
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 6))
plt.plot(prices.index, prices['price'],   color='steelblue', lw=1,   alpha=0.7, label='Actual Price')
plt.plot(prices.index, prices['SMA_5'],   color='gold',      lw=1.5, label='SMA(5) — 1 week')
plt.plot(prices.index, prices['SMA_20'],  color='orange',    lw=1.5, label='SMA(20) — 1 month')
plt.plot(prices.index, prices['SMA_50'],  color='tomato',    lw=2,   label='SMA(50)')
plt.plot(prices.index, prices['SMA_200'], color='purple',    lw=2,   label='SMA(200)')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — Moving Averages', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
plt.ylabel('Price ($)')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('03_moving_averages.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Using SMA(20) as a one-step-ahead forecast on the test set:
# forecast for each test day = SMA computed through the previous day
sma20_forecast = prices['price'].rolling(window=20).mean().shift(1)
sma20_test = sma20_forecast.loc[test.index]

5.5 Exponential Smoothing AN300 AN306

Exponential Smoothing fixes the SMA's equal-weight problem. Instead of weighting all k periods equally, it gives exponentially decreasing weight to older observations — recent data matters more, but nothing is ever completely forgotten.

Simple Exponential Smoothing (SES)

Forecast: ŷ_{t+1} = α × y_t + (1 − α) × ŷ_t

Expanded: ŷ_{t+1} = α × y_t + α(1−α) × y_{t-1} + α(1−α)² × y_{t-2} + …

Where α (alpha) is the smoothing parameter, 0 < α < 1:

  • α close to 1 (e.g., 0.9): Recent observations dominate; model reacts quickly; similar to Naïve when α=1
  • α close to 0 (e.g., 0.1): Old observations have high influence; model is very smooth and slow to react; similar to SMA with large k when α→0
  • Optimal α is chosen by minimizing SSE on the training data (statsmodels does this automatically)
💡 Plain-English Example: Tracing α Through Actual Prices

Suppose prices over three days were $100, $110, $105, and you want to forecast Day 4.

With α = 0.9 (reacts fast — almost only uses yesterday):
  After Day 1: forecast = $100
  After Day 2: forecast = 0.9 × $110 + 0.1 × $100 = $99 + $10 = $109
  After Day 3: forecast = 0.9 × $105 + 0.1 × $109 = $94.50 + $10.90 = $105.40
Day 4 prediction: ~$105 — closely tracks yesterday's close

With α = 0.1 (slow — still remembering prices from weeks ago):
  After Day 1: forecast = $100
  After Day 2: forecast = 0.1 × $110 + 0.9 × $100 = $11 + $90 = $101
  After Day 3: forecast = 0.1 × $105 + 0.9 × $101 = $10.50 + $90.90 = $101.40
Day 4 prediction: ~$101 — barely moved despite seeing $110 and $105

Use high α when price movements are real signals you want to follow. Use low α when you want to ignore short-term noise.

Holt's Method — Trend-Adjusted Exponential Smoothing

SES has no trend component — it always reverts toward the recent mean. Holt's method adds a second equation to track the trend:

Level: Lₜ = α × yₜ + (1 − α) × (Lₜ₋₁ + Tₜ₋₁) Trend: Tₜ = β × (Lₜ − Lₜ₋₁) + (1 − β) × Tₜ₋₁ Forecast: ŷₜ₊ₕ = Lₜ + h × Tₜ

Where α smooths the level and β smooths the trend. h is the forecast horizon.

Holt-Winters — Trend + Seasonality

Holt-Winters (also called Triple Exponential Smoothing) adds a third equation for seasonality. This is the most general exponential smoothing model:

Level: Lₜ = α × (yₜ / Sₜ₋ₘ) + (1 − α) × (Lₜ₋₁ + Tₜ₋₁) # multiplicative version Trend: Tₜ = β × (Lₜ − Lₜ₋₁) + (1 − β) × Tₜ₋₁ Seasonal: Sₜ = γ × (yₜ / Lₜ) + (1 − γ) × Sₜ₋ₘ Forecast: ŷₜ₊ₕ = (Lₜ + h × Tₜ) × Sₜ₋ₘ₊ₕ
💡 Plain-English Example: What Each Holt-Winters Equation Actually Does

Imagine you run an ice cream shop. Sales grow slowly each year (trend) and spike every summer (seasonality).

Level (Lₜ) — "What are sales like right now, stripped of the seasonal effect?"
If July always sells 2× the monthly average, you divide today's July sales by that 2× seasonal factor to get the "true" base level. Then you blend it with your previous estimate of the base level.

Trend (Tₜ) — "How fast is the base level growing?"
If Level was $5,000 last month and $5,100 this month, the trend grew by $100. Holt-Winters blends this new trend reading with the old trend estimate.

Seasonal (Sₜ) — "What is the seasonal multiplier for this month of year?"
If actual July sales are 2.1× the current level, and last year July was 1.95× the level, the seasonal index updates toward 2.1 — learning that summers are getting relatively bigger.

Forecast — combine them: take the trending level (base + slope × horizon), then multiply by the seasonal factor from the same month last year. The result is a prediction that accounts for both long-term growth and seasonal patterns.

Implementation with statsmodels

from statsmodels.tsa.holtwinters import SimpleExpSmoothing, ExponentialSmoothing

# ── Method 1: Simple Exponential Smoothing ───────────────────────
ses_model = SimpleExpSmoothing(train['price'], initialization_method='estimated').fit()
ses_forecast = ses_model.forecast(len(test))
print(f"SES — Optimal alpha: {ses_model.model.params['smoothing_level']:.4f}")

# ── Method 2: Holt's (trend, no seasonality) ────────────────────
holt_model = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train['price'],
    trend='add',
    seasonal=None,
    initialization_method='estimated'
).fit()
holt_forecast = holt_model.forecast(len(test))

# ── Method 3: Holt-Winters (trend + seasonality) ─────────────────
# Requires seasonal_periods to match your data frequency
# For daily data with ~252 trading days per year, m=252
# For monthly data, m=12; for quarterly, m=4
hw_model = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train['price'],
    trend='add',
    seasonal='add',
    seasonal_periods=252,
    initialization_method='estimated'
).fit()
hw_forecast = hw_model.forecast(len(test))

# ── Compare visually ─────────────────────────────────────────────
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 6))
plt.plot(test.index, test['price'],    color='steelblue', lw=2,   label='Actual')
plt.plot(test.index, ses_forecast,     color='gold',      lw=1.5, linestyle='--', label='SES')
plt.plot(test.index, holt_forecast,    color='orange',    lw=1.5, linestyle='--', label="Holt's")
plt.plot(test.index, hw_forecast,      color='tomato',    lw=1.5, linestyle='--', label='Holt-Winters')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — Exponential Smoothing Methods vs. Actual')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('04_exp_smoothing.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

5.6 Trend & Seasonality with Regression AN300 AN306

AN300 Module 1 covers using OLS regression to model trend and seasonality explicitly. Rather than letting an algorithm adaptively estimate them, you encode them as predictors and let regression estimate their size. This approach is fully interpretable.

Modeling Trend

Trend is modeled by including the time index t as a predictor:

ŷ_t = b₀ + b₁ × t

b₁ is the average change in y per period. If t is measured in days and y is stock price, b₁ = $0.08 means the stock trends up $0.08 per trading day on average.

Modeling Seasonality

Seasonality is encoded as dummy (indicator) variables for each period within the cycle. For monthly seasonality, create 11 monthly dummies (the 12th is the reference). For daily trading data, create weekday dummies (Mon–Thu, with Friday as reference).

Full Trend + Seasonality Regression

import statsmodels.formula.api as smf

# ── Build features on the training set ───────────────────────────
train_reg = train.copy()
train_reg['t'] = np.arange(len(train_reg))

# Monthly seasonality dummies (use month 1 = January as reference)
for m in range(2, 13):
    train_reg[f'month_{m}'] = (train_reg.index.month == m).astype(int)

# Quarter dummies (optional — quarter 1 as reference)
for q in range(2, 5):
    train_reg[f'q_{q}'] = (train_reg.index.quarter == q).astype(int)

# ── Fit model: trend + monthly seasonality ───────────────────────
month_terms = ' + '.join([f'month_{m}' for m in range(2, 13)])
formula = f'price ~ t + {month_terms}'

reg_model = smf.ols(formula, data=train_reg).fit()
print(f"Trend + Seasonality — R²: {reg_model.rsquared:.4f}  Adj R²: {reg_model.rsquared_adj:.4f}")
print(f"Trend coefficient (b₁): {reg_model.params['t']:.4f} $/day")

# ── Build test features and forecast ─────────────────────────────
test_reg = test.copy()
n_train = len(train_reg)
test_reg['t'] = np.arange(n_train, n_train + len(test_reg))

for m in range(2, 13):
    test_reg[f'month_{m}'] = (test_reg.index.month == m).astype(int)

reg_forecast = reg_model.predict(test_reg)

# ── Plot ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 6))
plt.plot(train.index[-200:], train['price'].iloc[-200:], color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Train (last 200)')
plt.plot(test.index, test['price'],  color='steelblue',  lw=2,   alpha=0.5, label='Actual (Test)')
plt.plot(test.index, reg_forecast,   color='tomato',     lw=2,   linestyle='--', label='Trend+Seasonality Regression')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — Regression Forecast (Trend + Monthly Seasonality)')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('05_regression_forecast.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

Polynomial Trend (Non-Linear)

If the trend is curved rather than linear, add t² (and optionally t³):

train_reg['t2'] = train_reg['t'] ** 2
test_reg['t2']  = test_reg['t']  ** 2

poly_formula = f'price ~ t + t2 + {month_terms}'
poly_model   = smf.ols(poly_formula, data=train_reg).fit()
poly_forecast = poly_model.predict(test_reg)

print(f"Polynomial Trend — Adj R²: {poly_model.rsquared_adj:.4f}")

5.7 Error Metrics for Time-Series AN300 AN306

All five error metrics from Part 4 (Section 4.5) apply directly to time-series forecasting. The formulas are identical — the only difference is that your y_actual values are in time order and your y_predicted are forecasts at each time step.

Applying the Metrics

def ts_metrics(actual, forecast, model_name="Model"):
    """Compute all 5 error metrics for a time-series forecast."""
    actual   = np.array(actual)
    forecast = np.array(forecast)
    residuals = actual - forecast

    n    = len(actual)
    SSE  = np.sum(residuals ** 2)
    MSE  = SSE / n
    RMSE = np.sqrt(MSE)
    MAE  = np.mean(np.abs(residuals))
    nonzero = actual != 0
    MAPE = np.mean(np.abs(residuals[nonzero] / actual[nonzero])) * 100
    ss_total = np.sum((actual - np.mean(actual)) ** 2)
    R2   = 1 - SSE / ss_total

    print(f"\\n── {model_name} ──")
    print(f"  SSE  = {SSE:>14,.2f}  (units: y²;  range: 0 → ∞)")
    print(f"  MSE  = {MSE:>14,.2f}  (units: y²;  range: 0 → ∞)")
    print(f"  RMSE = {RMSE:>14,.2f}  (units: y;   range: 0 → ∞)")
    print(f"  MAE  = {MAE:>14,.2f}  (units: y;   range: 0 → ∞)")
    print(f"  MAPE = {MAPE:>13.2f}%  (unitless;   range: 0 → ∞)")
    print(f"  R²   = {R2:>14.4f}  (unitless;   range: 0 → 1)")
    return {'SSE':SSE,'MSE':MSE,'RMSE':RMSE,'MAE':MAE,'MAPE':MAPE,'R2':R2}

actual = test['price'].values

# Align naïve, SMA forecasts to test index first (handle NaN from rolling)
naïve_aligned = naive_test.values
sma20_aligned = sma20_test.values

m_naive  = ts_metrics(actual, naïve_aligned,              "Naïve Forecast")
m_sma    = ts_metrics(actual, sma20_aligned,              "SMA(20)")
m_ses    = ts_metrics(actual, ses_forecast.values,        "Simple Exp. Smoothing")
m_holt   = ts_metrics(actual, holt_forecast.values,       "Holt's Method")
m_hw     = ts_metrics(actual, hw_forecast.values,         "Holt-Winters")
m_reg    = ts_metrics(actual, reg_forecast.values,        "Regression (Trend+Season)")

Building the Model Comparison Table

comparison = pd.DataFrame([m_naive, m_sma, m_ses, m_holt, m_hw, m_reg],
    index=['Naïve','SMA(20)','SES','Holt','Holt-Winters','Regression'])
comparison = comparison.round({'SSE':0,'MSE':2,'RMSE':2,'MAE':2,'MAPE':2,'R2':4})

print("\\n═══ MODEL COMPARISON TABLE ═══")
print(comparison.to_string())
print("\\nBest model by RMSE:", comparison['RMSE'].idxmin())
print("Best model by MAPE:", comparison['MAPE'].idxmin())
print("Best model by R²:  ", comparison['R2'].idxmax())
🎯 Exam Trap — RMSE Range (Repeated for Time-Series Context)
RMSE for a stock price forecast might be $14.73. That is not "14.73 out of 1" — it means your forecasts are off by roughly $14.73 per day on average. RMSE is 0 to ∞. If the stock trades at $150 and RMSE is $14.73, your MAPE would be roughly 10% — MAPE gives the scale-independent view.

5.8 SARIMA — Theory AN306 Docx

SARIMA (Seasonal AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average) is the most flexible classical time-series model. It combines three mechanisms — autoregression, differencing, and moving average — with a seasonal layer on top. AN306 Chapter 3 and the P3 guide both cover SARIMA as the capstone forecasting method.

Building Up to SARIMA

AR (AutoRegressive) — p

An AR(p) model predicts y_t as a linear combination of the p most recent values:

y_t = c + φ₁y_{t-1} + φ₂y_{t-2} + … + φₚy_{t-p} + ε_t

φ (phi) are the autoregressive coefficients. High autocorrelation in a series → AR terms are needed. The ACF (Autocorrelation Function) plot reveals how many lags to include.

I (Integrated) — d

Most real-world time series are non-stationary — their mean and/or variance changes over time. A stock price trending upward is non-stationary. ARIMA differencing (d) subtracts each value from the previous to remove the trend:

y'_t = y_t − y_{t-1} (first difference, d=1) y''_t = y'_t − y'_{t-1} (second difference, d=2)

d=1 removes a linear trend; d=2 removes a quadratic trend. Most series need d=0 or d=1.

MA (Moving Average) — q

An MA(q) model predicts y_t using the q most recent forecast errors:

y_t = c + ε_t + θ₁ε_{t-1} + θ₂ε_{t-2} + … + θ_qε_{t-q}

θ (theta) are the moving average coefficients. The ACF (Autocorrelation Function) plot reveals how many MA terms to include — the lag at which ACF cuts off is q.

💡 Plain-English Example: What Are "Forecast Errors"?

An MA model doesn't use past prices directly — it uses past mistakes. Here's what that means:

Suppose on Monday you predicted Tuesday's price would be $105, but it came in at $110. Your forecast error was +$5 (you under-predicted by $5).

An MA(1) model says: "My prediction today is the baseline (c) plus a correction based on how wrong I was yesterday."

If θ₁ = 0.8, then today's forecast = c + 0.8 × (+$5) = c + $4. You're nudging today's forecast upward because yesterday's actual was higher than expected.

This is powerful for capturing short bursts of unexpected movement — like a one-day news event that briefly pushed prices up. The error term captures that shock and fades it out over q periods.

ARIMA(p, d, q)

Combining all three: ARIMA(p, d, q) = AR(p) + I(d) + MA(q). It models the differenced series as an autoregressive moving-average process.

Seasonal SARIMA(p, d, q)(P, D, Q)[m]

SARIMA adds a second set of seasonal parameters — capitalized to distinguish from the non-seasonal (regular) layer:

ParameterMeaningApplies to
pNon-seasonal AR orderShort-term autoregression
dNon-seasonal differencingRemove non-seasonal trend
qNon-seasonal MA orderShort-term error correction
PSeasonal AR orderYear-over-year autoregression
DSeasonal differencingRemove seasonal pattern
QSeasonal MA orderSeasonal error correction
mSeasonal period12 for monthly; 4 for quarterly; 252 for daily
💡 Reading SARIMA(1,1,1)(0,1,1)[12] Aloud — What Each Number Means

Say you see SARIMA(1, 1, 1)(0, 1, 1)[12] for monthly retail sales data. Here's how to decode it in plain English:

NumberWhat It IsWhat It Does
p = 1Non-seasonal AR(1)Today's value depends on yesterday's value directly
d = 1Non-seasonal differencingTake first differences (remove the trend — the series was drifting upward)
q = 1Non-seasonal MA(1)Adjust for how wrong we were last month
P = 0Seasonal AR(0)No year-over-year autoregression
D = 1Seasonal differencingSubtract last year's same month (removes annual seasonality — December always spikes)
Q = 1Seasonal MA(1)Adjust for how wrong we were in the same month last year
m = 12Seasonal periodThe cycle repeats every 12 months (monthly data, annual seasonality)

In one sentence: "This model uses yesterday's value and last month's error to predict today, after removing both a linear upward trend AND a December-spike pattern." That's the story behind the seven numbers.

Starting point for most AN306 problems: ARIMA(1,1,1) with no seasonal layer is a sensible first guess; auto_arima will refine it. For monthly data with clear annual patterns, try (1,1,1)(0,1,1)[12].

How to Choose Parameters (p, d, q)

Step 1 — Check Stationarity
Plot the series. If it trends, d=1. Run the ADF test to confirm: p-value > 0.05 → non-stationary → difference. p-value < 0.05 → stationary → d=0.
Step 2 — Plot ACF and PACF
After differencing, plot ACF (q) and PACF (p). ACF cuts off at lag q → MA(q). PACF cuts off at lag p → AR(p). Both tail off → both AR and MA terms needed.
💡 Memory Hook: Which Plot Gives p and Which Gives q?

This is the most commonly confused ARIMA concept. Here's the trick:

PACF → p (AR order) — "P for PACF, P for ARP(p)"
PACF measures the direct relationship between today and each past day, after removing the indirect links. If PACF has a sharp cutoff at lag 3, your time series "directly" depends on the last 3 days → AR(3), p=3.

ACF → q (MA order) — "ACF gives q for MA(q)"
ACF measures total correlation (direct + indirect). If ACF cuts off sharply at lag 2, only the last 2 error terms matter → MA(2), q=2.

Mnemonic: "PACF is Picky — it only counts direct connections" (→ p). "ACF counts All Correlations" (→ q).

In practice: if only PACF cuts off → pure AR. If only ACF cuts off → pure MA. If both tail off slowly → ARMA (need both p and q terms).

Step 3 — Fit candidate models
Try a small grid of (p, q) combinations. Compare AIC/BIC. Lower is better.
Step 4 — Check residuals
Fit is acceptable when residuals look like white noise: ACF/PACF of residuals shows no significant spikes; Ljung-Box p-value > 0.05.

5.9 SARIMA — Implementation AN306 Docx

Stationarity Check — ADF Test

from statsmodels.tsa.stattools import adfuller

def adf_test(series, name="Series"):
    result = adfuller(series.dropna(), autolag='AIC')
    print(f"\\nADF Test: {name}")
    print(f"  ADF Statistic : {result[0]:.4f}")
    print(f"  p-value       : {result[1]:.4f}")
    print(f"  Critical 5%   : {result[4]['5%']:.4f}")
    if result[1] < 0.05:
        print("  → STATIONARY (p < 0.05) — no differencing needed")
    else:
        print("  → NON-STATIONARY (p ≥ 0.05) — apply first difference (d=1)")
    return result[1]

adf_test(train['price'],          'Price Level (d=0)')
adf_test(train['price'].diff(),   'First Difference (d=1)')
💡 Plain-English Example: What Is Stationarity?

A series is stationary if its statistical properties (mean, variance) stay constant over time — it has no overall upward or downward trend and doesn't become more or less volatile over time.

Non-stationary example: Apple's stock price from 2010 to 2024. The mean keeps rising ($10 → $200 → $180). If you measured the average price in 2012 it was ~$80; in 2022 it was ~$160. The mean is not constant. ARIMA can't model this directly — it needs you to "difference" the series first.

Stationary after differencing: Instead of using the price itself, use the daily change: (+$2, −$1, +$3, −$0.50, …). Daily changes do fluctuate around a relatively stable mean near zero, regardless of whether the price is $80 or $160. That's what d=1 achieves.

ADF test intuition: The test checks "is there a unit root?" — a statistical way of asking "does this series drift systematically over time?" A p-value below 0.05 means the drift is NOT statistically significant → stationary. Counterintuitively, you want a low p-value here.

ACF and PACF Plots

from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_acf, plot_pacf

# Apply first difference if needed
price_diff = train['price'].diff().dropna()

fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 2, figsize=(14, 8))

# Level series ACF/PACF
plot_acf(train['price'],  lags=40, ax=axes[0,0], title='ACF — Price Level')
plot_pacf(train['price'], lags=40, ax=axes[0,1], title='PACF — Price Level')

# Differenced series ACF/PACF
plot_acf(price_diff,  lags=40, ax=axes[1,0], title='ACF — First Difference')
plot_pacf(price_diff, lags=40, ax=axes[1,1], title='PACF — First Difference')

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_acf_pacf.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Reading the plots:
# ACF: spikes above the blue band = significant autocorrelation at that lag
# PACF: cut-off at lag p → AR(p) suggests p AR terms
# ACF: cut-off at lag q → MA(q) suggests q MA terms

Auto-ARIMA — Finding the Best Parameters Automatically

# Install pmdarima for auto_arima (auto-selects p,d,q by AIC)
!pip install pmdarima --quiet

from pmdarima import auto_arima

# For daily stock prices: no strong annual seasonality in short windows
# Try ARIMA first (no seasonal layer), then add seasonal if needed
arima_model = auto_arima(
    train['price'],
    start_p=0, start_q=0,
    max_p=5,   max_q=5,
    d=None,              # auto-detect d via ADF test
    seasonal=False,      # no seasonal layer for daily prices
    information_criterion='aic',
    stepwise=True,
    suppress_warnings=True,
    trace=True           # print the search progress
)

print(f"\\nBest model: ARIMA{arima_model.order}")
print(arima_model.summary())

Fitting SARIMA with statsmodels (Manual)

from statsmodels.tsa.statespace.sarimax import SARIMAX

# SARIMA(p, d, q)(P, D, Q)[m]
# For daily stock prices, a simple ARIMA(1,1,1) is a common starting point
sarima_model = SARIMAX(
    train['price'],
    order=(1, 1, 1),          # (p, d, q) — non-seasonal
    seasonal_order=(0, 0, 0, 0),  # no seasonal layer for daily
    enforce_stationarity=False,
    enforce_invertibility=False
).fit(disp=False)

print(sarima_model.summary())

# Forecast on the test set
sarima_forecast_obj = sarima_model.get_forecast(steps=len(test))
sarima_forecast     = sarima_forecast_obj.predicted_mean
conf_int            = sarima_forecast_obj.conf_int()

# Plot with confidence interval
plt.figure(figsize=(14, 6))
plt.plot(train.index[-150:], train['price'].iloc[-150:], color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Train')
plt.plot(test.index,  test['price'],      color='steelblue', lw=2, alpha=0.5, label='Actual')
plt.plot(test.index,  sarima_forecast,    color='tomato',    lw=2, label='SARIMA Forecast')
plt.fill_between(test.index,
                 conf_int.iloc[:,0], conf_int.iloc[:,1],
                 color='tomato', alpha=0.15, label='95% Confidence Interval')
plt.title(f'{ticker} — SARIMA(1,1,1) Forecast')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('07_sarima_forecast.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

m_sarima = ts_metrics(test['price'].values, sarima_forecast.values, "SARIMA(1,1,1)")

SARIMA Diagnostic Plots

# Check that residuals are white noise (required for a valid SARIMA fit)
sarima_model.plot_diagnostics(figsize=(14, 8))
plt.suptitle('SARIMA Residual Diagnostics', fontsize=16, fontweight='bold')
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('08_sarima_diagnostics.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# Ljung-Box test on residuals (want p-value > 0.05)
from statsmodels.stats.diagnostic import acorr_ljungbox
lb_test = acorr_ljungbox(sarima_model.resid, lags=[10, 20], return_df=True)
print("\\nLjung-Box Test on SARIMA Residuals:")
print(lb_test)
print("\\nIf p-value > 0.05: residuals are white noise → model is adequate")

5.10 Choosing a Forecast Model AN300 AN306

With six models (Naïve, SMA, SES, Holt, Holt-Winters, Regression, SARIMA), you need a principled decision framework. This module gives you the step-by-step selection logic used in AN300 and AN306.

Decision Framework

QuestionAnswer → Model Family
Is there a clear trend?Yes → Holt's, Holt-Winters, Regression, or SARIMA with d≥1
Is there clear seasonality?Yes → Holt-Winters (multiplicative if grows with trend), Regression + seasonal dummies, SARIMA with D≥1
No trend, no seasonality (stationary)SES, SMA, SARIMA with d=0
Need a simple interpretable benchmark?Naïve or SMA; always compute as a baseline
Need the most flexible statistical model?SARIMA — but requires stationarity checks and ACF/PACF analysis
Need to explain "how much does January matter?"Regression with seasonal dummies — coefficients are directly interpretable

The Selection Rule

  1. Always compute the Naïve baseline first
  2. Visually inspect the series: trend? seasonality? outliers?
  3. Fit candidate models appropriate for the observed patterns
  4. Compare all models using RMSE and MAPE on the test set (not training!)
  5. Select the model with the best test-set metrics — lower RMSE and lower MAPE
  6. Check residuals of the selected model — if they show patterns, the model is missing something
✓ For P3: What to Report
Your P3 presentation must show: (1) the raw price plot with the train/test split labeled, (2) a model comparison table with all 5 metrics, (3) the best model's forecast plotted against actuals, (4) a discussion of why that model outperformed the others, and (5) any limitations (e.g., SARIMA residuals not white noise = model could be improved).

5.11 P3 Stock Forecast Walkthrough AN306 Docx

This module assembles the complete P3 pipeline into a single, submission-ready notebook structure. Every cell below maps to a P3 deliverable.

Recommended Notebook Structure

### ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
### AN 306 — Presentation 3: Stock Price Forecasting
### Student: [Your Name] | Ticker: [TICKER] | Date: [Date]
### ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

# ── CELL 1: Setup ────────────────────────────────────────────────
!pip install yfinance pmdarima --quiet
import yfinance as yf
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import warnings
warnings.filterwarnings('ignore')

from statsmodels.tsa.holtwinters import ExponentialSmoothing, SimpleExpSmoothing
from statsmodels.tsa.statespace.sarimax import SARIMAX
from statsmodels.tsa.stattools import adfuller
from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_acf, plot_pacf
import statsmodels.formula.api as smf

TICKER    = 'MSFT'   # ← your stock
START     = '2019-01-01'
END       = '2024-12-31'
SPLIT_PCT = 0.80
# ── CELL 2: Data Download & Preparation ─────────────────────────
raw = yf.download(TICKER, start=START, end=END, auto_adjust=True)
prices = raw[['Close']].rename(columns={'Close': 'price'}).dropna()

split_idx = int(len(prices) * SPLIT_PCT)
train = prices.iloc[:split_idx].copy()
test  = prices.iloc[split_idx:].copy()

print(f"Ticker: {TICKER}")
print(f"Train: {len(train)} days | Test: {len(test)} days")
print(f"Price range: \${prices['price'].min():.2f} – \${prices['price'].max():.2f}")

# ── CELL 3: Visualization — Raw Series + Split ───────────────────
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(14, 5))
ax.plot(train.index, train['price'], color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Training Period')
ax.plot(test.index,  test['price'],  color='tomato',    lw=1.5, label='Test Period (Hold-out)')
ax.axvline(x=train.index[-1], color='gray', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, alpha=0.8)
ax.set_title(f'{TICKER} Adjusted Close Price — Train/Test Split', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_ylabel('Price ($)')
ax.legend(fontsize=11)
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('p3_01_raw_series.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()
# ── CELL 4: All Forecasts ────────────────────────────────────────
n_test = len(test)

# 1. Naïve
naive_pred = prices['price'].shift(1).loc[test.index]

# 2. SMA(20)
sma_pred = prices['price'].rolling(20).mean().shift(1).loc[test.index]

# 3. Holt's (trend, no seasonality) — good for trending stocks
holt = ExponentialSmoothing(train['price'], trend='add',
                            initialization_method='estimated').fit()
holt_pred = holt.forecast(n_test)

# 4. Holt-Winters (trend + seasonality)
try:
    hw = ExponentialSmoothing(train['price'], trend='add', seasonal='add',
                              seasonal_periods=252,
                              initialization_method='estimated').fit()
    hw_pred = hw.forecast(n_test)
except Exception:
    hw_pred = holt_pred   # fallback if seasonal model fails

# 5. Regression (trend + monthly seasonality)
train_reg = train.copy()
train_reg['t'] = np.arange(len(train_reg))
for m in range(2, 13):
    train_reg[f'm{m}'] = (train_reg.index.month == m).astype(int)
month_terms = ' + '.join([f'm{m}' for m in range(2, 13)])
reg = smf.ols(f'price ~ t + {month_terms}', data=train_reg).fit()

test_reg = test.copy()
test_reg['t'] = np.arange(len(train_reg), len(train_reg) + n_test)
for m in range(2, 13):
    test_reg[f'm{m}'] = (test_reg.index.month == m).astype(int)
reg_pred = reg.predict(test_reg)

# 6. SARIMA — auto-select
from pmdarima import auto_arima
auto = auto_arima(train['price'], seasonal=False, stepwise=True,
                  suppress_warnings=True, error_action='ignore')
sarima_fit = SARIMAX(train['price'],
                     order=auto.order,
                     seasonal_order=(0,0,0,0),
                     enforce_stationarity=False).fit(disp=False)
sarima_pred = sarima_fit.get_forecast(n_test).predicted_mean
# ── CELL 5: Error Metrics Table ─────────────────────────────────
def ts_metrics_row(actual, forecast, name):
    r = actual - forecast
    n = len(r)
    SSE  = np.sum(r**2)
    RMSE = np.sqrt(SSE/n)
    MAE  = np.mean(np.abs(r))
    nz   = actual != 0
    MAPE = np.mean(np.abs(r[nz]/actual[nz]))*100
    R2   = 1 - SSE/np.sum((actual - actual.mean())**2)
    return {'Model':name, 'RMSE':round(RMSE,2), 'MAE':round(MAE,2),
            'MAPE':round(MAPE,2), 'R²':round(R2,4)}

act = test['price'].values

results = pd.DataFrame([
    ts_metrics_row(act, naive_pred.values,   'Naïve'),
    ts_metrics_row(act, sma_pred.values,     'SMA(20)'),
    ts_metrics_row(act, holt_pred.values,    "Holt's"),
    ts_metrics_row(act, hw_pred.values,      'Holt-Winters'),
    ts_metrics_row(act, reg_pred.values,     'Regression'),
    ts_metrics_row(act, sarima_pred.values,  f'SARIMA{auto.order}'),
])
results = results.sort_values('RMSE').reset_index(drop=True)
print("═══ MODEL COMPARISON ═══")
print(results.to_string(index=False))
print(f"\\n✓ Best Model: {results.iloc[0]['Model']}  (RMSE={results.iloc[0]['RMSE']}, MAPE={results.iloc[0]['MAPE']}%)")
# ── CELL 6: Best Model Plot ──────────────────────────────────────
best_name  = results.iloc[0]['Model']
best_preds = {'Naïve':naive_pred.values, "SMA(20)":sma_pred.values,
              "Holt's":holt_pred.values, 'Holt-Winters':hw_pred.values,
              'Regression':reg_pred.values, f'SARIMA{auto.order}':sarima_pred.values}
best_arr = best_preds[best_name]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(14, 5))
ax.plot(train.index[-100:], train['price'].iloc[-100:],
        color='steelblue', lw=1.5, label='Historical (last 100 days)')
ax.plot(test.index, test['price'],
        color='steelblue', lw=2, alpha=0.5, label='Actual (Test)')
ax.plot(test.index, best_arr,
        color='tomato', lw=2, linestyle='--', label=f'Best: {best_name}')
ax.set_title(f'{TICKER} Forecast — Best Model: {best_name}', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_ylabel('Price ($)')
ax.legend(fontsize=11)
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('p3_02_best_forecast.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()
