Part 6 — Clustering from Zero

6.1 Why Clustering Exists AN306 AN300

All of the methods in Parts 4 and 5 were supervised — there was a labeled target variable y (salary, stock price, loan default) that the model learned to predict. Clustering is fundamentally different. It is unsupervised: there is no y. There is no right answer. There is no ground truth to compare against.

Instead, the goal is to discover natural groupings in the data — patterns the data exhibits on its own. If you have 10,000 job postings with salary, required skills, and location, clustering can reveal that some postings cluster together at high salary + cloud skills + urban, while others cluster at mid salary + Excel + suburban. No one told the algorithm what "types" to look for — it found them.

The Core Intuition

Good clusters have two properties simultaneously:

  1. High intra-cluster similarity — observations within the same cluster are close to each other (similar skill profiles, similar salary bands)
  2. High inter-cluster dissimilarity — observations in different clusters are far from each other (the data science cluster looks very different from the operations cluster)

What Clustering Is Used For

  • Customer segmentation — group buyers by behavior to target marketing
  • Job market analysis — AN306 P4: cluster job postings to discover labor market segments
  • Document clustering — group articles or customer support tickets by topic (leads into NLP, Part 8)
  • Anomaly detection — points that don't fit any cluster are outliers (DBSCAN, Module 6.10)
  • Compression / summarization — represent many points by their cluster centroids

Supervised vs. Unsupervised

PropertySupervised (Regression, Logistic)Unsupervised (Clustering)
Target variable (y)Required — labeled examplesNone — no labels
EvaluationCompare ŷ to y (R², RMSE, accuracy)Internal metrics only (inertia, silhouette)
OutputA predicted value or probabilityGroup assignment (cluster ID)
GoalPredict future y from new xDescribe structure hidden in x
ℹ Cluster Labels Are Arbitrary
When K-Means assigns observations to "Cluster 0", "Cluster 1", and "Cluster 2", those numbers mean nothing on their own — cluster 0 and cluster 1 could be swapped and the algorithm would be equally valid. What matters is which observations are grouped together, not what number the group got. You will always need to profile and interpret each cluster after fitting (Module 6.12).

6.2 K-Means — The Algorithm AN306 AN300

K-Means is the most widely used clustering algorithm. Its goal is to partition n observations into K clusters so that each observation belongs to the cluster with the nearest mean (centroid), minimizing the total within-cluster variation.

The Objective: Minimize Inertia

Inertia = Σₖ Σ_{xᵢ ∈ Cₖ} ||xᵢ − μₖ||²

Where Cₖ is cluster k, xᵢ is a data point, and μₖ is the centroid (mean) of cluster k. ||xᵢ − μₖ||² is the squared Euclidean distance from each point to its cluster's centroid. Lower inertia = tighter, better-defined clusters.

💡 Inertia by Hand

Suppose Cluster 1 has centroid μ₁ = (50, 5) and two assigned points: A = (52, 6) and B = (48, 4).

Distance² from A to centroid: (52−50)² + (6−5)² = 4 + 1 = 5
Distance² from B to centroid: (48−50)² + (4−5)² = 4 + 1 = 5
Cluster 1's contribution to inertia: 5 + 5 = 10

Add up these contributions across all clusters to get total inertia. Lower = points are sitting tighter around their centroids = better-defined clusters.

The K-Means Algorithm Step by Step

Step 1 — Initialize: Place K centroids randomly

Choose K starting centroid positions. The simplest method picks K data points at random. (K-Means++ uses a smarter initialization — Module 6.6.)

Step 2 — Assignment: Assign each point to its nearest centroid

For every observation xᵢ, compute the Euclidean distance to all K centroids. Assign xᵢ to the cluster whose centroid is closest:

cᵢ = argmin_k ||xᵢ − μₖ||²
💡 "Nearest Centroid" in Practice

Say you have 2 centroids: μ₁ = (1, 1) and μ₂ = (8, 8). A new point x = (3, 2) needs to be assigned.

Distance² to μ₁: (3−1)² + (2−1)² = 4 + 1 = 5
Distance² to μ₂: (3−8)² + (2−8)² = 25 + 36 = 61

5 < 61, so x joins Cluster 1. That's Step 2 — repeat this for every single point in the dataset.

Step 3 — Update: Recompute each centroid

After all points are assigned, recalculate each centroid as the mean of all points currently assigned to it:

μₖ = (1/|Cₖ|) Σ_{xᵢ ∈ Cₖ} xᵢ
Step 4 — Repeat: Iterate until convergence

Go back to Step 2 and re-assign all points to the new centroids. Continue until assignments stop changing (or the maximum number of iterations is reached).

💡 K-Means By Hand — 6 Points, K=2, One Full Iteration

Say you have 6 job postings with just one feature: salary (in $10k units for simplicity).

Points: A=3, B=4, C=4.5, D=8, E=9, F=10

Step 1 — Random init: Pick K=2 random centroids. Say μ₁=4 (near point B), μ₂=9 (near point E).

Step 2 — Assignment: Each point goes to the nearest centroid.

PointValueDistance to μ₁=4Distance to μ₂=9Assigned to
A3|3−4|=1|3−9|=6Cluster 1 ✓
B4|4−4|=0|4−9|=5Cluster 1 ✓
C4.5|4.5−4|=0.5|4.5−9|=4.5Cluster 1 ✓
D8|8−4|=4|8−9|=1Cluster 2 ✓
E9|9−4|=5|9−9|=0Cluster 2 ✓
F10|10−4|=6|10−9|=1Cluster 2 ✓

Step 3 — Update centroids: Recalculate the mean of each cluster.

New μ₁ = mean(A, B, C) = (3 + 4 + 4.5) / 3 = 3.83
New μ₂ = mean(D, E, F) = (8 + 9 + 10) / 3 = 9.0

Step 4 — Repeat: Re-assign with the new centroids. The assignments don't change → converged in 1 iteration! Final clusters: {A, B, C} = "lower salary" and {D, E, F} = "higher salary."

In real data with many features, this same logic runs in multiple dimensions, using Euclidean distance instead of absolute difference.

Convergence

K-Means is guaranteed to converge — the inertia decreases monotonically with each iteration. However, it converges to a local minimum, not necessarily the global minimum. The solution depends on the initial centroid placement. This is why scikit-learn runs K-Means multiple times (n_init times) with different random initializations and keeps the best result.

Euclidean Distance

K-Means uses Euclidean distance — the straight-line distance between two points in feature space:

d(a, b) = √((a₁−b₁)² + (a₂−b₂)² + … + (aₙ−bₙ)²)

This means features measured in large units (e.g., salary in dollars: 50,000–200,000) will dominate distance calculations over features measured in small units (e.g., years of experience: 0–20). Feature scaling is mandatory before K-Means. (Module 6.7.)

⚠ K-Means Assumptions
K-Means works best when clusters are roughly spherical, similar in size, and similar in density. It struggles with elongated clusters, clusters of very different sizes, and irregularly shaped clusters. DBSCAN (Module 6.10) handles those cases better.

6.3 K-Means — Implementation AN306

Setup and Data Loading

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.metrics import silhouette_score, adjusted_rand_score
import warnings
warnings.filterwarnings('ignore')

# ── Load dataset (P4 uses job posting data with salary + skills) ──
# For this tutorial, we build a synthetic job market dataset
np.random.seed(42)
n = 500

data = pd.DataFrame({
    'salary':    np.concatenate([
                     np.random.normal(55000, 10000, 150),   # entry-level
                     np.random.normal(90000, 15000, 200),   # mid-level
                     np.random.normal(145000, 20000, 150)   # senior
                 ]),
    'years_exp': np.concatenate([
                     np.random.normal(1.5, 1, 150),
                     np.random.normal(5, 2, 200),
                     np.random.normal(12, 3, 150)
                 ]),
    'skill_score': np.concatenate([
                     np.random.normal(30, 10, 150),
                     np.random.normal(55, 12, 200),
                     np.random.normal(80, 10, 150)
                 ])
})

print(f"Dataset shape: {data.shape}")
print(data.describe().round(1))

Scaling and Initial Fit

# ── ALWAYS scale before clustering ───────────────────────────────
scaler = StandardScaler()
X_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(data)
X_scaled_df = pd.DataFrame(X_scaled, columns=data.columns)

# ── Fit K-Means with K=3 ──────────────────────────────────────────
kmeans = KMeans(
    n_clusters=3,
    init='k-means++',      # smart initialization (Module 6.6)
    n_init=10,             # run 10 times, keep best result
    max_iter=300,          # max iterations per run
    random_state=42        # reproducibility
)

kmeans.fit(X_scaled)

# Add cluster labels to original (unscaled) dataframe
data['cluster'] = kmeans.labels_

print(f"\\nCluster assignments: {np.unique(kmeans.labels_)}")
print(f"Cluster sizes:")
print(data['cluster'].value_counts().sort_index())

Inspecting the Model

# Inertia: total sum of squared distances from each point to its centroid
print(f"Inertia: {kmeans.inertia_:.2f}")

# Centroids in scaled space
centroids_scaled = kmeans.cluster_centers_
print("\\nCentroids (scaled space):")
print(pd.DataFrame(centroids_scaled, columns=data.columns[:-1]).round(3))

# Centroids in original units (inverse transform)
centroids_original = scaler.inverse_transform(centroids_scaled)
centroids_df = pd.DataFrame(centroids_original, columns=data.columns[:-1])
centroids_df.index.name = 'cluster'
print("\\nCentroids (original units):")
print(centroids_df.round(0))

# Number of iterations until convergence
print(f"\\nConverged in {kmeans.n_iter_} iterations")

Visualizing Clusters

colors = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(14, 5))

# Panel 1: Salary vs Years Experience
for k in range(3):
    mask = data['cluster'] == k
    axes[0].scatter(data.loc[mask, 'years_exp'],
                    data.loc[mask, 'salary'],
                    c=colors[k], alpha=0.6, s=30, label=f'Cluster {k}')
axes[0].scatter(centroids_original[:,1], centroids_original[:,0],
                c='white', marker='X', s=200, zorder=5, edgecolors='gray', label='Centroids')
axes[0].set_xlabel('Years of Experience')
axes[0].set_ylabel('Salary ($)')
axes[0].set_title('K-Means Clusters — Salary vs Experience')
axes[0].legend()
axes[0].grid(True, alpha=0.3)

# Panel 2: Salary vs Skill Score
for k in range(3):
    mask = data['cluster'] == k
    axes[1].scatter(data.loc[mask, 'skill_score'],
                    data.loc[mask, 'salary'],
                    c=colors[k], alpha=0.6, s=30, label=f'Cluster {k}')
axes[1].scatter(centroids_original[:,2], centroids_original[:,0],
                c='white', marker='X', s=200, zorder=5, edgecolors='gray')
axes[1].set_xlabel('Skill Score')
axes[1].set_ylabel('Salary ($)')
axes[1].set_title('K-Means Clusters — Salary vs Skill Score')
axes[1].legend()
axes[1].grid(True, alpha=0.3)

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_01_kmeans_clusters.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

6.4 Choosing K — The Elbow Method AN306 AN300

K is the single most important hyperparameter in K-Means and must be specified before fitting. You don't always know the right K. The elbow method is the standard approach for selecting K based on inertia.

How the Elbow Method Works

The idea is simple: fit K-Means for K = 1, 2, 3, …, up to some maximum. Plot inertia (total within-cluster sum of squares) on the y-axis against K on the x-axis. As K increases, inertia always decreases — adding more clusters can always reduce within-cluster variation. But the rate of decrease slows.

The "elbow" is the point where the inertia curve bends sharply — the point of diminishing returns. Before the elbow, adding one more cluster reduces inertia a lot. After the elbow, adding more clusters gives smaller and smaller improvements. The elbow point is the suggested optimal K.

💡 Reading the Elbow: A Concrete Example

Suppose you get these inertia values as K increases:

KInertiaDrop from previous K
11,200—
2650−550 (big drop!)
3340−310 (big drop!)
4310−30 (small)
5295−15 (tiny)

The elbow is at K=3. Going from K=3 to K=4 only saves 30 units of inertia — not worth the added complexity. After K=3 you're just splitting natural groups into smaller pieces for diminishing gains.

Implementation

# ── Compute inertia for K = 1 to 12 ─────────────────────────────
inertias = []
K_range = range(1, 13)

for k in K_range:
    km = KMeans(n_clusters=k, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
    km.fit(X_scaled)
    inertias.append(km.inertia_)

# ── Plot the Elbow Curve ─────────────────────────────────────────
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))
ax.plot(K_range, inertias, 'o-', color='steelblue', lw=2, ms=8)
ax.axvline(x=3, color='tomato', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label='Elbow at K=3')
ax.set_xlabel('Number of Clusters (K)', fontsize=12)
ax.set_ylabel('Inertia (Within-Cluster SSE)', fontsize=12)
ax.set_title('Elbow Method for Optimal K', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_xticks(range(1, 13))
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_02_elbow.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

print("K → Inertia:")
for k, sse in zip(K_range, inertias):
    drop = '' if k == 1 else f'  Δ = {inertias[k-2] - sse:,.1f}'
    print(f"  K={k}: {sse:>10,.1f}{drop}")
💡 Reading the Elbow — Actual Inertia Numbers

Here's what a real inertia table might look like for the job market dataset:

KInertiaDrop from K−1Drop slowing?
11,450——
2620−830No — huge drop
3285−335No — still big
4230−55Yes — small drop ← elbow here
5195−35Yes — diminishing
6170−25Yes — diminishing

The elbow is at K=3 → K=4: inertia dropped by 830, then 335, then only 55. That sudden slowdown (from −335 to −55) is the bend in the curve. The model already captured most of the natural structure at K=3; adding a 4th cluster buys very little. K=3 is the answer.

In code, print(f"K={k}: {sse:>10,.1f} Δ={drop:,.1f}") gives you this table instantly — read the Δ column to spot where the drops plateau.

⚠ The Elbow Is Sometimes Ambiguous
Real-world data often has a gradual curve with no sharp elbow. When that happens, use the elbow method to narrow the range (e.g., "K is probably between 3 and 6"), then use silhouette scores (Module 6.5) to choose the best within that range. Never rely on just one method.

Computing Percentage of Variance Explained

# An alternative view: % of variance explained as K increases
total_sse = KMeans(n_clusters=1, random_state=42).fit(X_scaled).inertia_
var_explained = [(1 - sse/total_sse)*100 for sse in inertias]

for k, pct in zip(K_range, var_explained):
    print(f"K={k}: {pct:.1f}% variance explained")

6.5 Silhouette Analysis AN306

The silhouette score measures how well-separated clusters are by considering both the within-cluster cohesion and the between-cluster separation for each point. It is a richer evaluation than inertia alone.

The Silhouette Score Formula

For each point i: a(i) = mean distance from point i to all other points in its cluster (cohesion) b(i) = mean distance from point i to all points in the NEAREST other cluster (separation) Silhouette score for point i: s(i) = (b(i) − a(i)) / max(a(i), b(i)) Overall silhouette score = mean of s(i) over all points
💡 Silhouette by Hand

Say point P is in Cluster A. The 3 other points in its cluster are at distances 1.0, 1.2, and 0.8 from P. The nearest other cluster (B) has points at distances 2.5, 3.0, and 2.8 from P.

a(P) = average distance within cluster = (1.0 + 1.2 + 0.8) / 3 = 1.0
b(P) = average distance to nearest other cluster = (2.5 + 3.0 + 2.8) / 3 = 2.77

s(P) = (2.77 − 1.0) / max(1.0, 2.77) = 1.77 / 2.77 = 0.64 — a well-placed point (close to 1).

If P were on a cluster boundary where a(P) ≈ b(P) ≈ 2.0, then s(P) ≈ 0 — that point is ambiguous. If a(P) = 3.0 and b(P) = 1.0, s(P) is negative — the point was assigned to the wrong cluster.

Interpreting Silhouette Values

RangeInterpretation
s(i) close to +1Point is well-matched to its own cluster, far from neighboring clusters — excellent
s(i) close to 0Point is on or near the boundary between two clusters — ambiguous
s(i) close to −1Point is probably assigned to the wrong cluster — poor

Using Silhouette to Choose K

from sklearn.metrics import silhouette_score

# ── Compute silhouette for K = 2 to 12 ──────────────────────────
# (K=1 is undefined — no "other cluster" to compare)
silhouette_scores = []
K_range_sil = range(2, 13)

for k in K_range_sil:
    km = KMeans(n_clusters=k, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
    labels = km.fit_predict(X_scaled)
    score = silhouette_score(X_scaled, labels)
    silhouette_scores.append(score)
    print(f"K={k}: silhouette = {score:.4f}")

# ── Plot Silhouette by K ─────────────────────────────────────────
best_k_sil = K_range_sil[np.argmax(silhouette_scores)]
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))
ax.plot(K_range_sil, silhouette_scores, 'o-', color='#56d9b0', lw=2, ms=8)
ax.axvline(x=best_k_sil, color='tomato', linestyle='--', lw=1.5,
           label=f'Best K={best_k_sil} (silhouette={max(silhouette_scores):.3f})')
ax.set_xlabel('Number of Clusters (K)', fontsize=12)
ax.set_ylabel('Silhouette Score', fontsize=12)
ax.set_title('Silhouette Analysis for Optimal K', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_xticks(range(2, 13))
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_03_silhouette.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

Silhouette Plot (Per-Point)

from sklearn.metrics import silhouette_samples

# ── Detailed silhouette plot for K=3 ────────────────────────────
k_best = 3
km_best = KMeans(n_clusters=k_best, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
labels_best = km_best.fit_predict(X_scaled)

sil_vals  = silhouette_samples(X_scaled, labels_best)
sil_avg   = silhouette_score(X_scaled, labels_best)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 6))
y_lower = 10
colors = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']

for k in range(k_best):
    sil_k = np.sort(sil_vals[labels_best == k])
    size_k = sil_k.shape[0]
    y_upper = y_lower + size_k

    ax.fill_betweenx(np.arange(y_lower, y_upper), 0, sil_k,
                     alpha=0.7, color=colors[k], label=f'Cluster {k}')
    ax.text(-0.05, y_lower + 0.5*size_k, str(k), fontsize=10, color=colors[k], fontweight='bold')
    y_lower = y_upper + 10

ax.axvline(x=sil_avg, color='red', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label=f'Mean = {sil_avg:.3f}')
ax.set_xlabel('Silhouette Coefficient', fontsize=12)
ax.set_title(f'Silhouette Plot — K={k_best}', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.2)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_04_silhouette_detail.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

6.6 K-Means++ Initialization AN306

Standard K-Means picks initial centroids completely at random, which can lead to bad starting configurations where two centroids are initialized close together and converge to a suboptimal solution. K-Means++ fixes this with a smarter initialization strategy.

K-Means++ Algorithm

  1. Choose the first centroid uniformly at random from the data points.
  2. For each remaining data point xᵢ, compute D(xᵢ) = the squared distance from xᵢ to the nearest centroid already chosen.
  3. Choose the next centroid with probability proportional to D(xᵢ)² — points that are farther from existing centroids have a higher probability of being selected.
  4. Repeat steps 2–3 until K centroids have been chosen.
  5. Proceed with the standard K-Means assignment and update steps.

Why It Works

By spreading initial centroids far apart, K-Means++ reduces the chance that two centroids start near the same group and "compete" for the same cluster. This leads to:

  • Fewer iterations to convergence
  • Lower final inertia (better solutions)
  • More consistent results across runs (less variance between n_init attempts)
💡 K-Means++ Selection in Practice

Say K=2 and you have 4 data points: A=(0,0), B=(1,0), C=(9,0), D=(10,0). Two obvious clusters: {A,B} on the left and {C,D} on the right.

Step 1: Pick the first centroid randomly — say it lands on A=(0,0).

Step 2: Compute D²(xᵢ) for each remaining point (squared distance to nearest centroid A):

D²(B) = (1−0)² = 1  |  D²(C) = (9−0)² = 81  |  D²(D) = (10−0)² = 100

Step 3: Total = 1+81+100 = 182. Selection probabilities:
B → 1/182 ≈ 0.5%  |  C → 81/182 ≈ 44.5%  |  D → 100/182 ≈ 55%

C or D is overwhelmingly likely to become the second centroid — which is exactly right, since we want one centroid per natural group. Random init could have picked B, putting both centroids on the same side.

✓ Always Use K-Means++
In scikit-learn, K-Means++ is the default: init='k-means++'. There is almost never a reason to use init='random'. The computational cost of K-Means++ initialization is negligible, and the quality improvement is consistent. 
# Demonstration: random init vs K-Means++ init (10 runs each)
print("Comparing inertia: random vs k-means++ init (K=3, 10 trials each)")
print(f"{'Run':>4}  {'Random':>12}  {'K-Means++':>12}")

for trial in range(1, 11):
    km_rand = KMeans(n_clusters=3, init='random',    n_init=1, random_state=trial*7)
    km_pp   = KMeans(n_clusters=3, init='k-means++', n_init=1, random_state=trial*7)
    km_rand.fit(X_scaled)
    km_pp.fit(X_scaled)
    print(f"{trial:>4}  {km_rand.inertia_:>12.2f}  {km_pp.inertia_:>12.2f}")

6.7 Feature Scaling for Clustering AN306 Docx

Because K-Means uses Euclidean distance, features with larger numerical ranges dominate the distance calculation. A feature ranging 0–100,000 will contribute 10,000× more to distance than a feature ranging 0–10 — even if both are equally important for grouping observations. Feature scaling ensures every feature contributes proportionally.

Standardization (Z-score Scaling)

z = (x − μ) / σ

Subtracts the mean and divides by the standard deviation (σ). The result has mean 0 and standard deviation 1. This is StandardScaler in scikit-learn.

⚠ Source Error — Built In Article
One of the linked readings stated "standardization divides by variance." This is WRONG. Standardization divides by the standard deviation (σ), not the variance (σ²). The formula is z = (x − μ) / σ. Dividing by variance would produce values in units of σ⁻¹, not the standard z-score scale. Always use σ in the denominator.

Min-Max Normalization

x_norm = (x − x_min) / (x_max − x_min)

Rescales values to the [0, 1] range. Use MinMaxScaler when you need bounded output or when you know the range is meaningful. Sensitive to outliers — one extreme outlier compresses all other values toward the middle.

Standardization vs. Normalization for Clustering

MethodRangeOutlier sensitivityWhen to use for clustering
StandardScaler (z-score)Unbounded (~−3 to +3)ModerateDefault choice for K-Means — most robust
MinMaxScaler[0, 1]High (outliers compress the rest)When all features are truly bounded with no outliers
RobustScalerUnboundedLow (uses median + IQR)When data has many outliers you want to keep
 
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, MinMaxScaler, RobustScaler

# Standard (default for clustering)
X_std   = StandardScaler().fit_transform(data[['salary','years_exp','skill_score']])

# Min-Max
X_minmax = MinMaxScaler().fit_transform(data[['salary','years_exp','skill_score']])

# Robust
X_robust = RobustScaler().fit_transform(data[['salary','years_exp','skill_score']])

# Compare column statistics after scaling
print("After StandardScaler:  mean={:.4f}  std={:.4f}".format(
    X_std.mean(), X_std.std()))
print("After MinMaxScaler:    min={:.4f}   max={:.4f}".format(
    X_minmax.min(), X_minmax.max()))

# IMPORTANT: fit_transform on training/all data only
# If you later add new observations and want to assign them to clusters,
# use scaler.transform(new_data) — do NOT refit the scaler on new data
# (this is the same train-only fit rule as in Part 3)

6.8 Hierarchical Clustering AN306 AN300

Hierarchical clustering is an entirely different approach to grouping. Instead of starting with K clusters and iterating, it builds a tree of nested clusters — a dendrogram — that you can "cut" at any level to produce any number of clusters K without refitting.

Agglomerative vs. Divisive

There are two directions:

  • Agglomerative (bottom-up): Start with every observation as its own cluster (n clusters), then iteratively merge the two most similar clusters until one cluster remains. This is by far the most common approach and what AN306 uses.
  • Divisive (top-down): Start with all observations in one cluster, then split recursively. Rarely used in practice.

Linkage Criteria

At each step of agglomerative clustering, you need to define "distance between two clusters" (not just between two points). This is the linkage criterion:

LinkageDistance MeasureBehaviorBest For
WardIncrease in within-cluster variance when mergingMinimizes total within-cluster SSE; tends to produce compact, equal-size clustersDefault for most applications; best for compact clusters
CompleteMaximum distance between any two points across the two clustersConservative; prevents chaining; compact clustersWhen outliers need to be contained
AverageMean distance between all pairs of points across the two clustersCompromise between single and completeGeneral purpose
SingleMinimum distance between any two points across the two clusters"Chaining" tendency — one large elongated cluster formsDetecting elongated clusters; usually not ideal
💡 Plain-English Example: Why Ward Linkage Wins

Imagine you have two tight groups of points — Group A (tightly packed) and Group B (tightly packed, far from A) — plus a bridge point C halfway between them.

Single linkage measures the closest pair across clusters. C is fairly close to both A and B, so single linkage merges all three into one giant cluster too early. Result: one big blob instead of two clean groups — the "chaining" problem.

Ward linkage asks: "If I merge these two clusters, how much does the total within-cluster variance jump?" Merging A+B would cause a huge variance jump (they're far apart). Merging small nearby clusters costs little variance. Ward will naturally keep A and B separate until the very last steps, giving you the clearest structure. This is why Ward is the default for most clustering tasks.

Implementation

from sklearn.cluster import AgglomerativeClustering
from scipy.cluster.hierarchy import dendrogram, linkage

# ── Fit agglomerative clustering ──────────────────────────────────
# Ward linkage minimizes within-cluster variance at each step
agg = AgglomerativeClustering(
    n_clusters=3,
    linkage='ward'     # 'ward', 'complete', 'average', 'single'
)
agg_labels = agg.fit_predict(X_scaled)

data['cluster_agg'] = agg_labels
print("Agglomerative cluster sizes:")
print(data['cluster_agg'].value_counts().sort_index())

# ── Silhouette score for agglomerative result ─────────────────────
agg_sil = silhouette_score(X_scaled, agg_labels)
print(f"Silhouette score (Ward, K=3): {agg_sil:.4f}")

6.9 Dendrograms AN306

A dendrogram visualizes the entire merge history of hierarchical clustering. It is the definitive output of agglomerative clustering — it shows which observations were merged, in what order, and at what distance (height). By drawing a horizontal cutline at a chosen height, you can read off any number of clusters K.

Reading a Dendrogram

  • The x-axis shows individual observations (or small groups already merged)
  • The y-axis shows the linkage distance (Ward = variance increase; higher = more dissimilar)
  • Each horizontal bar represents a merge of two groups at the labeled height
  • A vertical cutline at height h gives you as many clusters as the line crosses branches
  • Long vertical bars before a cutline indicate well-separated natural clusters
💡 Plain-English Example: Cutting a Dendrogram

Say you have 5 job candidates: Alice, Bob, Charlie, Dana, Eve. After running hierarchical clustering, the merge history looks like this (read bottom to top):

Height 0.5: Alice + Bob merge (they're very similar — both junior Python devs)
Height 0.8: Charlie + Dana merge (both mid-level data engineers)
Height 1.2: (Alice+Bob) + Eve merge (Eve is also junior-ish)
Height 3.1: (Alice+Bob+Eve) + (Charlie+Dana) merge (now we're connecting juniors to mid-levels)

Draw a horizontal line at height 2.0. It crosses 2 vertical branches — so you get 2 clusters: {Alice, Bob, Eve} and {Charlie, Dana}.

Draw it at height 1.0. It crosses 3 branches — 3 clusters: {Alice, Bob}, {Eve}, {Charlie, Dana}.

The long gap between height 1.2 and 3.1 is the signal — that's where natural structure lives. Place your cut in the middle of the longest vertical bar you can find.

# ── Compute full linkage matrix ───────────────────────────────────
# scipy's linkage function does the full hierarchical computation
# subsample to 100 points for legible dendrogram display
idx = np.random.choice(len(X_scaled), size=100, replace=False)
X_sub = X_scaled[idx]

Z = linkage(X_sub, method='ward')

# ── Plot dendrogram ───────────────────────────────────────────────
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(16, 6))
dendrogram(
    Z,
    ax=ax,
    truncate_mode='lastp',  # show only the last p merges
    p=20,                   # show last 20 merges for readability
    color_threshold=3.5,    # color branches below this height
    leaf_rotation=90,
    leaf_font_size=9
)
ax.set_title('Dendrogram — Ward Linkage (subsample n=100)', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.set_xlabel('Observation Index')
ax.set_ylabel('Ward Linkage Distance (Δ Within-Cluster Variance)')
ax.axhline(y=3.5, color='tomato', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label='Cut at 3.5 → 3 clusters')
ax.legend()
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_05_dendrogram.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

Choosing K from the Dendrogram

# Find the ideal cut: look for the longest vertical bar you can cut
# The n largest gaps between merge heights suggest natural K values
merge_heights = Z[:, 2]  # the merge distances (column 2 of Z)
height_diffs  = np.diff(merge_heights)

# Top-5 largest jumps in merge height (working from the last merges)
largest_gaps = np.argsort(height_diffs)[-5:][::-1]
print("Largest gaps in merge height (suggests natural K values):")
for idx in largest_gaps:
    n_clusters = len(merge_heights) - idx  # clusters if cut after this merge
    print(f"  Gap of {height_diffs[idx]:.3f} at merge {idx} → cut here = ~{n_clusters} clusters")

6.10 DBSCAN AN306

DBSCAN (Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise) takes a completely different approach. Instead of assigning every point to a cluster, it defines clusters as dense regions separated by sparse regions — and labels any point in a sparse region as an outlier (noise), cluster label = −1.

DBSCAN Core Concepts

DBSCAN has two parameters: epsilon (ε) and min_samples.

  • Epsilon (ε): The neighborhood radius. A point's ε-neighborhood is the set of all points within distance ε of it.
  • min_samples: The minimum number of points (including the point itself) that must be in its ε-neighborhood for it to be a core point.

Three types of points:

  • Core point: Has at least min_samples points (including itself) within distance ε. This point sits in the dense interior of a cluster.
  • Border point: Within distance ε of a core point, but has fewer than min_samples in its own neighborhood. On the edge of a cluster.
  • Noise point (outlier): Neither a core point nor a border point. Gets label = −1.

The DBSCAN Algorithm

  1. Label every point as core, border, or noise using ε and min_samples.
  2. Connect core points that are within ε of each other (they form a cluster).
  3. Assign border points to a neighboring core point's cluster.
  4. All noise points are left unlabeled (−1).
💡 Plain-English Example: Core, Border, and Noise in a Grid

Set ε = 1.5 units, min_samples = 3. Imagine 9 points on a 2D grid:

Dense group: A(1,1), B(1,2), C(2,1), D(2,2) — all within 1.5 of each other.
Bridge point: E(3,2) — within 1.5 of D, but only D and C are in its neighborhood (2 points, less than min_samples=3).
Loner: F(8,8) — far from everything, only 1 point in its neighborhood.

A, B, C, D each have 3+ neighbors within ε → core points. They form Cluster 1.
E is within ε of core point D → border point. It joins Cluster 1 but cannot expand it.
F has no core point within ε → noise point, labeled −1 (outlier).

This is what makes DBSCAN powerful: it found the dense blob automatically and flagged F as an outlier without you having to specify K=1 beforehand.

Key Advantages over K-Means

  • Does not require specifying K in advance — the number of clusters is determined automatically
  • Can find arbitrarily shaped clusters (not just spherical)
  • Explicitly identifies outliers — K-Means forces every point into a cluster
  • Not sensitive to outliers pulling centroids (K-Means is very sensitive)
💡 Core, Border, Noise — 8 Points, ε=1.5, min_samples=3

Picture 8 job postings plotted on a 2D salary/experience axis (coordinates simplified). Set ε=1.5 (neighborhood radius) and min_samples=3 (need at least 3 neighbors to be a "core" point).

PointNeighbors within ε=1.5CountType
AB, C (+ itself)3Core — dense interior
BA, C (+ itself)3Core
CA, B (+ itself)3Core
DE, F (+ itself)3Core
ED (+ itself)2Border — within ε of D (a core), but not dense enough itself
FD (+ itself)2Border
Gonly itself1Noise (−1) — isolated point, no cluster
Honly itself1Noise (−1)

Result: DBSCAN finds 2 clusters: Cluster 0 = {A, B, C, E, F} (E and F get pulled in because they're within ε of core point D — wait, D is in this cluster too). Actually Cluster 0 = {A,B,C} and Cluster 1 = {D,E,F}. Points G and H are labeled −1 (outliers).

Key insight: K-Means would have forced G and H into some cluster. DBSCAN says "these don't belong anywhere" — they're genuine outliers (maybe data entry errors or exotic job postings).

Tuning tip: If ε is too small, almost every point becomes noise. If ε is too large, everything merges into one cluster. The k-NN distance plot below finds the right middle ground.

Choosing ε and min_samples

The standard heuristic: plot the k-nearest-neighbor distance for each point, sorted ascending. The "knee" in this plot suggests a good ε. min_samples is usually set to 2 × number_of_features or at least 5.

from sklearn.cluster import DBSCAN
from sklearn.neighbors import NearestNeighbors

# ── k-NN distance plot for choosing epsilon ───────────────────────
k = 5   # min_samples candidate
nbrs = NearestNeighbors(n_neighbors=k).fit(X_scaled)
distances, _ = nbrs.kneighbors(X_scaled)
knn_dists = np.sort(distances[:, k-1])  # k-th nearest neighbor distance

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))
ax.plot(knn_dists, color='steelblue', lw=1.5)
ax.axhline(y=0.4, color='tomato', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label='Suggested ε ≈ 0.4')
ax.set_xlabel('Points (sorted by k-NN distance)')
ax.set_ylabel(f'{k}-NN Distance')
ax.set_title('k-NN Distance Plot for DBSCAN Epsilon Selection')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_06_knn_dist.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# ── Fit DBSCAN ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
dbscan = DBSCAN(eps=0.4, min_samples=5)
db_labels = dbscan.fit_predict(X_scaled)

n_clusters_db = len(set(db_labels)) - (1 if -1 in db_labels else 0)
n_noise_db    = np.sum(db_labels == -1)

print(f"DBSCAN found {n_clusters_db} clusters")
print(f"Noise points (outliers): {n_noise_db} ({100*n_noise_db/len(db_labels):.1f}%)")
print(f"Cluster sizes: {pd.Series(db_labels[db_labels != -1]).value_counts().to_dict()}")

# Silhouette score (only on non-noise points; need at least 2 clusters)
if n_clusters_db >= 2:
    mask_valid = db_labels != -1
    db_sil = silhouette_score(X_scaled[mask_valid], db_labels[mask_valid])
    print(f"Silhouette (non-noise only): {db_sil:.4f}")

# ── Visualize DBSCAN ─────────────────────────────────────────────
data['cluster_db'] = db_labels
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 6))
colors_db = ['#7c8ff5','#56d9b0','#f59e45','#f56b6b','#c084fc']

for label in sorted(set(db_labels)):
    mask  = db_labels == label
    color = '#888888' if label == -1 else colors_db[label % len(colors_db)]
    name  = 'Noise (outlier)' if label == -1 else f'Cluster {label}'
    alpha = 0.3 if label == -1 else 0.7
    ms    = 20 if label == -1 else 30
    ax.scatter(data.loc[mask, 'years_exp'], data.loc[mask, 'salary'],
               c=color, alpha=alpha, s=ms, label=name)
ax.set_xlabel('Years of Experience')
ax.set_ylabel('Salary ($)')
ax.set_title('DBSCAN Clustering (ε=0.4, min_samples=5)', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_07_dbscan.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

6.11 PCA — Dimensionality Reduction AN306

As the number of features grows, two problems emerge: (1) visualizing clusters becomes impossible beyond 3 dimensions, and (2) the "curse of dimensionality" makes distance-based clustering less effective. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) projects high-dimensional data into a lower-dimensional space while retaining as much variance as possible.

What PCA Does

PCA finds a new set of axes — the principal components (PCs) — that are:

  • Linear combinations of the original features
  • Orthogonal to each other (uncorrelated)
  • Ordered by variance explained: PC1 captures the most variance, PC2 the second most, and so on
PC1 = w₁₁x₁ + w₁₂x₂ + … + w₁ₙxₙ (direction of maximum variance) PC2 = w₂₁x₁ + w₂₂x₂ + … + w₂ₙxₙ (orthogonal to PC1; second-most variance)
💡 What PCA Loadings Actually Mean

Say you have two features: salary (scaled) and years_exp (scaled). PCA might find:

PC1 = 0.71 × salary + 0.71 × years_exp

This is a "career achievement" axis — PC1 is high when both salary AND experience are high. A senior engineer with high salary and 12 years gets a large PC1 score. A new grad with low salary and 1 year gets a small one.

PC2 = 0.71 × salary − 0.71 × years_exp

PC2 captures the gap between salary and experience. High salary but low experience = positive PC2 (overachiever). Low salary but high experience = negative PC2 (underearner). The 0.71 and −0.71 are what appear in the loadings table from your PCA.

The weights w are called loadings. They tell you how much each original feature contributes to each PC.

When to Use PCA in Clustering

  • Visualization: Reduce to 2D or 3D to plot clusters
  • Noise reduction: Lower-variance components often capture noise; dropping them can improve cluster quality
  • Speed: Fewer dimensions means faster distance calculations for large datasets
⚠ Scale Before PCA
PCA is based on variance. If features have different scales, PCA will be dominated by the high-variance features — the same problem as with K-Means distances. Always StandardScaler → PCA → KMeans, in that order.

Implementation

from sklearn.decomposition import PCA

# ── Fit PCA on scaled data ────────────────────────────────────────
pca = PCA()   # keep all components first to inspect explained variance
pca.fit(X_scaled)

# Explained variance ratio
evr = pca.explained_variance_ratio_
print("Explained Variance by Component:")
cumulative = 0
for i, ev in enumerate(evr):
    cumulative += ev
    print(f"  PC{i+1}: {ev*100:.1f}%  (cumulative: {cumulative*100:.1f}%)")

# ── Scree plot ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(12, 5))
axes[0].bar(range(1, len(evr)+1), evr*100, color='steelblue')
axes[0].set_xlabel('Principal Component')
axes[0].set_ylabel('% Variance Explained')
axes[0].set_title('Scree Plot — Individual Variance')
axes[0].grid(True, alpha=0.3)

cumulative_evr = np.cumsum(evr)
axes[1].plot(range(1, len(evr)+1), cumulative_evr*100, 'o-', color='#56d9b0', lw=2)
axes[1].axhline(y=95, color='tomato', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label='95% threshold')
axes[1].axhline(y=80, color='orange', linestyle='--', lw=1.5, label='80% threshold')
axes[1].set_xlabel('Number of Components')
axes[1].set_ylabel('Cumulative % Variance Explained')
axes[1].set_title('Cumulative Explained Variance')
axes[1].legend()
axes[1].grid(True, alpha=0.3)

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_08_pca_scree.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# ── Reduce to 2D for visualization ───────────────────────────────
pca2 = PCA(n_components=2)
X_pca = pca2.fit_transform(X_scaled)
print(f"\\nPCA 2D: captures {sum(pca2.explained_variance_ratio_)*100:.1f}% of variance")

# Re-run K-Means on PCA-reduced data
km_pca = KMeans(n_clusters=3, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
labels_pca = km_pca.fit_predict(X_pca)

# 2D cluster plot
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 7))
colors = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']
for k in range(3):
    mask = labels_pca == k
    ax.scatter(X_pca[mask, 0], X_pca[mask, 1],
               c=colors[k], alpha=0.7, s=40, label=f'Cluster {k}')
ax.scatter(km_pca.cluster_centers_[:,0], km_pca.cluster_centers_[:,1],
           c='white', marker='X', s=250, zorder=5, edgecolors='gray', label='Centroids')
ax.set_xlabel(f'PC1 ({pca2.explained_variance_ratio_[0]*100:.1f}% variance)')
ax.set_ylabel(f'PC2 ({pca2.explained_variance_ratio_[1]*100:.1f}% variance)')
ax.set_title('K-Means on PCA-Reduced Data (2 Components)')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_09_pca_clusters.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

# ── PCA loadings — which original features drive each PC? ─────────
loadings = pd.DataFrame(pca2.components_.T,
                         columns=['PC1','PC2'],
                         index=data.columns[:3])  # original feature names
print("\\nPCA Loadings (contribution of each feature to each PC):")
print(loadings.round(3))

6.12 Cluster Profiling & Interpretation AN306 AN300

Fitting K-Means and getting cluster labels is only halfway. The other half — and the part that matters for a P4 presentation — is profiling each cluster: understanding what characterizes it, naming it, and explaining what it represents in business terms.

Statistical Profiling

# ── Profile clusters with descriptive statistics ──────────────────
# Use original (unscaled) data with cluster labels attached
profile = data.groupby('cluster')[['salary','years_exp','skill_score']].agg(
    ['mean','median','std','count']
)
print("Cluster Profiles:")
print(profile.round(1))

# Cleaner single-level column format
profile_summary = data.groupby('cluster').agg(
    count        = ('salary', 'count'),
    salary_mean  = ('salary', 'mean'),
    salary_std   = ('salary', 'std'),
    exp_mean     = ('years_exp', 'mean'),
    skill_mean   = ('skill_score', 'mean')
).round(1)
print("\\nCluster Summary:")
print(profile_summary)

Radar Charts for Multi-Dimensional Comparison

# Radar chart: compare clusters across all features simultaneously
# Normalize centroids to [0,1] for the radar axes
from matplotlib.patches import FancyArrowPatch
import matplotlib

n_vars  = 3
features = ['salary', 'years_exp', 'skill_score']
angles   = np.linspace(0, 2*np.pi, n_vars, endpoint=False).tolist()
angles  += angles[:1]   # close the polygon

# Normalize each feature 0–1 for the radar
feat_data = data.groupby('cluster')[features].mean()
feat_norm = (feat_data - feat_data.min()) / (feat_data.max() - feat_data.min())

colors_r = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']
fig, ax  = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 8), subplot_kw=dict(polar=True))

for k, row in feat_norm.iterrows():
    vals = row.tolist() + row.tolist()[:1]
    ax.plot(angles, vals, 'o-', lw=2, color=colors_r[k], label=f'Cluster {k}')
    ax.fill(angles, vals, alpha=0.1, color=colors_r[k])

ax.set_xticks(angles[:-1])
ax.set_xticklabels(features, fontsize=12)
ax.set_title('Cluster Radar Chart', fontsize=14, fontweight='bold', pad=20)
ax.legend(loc='upper right', bbox_to_anchor=(1.3, 1.1))
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_10_radar.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

Box Plots by Cluster

# Box plots show within-cluster spread, not just means
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(14, 5))
features_plot = ['salary', 'years_exp', 'skill_score']
ylabels = ['Salary ($)', 'Years Exp', 'Skill Score']
colors_bp = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']

for i, (feat, ylabel) in enumerate(zip(features_plot, ylabels)):
    groups = [data.loc[data['cluster']==k, feat] for k in range(3)]
    bp = axes[i].boxplot(groups, patch_artist=True, notch=False)
    for patch, color in zip(bp['boxes'], colors_bp):
        patch.set_facecolor(color)
        patch.set_alpha(0.7)
    axes[i].set_xticklabels([f'Cluster {k}' for k in range(3)])
    axes[i].set_ylabel(ylabel)
    axes[i].set_title(f'{feat.replace("_"," ").title()} by Cluster')
    axes[i].grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('06_11_boxplots.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

Naming the Clusters

After profiling, give each cluster a business-relevant label. This is the interpretive step that transforms a technical output ("Cluster 2") into an insight ("Senior Cloud Specialists"). The naming should be grounded in the actual statistics, not just intuition.

# Example naming based on profile statistics
cluster_names = {
    0: 'Entry-Level Generalists  (low salary, low exp, low skill)',
    1: 'Mid-Level Practitioners  (mid salary, ~5yr exp, mid skill)',
    2: 'Senior Specialists       (high salary, 12+ yr exp, high skill)'
}

data['cluster_name'] = data['cluster'].map(cluster_names)
print("\\nCluster Labels:")
print(data['cluster_name'].value_counts())

6.13 Cluster Stability — Adjusted Rand Index AN306

How confident should you be that your clusters are real and stable — not just artifacts of random initialization or the specific K chosen? The Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) measures agreement between two sets of cluster labels.

When You Have Ground Truth Labels

ARI compares a predicted clustering to a known ground truth:

ARI = (RI − E[RI]) / (max(RI) − E[RI])
  • ARI = 1.0: Perfect agreement — predicted clusters match ground truth exactly
  • ARI ≈ 0: Random agreement — no better than chance
  • ARI < 0: Systematic disagreement (worse than random)
💡 Plain-English Example: What ARI Actually Measures

Say you have 6 employees and you know their true department: {Alice, Bob} = Engineering; {Carol, Dan} = Marketing; {Eve, Frank} = Sales.

Your clustering predicts: Cluster 0 = {Alice, Bob}, Cluster 1 = {Carol, Dan}, Cluster 2 = {Eve, Frank}.
Every pair that belongs together is grouped together, every pair that shouldn't be is kept apart. ARI = 1.0 — perfect.

A second clustering predicts: Cluster 0 = {Alice, Carol, Eve}, Cluster 1 = {Bob, Dan, Frank}.
This just split everyone by some random pattern. The pairs that are co-grouped barely resemble the true structure. ARI ≈ 0 — no better than random.

ARI for stability testing: Run K-Means 20 times with different random seeds. If ARI between any two runs is 0.95+, the algorithm keeps finding the same clusters → the structure is real. If ARI between runs is 0.3, the clusters are unstable — the data doesn't have clear natural groups, and K is probably wrong.

Using ARI to Test Stability (Bootstrap)

from sklearn.metrics import adjusted_rand_score

# ── Stability test: run K-Means 20 times with different seeds ────
# Measure how often different runs agree with each other
n_runs = 20
all_labels = []

for seed in range(n_runs):
    km_run = KMeans(n_clusters=3, init='k-means++', n_init=1, random_state=seed)
    all_labels.append(km_run.fit_predict(X_scaled))

# Compute pairwise ARI between all runs
ari_matrix = np.zeros((n_runs, n_runs))
for i in range(n_runs):
    for j in range(n_runs):
        ari_matrix[i,j] = adjusted_rand_score(all_labels[i], all_labels[j])

print(f"Mean pairwise ARI across {n_runs} runs: {ari_matrix[np.triu_indices(n_runs,1)].mean():.4f}")
print(f"Min  pairwise ARI: {ari_matrix[np.triu_indices(n_runs,1)].min():.4f}")
print("ARI close to 1.0 → stable clusters; ARI < 0.5 → unstable, reconsider K")

# ── Example: comparing K-Means vs. Agglomerative ──────────────────
ari_vs_agg = adjusted_rand_score(kmeans.labels_, agg_labels)
print(f"\\nARI (K-Means vs. Agglomerative): {ari_vs_agg:.4f}")
print("High ARI between methods = both algorithms agree on the structure")

6.14 Choosing a Clustering Algorithm AN306 AN300

AlgorithmStrengthsWeaknessesUse When
K-Means Fast; scalable; interpretable centroids; works well with large n Requires specifying K; assumes spherical clusters; sensitive to outliers and scaling Large datasets; clusters expected to be roughly spherical; speed is a priority; P4 default
Hierarchical (Ward) No K needed upfront; dendrogram gives full picture; deterministic O(n² log n) — slow for large n; memory intensive; must commit to K at the cut Smaller datasets (<10,000); want to visually inspect cluster hierarchy; exploring K
DBSCAN No K needed; finds arbitrary shapes; labels outliers; not sensitive to outlier points pulling centroids Hard to tune ε and min_samples; struggles with varying density; poorly defined silhouette for noise points Anomaly detection; clusters of irregular shapes; outlier labeling is a goal
✓ Recommended P4 Workflow
1. Scale with StandardScaler. 2. Run K-Means with elbow + silhouette to find K. 3. Fit K-Means with the best K. 4. If the dataset is small enough (<5000 rows), also run hierarchical clustering and compare dendrograms. 5. Profile every cluster with means, box plots, and a radar chart. 6. Name and present each cluster with business interpretation.

Lab 6 P4 Job Market Clustering Lab

Build a complete P4-style clustering notebook on a real job market dataset. The pipeline below mirrors the P4 structure exactly.

Complete P4 Pipeline

### ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
### AN 306 — Presentation 4: Job Market Clustering Analysis
### Student: [Your Name] | Dataset: [job_postings.csv] | Date: [Date]
### ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

import pandas as pd, numpy as np, matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans, AgglomerativeClustering, DBSCAN
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.metrics import silhouette_score
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
from scipy.cluster.hierarchy import dendrogram, linkage
import warnings; warnings.filterwarnings('ignore')

# ── CELL 1: Load and inspect job posting data ─────────────────────
df = pd.read_csv('job_postings.csv')
print(f"Shape: {df.shape}")
print(df.dtypes)
print(df.head())

# ── CELL 2: Select and clean features ─────────────────────────────
# Choose numeric features relevant to clustering (varies by dataset)
features = ['salary_normalized', 'years_exp_required', 'remote_flag',
            'company_size', 'num_skills_required']
# (adjust to actual column names in your dataset)

cluster_df = df[features].dropna()
print(f"After dropping NaN: {cluster_df.shape[0]} rows")

X = StandardScaler().fit_transform(cluster_df)

# ── CELL 3: Elbow + Silhouette to find K ─────────────────────────
inertias, sil_scores = [], []
K_range = range(2, 11)

for k in K_range:
    km = KMeans(n_clusters=k, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
    lbls = km.fit_predict(X)
    inertias.append(km.inertia_)
    sil_scores.append(silhouette_score(X, lbls))

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(14, 5))
axes[0].plot(K_range, inertias, 'o-', color='steelblue', lw=2)
axes[0].set_title('Elbow Method'); axes[0].set_xlabel('K'); axes[0].set_ylabel('Inertia')
axes[0].grid(True, alpha=0.3)
axes[1].plot(K_range, sil_scores, 'o-', color='#56d9b0', lw=2)
axes[1].set_title('Silhouette Score'); axes[1].set_xlabel('K'); axes[1].set_ylabel('Silhouette')
axes[1].grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

best_k = K_range[np.argmax(sil_scores)]
print(f"Best K by silhouette: {best_k}  (score={max(sil_scores):.4f})")

# ── CELL 4: Fit final model ───────────────────────────────────────
final_km = KMeans(n_clusters=best_k, init='k-means++', n_init=10, random_state=42)
cluster_df = cluster_df.copy()
cluster_df['cluster'] = final_km.fit_predict(X)
df.loc[cluster_df.index, 'cluster'] = cluster_df['cluster']

# ── CELL 5: PCA visualization ─────────────────────────────────────
pca = PCA(n_components=2)
X_2d = pca.fit_transform(X)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 7))
colors = ['#7c8ff5','#56d9b0','#f59e45','#f56b6b','#c084fc']
for k in range(best_k):
    mask = cluster_df['cluster'] == k
    ax.scatter(X_2d[mask,0], X_2d[mask,1], c=colors[k%5], alpha=0.6, s=30, label=f'Cluster {k}')
ax.set_title(f'K-Means K={best_k} — PCA Visualization')
ax.set_xlabel(f'PC1 ({pca.explained_variance_ratio_[0]*100:.1f}% var)')
ax.set_ylabel(f'PC2 ({pca.explained_variance_ratio_[1]*100:.1f}% var)')
ax.legend(); ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3); plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

# ── CELL 6: Profile and name clusters ────────────────────────────
profile = cluster_df.groupby('cluster')[features].mean().round(2)
print("Cluster Profiles:"); print(profile)

# Then: name each cluster based on the profile statistics
# and add a 'cluster_name' column using a dict mapping
