Part 7 — Association Rule Analysis

From market baskets to actionable rules — support, confidence, lift, Apriori, and mlxtend from scratch

7.1 Why Association Rules?

AN300 AN306

Clustering (Part 6) grouped similar observations. Association rule mining groups items that co-occur in the same transaction. The canonical application is market basket analysis: given millions of shopping receipts, find which products are bought together.

The Core Question

If a customer buys diapers, how likely are they also to buy beer? The legendary "diapers and beer" finding from Walmart's data in the 1990s is often cited as the first real win of association rule mining — parents stocking up on diapers apparently also bought beer for the weekend. That insight drove shelf placement decisions.

More formally, an association rule has the form:

Association Rule Notation {Antecedent} → {Consequent}
e.g., {diapers, formula} → {beer}

The antecedent is the "if" side; the consequent is the "then" side. The rule does not imply causation — it means these items appear together in transactions more often than chance would predict.

Use Cases Beyond Retail

  • Streaming platforms — "Users who watched X also watched Y"
  • Healthcare — "Patients with diagnosis A and B frequently also receive treatment C"
  • Web analytics — "Pages visited together in the same session"
  • Cybersecurity — "Events that co-occur in attack sequences"
  • Banking — "Clients who hold product A and B also open product C"

Transaction Data Format

The raw data is a list of transactions. Each transaction is a set of items (the basket). There is no numerical target variable y — this is unsupervised, like clustering.

# Example transactions — each row is one receipt
Transaction 1: {milk, bread, eggs}
Transaction 2: {milk, diapers, beer, cereal}
Transaction 3: {bread, diapers, beer, cereal}
Transaction 4: {milk, bread, diapers, beer}
Transaction 5: {bread, cereal}
Key distinction from clustering: In clustering, every observation has feature columns (height, weight, income…). In association rule mining, the "features" are binary item indicators — an item is either in the basket or not. The dataset is typically very sparse (most items are absent from any given transaction).

The Vocabulary

  • Item — an individual product, page, event, etc.
  • Itemset — any subset of items (e.g., {milk, bread})
  • Transaction — one basket/receipt containing a set of items
  • k-itemset — an itemset of size k (a 2-itemset has two items)
  • Frequent itemset — an itemset whose support ≥ min_support threshold

7.2 Support, Confidence, and Lift — The Three Core Metrics

AN300 AN306

Three numbers describe every association rule. You need all three — no single metric is sufficient.

Support

Support measures how common an itemset is. It is the fraction of all transactions that contain the itemset.

Support of Itemset A support(A) = count(transactions containing A) / total transactions

Support applies to any itemset, not just rules. For the rule A → B:

Support of Rule A → B support(A → B) = support(A ∪ B) = count(A and B together) / N

Using the five transactions above, let's compute support for {diapers}:

  • Transactions containing diapers: T2, T3, T4 → count = 3
  • Total transactions: N = 5
  • support({diapers}) = 3/5 = 0.60
Low support = unreliable rule. A rule might appear to have perfect confidence while being based on only 2 transactions. Setting a minimum support threshold filters out rules that are statistically noise — they happened to co-occur once or twice by chance.
🔑 Why low support rules are dangerous — a concrete example:
Suppose you have 10,000 transactions. You find the rule {wasabi} → {soy sauce} with confidence = 1.0 and support = 0.0003 (3 transactions). Three people bought both items. Perfect confidence — yes, those 3 all bought soy sauce too. But is this actionable? Should you reorganize your store based on 3 customers?

Compare to {bread} → {butter} with confidence = 0.68 and support = 0.12 (1,200 transactions). Less than "perfect" confidence, but based on 1,200 real co-purchases. The bread/butter insight is actionable. The wasabi insight is noise that happens to look perfect.

Rule of thumb: In a dataset of 10,000 transactions, min_support = 0.01 means a rule needs to appear in at least 100 transactions before you trust it. Start there, then lower it if you need rarer patterns.

Confidence

Confidence measures how reliable the rule is, given that the antecedent occurred. It is a conditional probability:

Confidence of A → B confidence(A → B) = support(A ∪ B) / support(A) = P(B | A)

Confidence is "if someone bought A, what fraction of the time did they also buy B?"

Example: rule {diapers} → {beer}

  • Transactions with both diapers AND beer: T2, T3, T4 → count = 3, support = 3/5 = 0.60
  • Transactions with diapers only: T2, T3, T4 → support = 3/5 = 0.60
  • confidence({diapers} → {beer}) = 0.60 / 0.60 = 1.00

Perfect confidence! Every time diapers appear, beer appears too in this tiny dataset.

Confidence is a conditional probability, not joint probability. confidence(A → B) ≠ confidence(B → A). A rule and its reverse can have wildly different confidences. If confidence(A → B) = 0.9, that tells you nothing about confidence(B → A).

Lift

Lift is the most important of the three for actionability. It measures how much more often A and B co-occur than expected if they were independent.

Lift of A → B lift(A → B) = confidence(A → B) / support(B) = support(A ∪ B) / (support(A) × support(B))

Interpretation:

  • lift = 1 → A and B are independent; knowing A tells you nothing about B
  • lift > 1 → A and B co-occur more than chance; positive association
  • lift < 1 → A and B co-occur less than chance; negative association (substitutes)

Example: rule {diapers} → {beer}

  • confidence({diapers} → {beer}) = 1.00
  • support({beer}) = count(T2,T3,T4)/5 = 3/5 = 0.60
  • lift = 1.00 / 0.60 = 1.667

Beer appears with diapers 1.67× more often than it appears generally. That's a meaningful positive association.

💡 Lift Removes the Base Rate — Why We Can't Use Confidence Alone
Imagine "whole milk" appears in 25% of all baskets. Any customer buying anything has a 25% baseline chance of also having milk. So a rule that gets confidence = 0.25 for milk is actually worthless — it's just the base rate.

Lift fixes this by dividing out that baseline (support of the consequent):
RuleConfidencesupport(milk) = 0.25LiftMeaning
{random item} → {milk}0.25÷ 0.251.00No real association — just the base rate
{cereal} → {milk}0.38÷ 0.251.5252% more likely than chance — real signal
{cereal, yogurt} → {milk}0.55÷ 0.252.20Dairy shoppers strongly co-purchase milk
{non-dairy creamer} → {milk}0.10÷ 0.250.4060% less likely — a substitute, not complement
Key insight: Confidence = 0.38 looks mediocre until you realize the baseline is only 0.25. Lift = 1.52 reveals the genuine association. The non-dairy creamer row (lift = 0.40) also reveals a negative association — people buying creamer are less likely to also buy milk, which you'd never see from confidence alone.
Lift is symmetric: lift(A → B) = lift(B → A). Support is also symmetric. Confidence is NOT symmetric. This asymmetry is why you should always examine both directions of a rule.

Putting It Together

MetricMeasuresRangeThreshold
SupportFrequency / prevalence of the itemset0 to 1min_support (e.g., 0.01–0.05)
ConfidenceReliability of rule, given antecedent0 to 1min_confidence (e.g., 0.5–0.8)
LiftStrength of association vs. independence0 to ∞lift > 1 (meaningful); > 1.5 (strong)
Rule of thumb for minimum thresholds:
With millions of transactions (e-commerce), min_support as low as 0.001 (0.1%) can still catch important patterns. With hundreds of transactions (small survey), you might need 0.1 (10%) to avoid noise. Always tune thresholds to your domain. Confidence ≥ 0.5 and lift ≥ 1.0 are common starting points.

Additional Metrics: Conviction and Leverage

Two other metrics you may encounter:

Leverage

leverage(A → B) = support(A ∪ B) − support(A) × support(B)

Measures how much more co-occurrence there is than expected under independence. Zero means independence. Ranges from −1 to 1. Similar story to lift, but in additive rather than ratio form. mlxtend computes it.

Conviction

conviction(A → B) = (1 − support(B)) / (1 − confidence(A → B))

Measures how often the rule makes an incorrect prediction. High conviction = rule rarely fails. Ranges from 0 to ∞; conviction = 1 means independence; ∞ means perfect confidence (rule never wrong in one direction). Less commonly used than lift.

💡 Conviction by Hand — Why the Formula Goes to Infinity
Using a tiny 5-transaction dataset where {diapers} → {beer} holds perfectly (every diaper purchase includes beer), and support(beer) = 0.60:

conviction = (1 − support(B)) / (1 − confidence)

Perfect rule — diapers → beer (confidence = 1.00, support(beer) = 0.60)
conviction = (1 − 0.60) / (1 − 1.00) = 0.40 / 0.00 = ∞
Every single diaper transaction in our data also contains beer — zero failures. When confidence = 1.0, the denominator (1 − 1.0) = 0, and dividing by zero signals "this rule has zero failures," which is represented as infinity.

Moderate rule — bread → cereal (confidence = 0.75, support(cereal) = 0.60)
conviction = (1 − 0.60) / (1 − 0.75) = 0.40 / 0.25 = 1.60
This rule is 60% better than random. At conviction = 1.0, bread and cereal would be independent (knowing someone bought bread tells you nothing about cereal). Conviction = 1.60 means the rule performs better than that baseline.

Quick reference: conviction = 1 → independence (no predictive value); conviction > 1 → rule beats random; conviction = ∞ → perfect rule (confidence = 1.0, never fails).

7.3 The Apriori Algorithm — How Frequent Itemsets Are Found

AN300 AN306

Brute force is impossible. If a retailer sells 10,000 products, the number of possible itemsets is 2^10,000 — astronomically large. The Apriori algorithm uses a clever pruning strategy to explore only promising itemsets.

The Apriori Property (Anti-Monotonicity)

Key insight: If an itemset is infrequent (below min_support), then every superset of that itemset is also infrequent.

Equivalently: if an itemset is frequent, then every subset of it is also frequent.
🔑 Why this pruning makes sense — the umbrella logic:
Think about it this way: if only 5 out of 1,000 people bought {milk AND beer AND diapers}, then it's impossible for more than 5 people to have bought {milk AND beer AND diapers AND wipes}. The 4-item set can only be a subset of the 3-item set's transactions.

So if {milk, beer} is rare (say, 2% of baskets), there's zero point checking {milk, beer, diapers}, {milk, beer, wipes}, {milk, beer, cereal} — they can only be rarer. We skip them all. With a store selling 10,000 products, this pruning eliminates billions of combinations instantly.

This lets us prune the search space dramatically. If {milk, beer} is infrequent, we don't need to check {milk, beer, diapers}, {milk, beer, bread}, etc. We prune the entire branch.

The Algorithm — Step by Step

1
Generate 1-itemsets (k=1). Count how often each individual item appears. Keep only those with support ≥ min_support. This is the set of frequent 1-itemsets L₁.
2
Generate candidate k+1-itemsets by joining. Combine pairs of frequent k-itemsets that share the first k−1 items (lexicographic join). For k=1, every pair of frequent items forms a candidate 2-itemset.
3
Prune candidates using Apriori property. Any candidate k-itemset whose (k−1)-item subsets are NOT all frequent is pruned immediately without counting. This prevents unnecessary database scans.
4
Scan database to count surviving candidates. Compute support for each surviving candidate by scanning all transactions. Keep only those with support ≥ min_support. These are Lₖ.
5
Repeat until no new frequent itemsets found. Increment k and repeat from Step 2. Stop when no new candidates can be generated.
6
Generate rules from all frequent itemsets. For each frequent itemset F with |F| ≥ 2, generate all possible A → (F − A) rules where A ⊂ F. Keep those with confidence ≥ min_confidence (and optionally min_lift).

Worked Example — 5 Transactions

Using the 5 transactions from Module 7.1 with min_support = 0.6 (3/5 transactions):

Step 1 — Frequent 1-itemsets L₁

ItemCountSupportFrequent?
milk30.60✓ Yes
bread40.80✓ Yes
eggs10.20✗ No
diapers30.60✓ Yes
beer30.60✓ Yes
cereal30.60✓ Yes

L₁ = {milk, bread, diapers, beer, cereal}. Eggs dropped (support 0.20 < 0.60).

Step 2 — Candidate 2-itemsets C₂ (join L₁ with itself)

All pairs from L₁: {milk,bread}, {milk,diapers}, {milk,beer}, {milk,cereal}, {bread,diapers}, {bread,beer}, {bread,cereal}, {diapers,beer}, {diapers,cereal}, {beer,cereal} — 10 candidates. All subsets of size 1 are in L₁, so none pruned yet. Count each by scanning transactions.

Step 3 — Frequent 2-itemsets L₂ (keep support ≥ 0.60)

2-itemsetCountSupportFrequent?
{milk, bread}20.40✗
{milk, diapers}20.40✗
{milk, beer}20.40✗
{milk, cereal}10.20✗
{bread, diapers}20.40✗
{bread, beer}20.40✗
{bread, cereal}30.60✓
{diapers, beer}30.60✓
{diapers, cereal}20.40✗
{beer, cereal}20.40✗

L₂ = {{bread,cereal}, {diapers,beer}}. For 3-itemsets, we'd need pairs from L₂ that share one item — but {bread,cereal} and {diapers,beer} share no items, so C₃ = ∅. Algorithm terminates.

Generate Rules from L₂

From {diapers, beer} (support = 0.60), min_confidence = 0.60:

  • diapers → beer: conf = 0.60/0.60 = 1.00 ✓, lift = 1.00/0.60 = 1.67
  • beer → diapers: conf = 0.60/0.60 = 1.00 ✓, lift = 1.00/0.60 = 1.67

From {bread, cereal} (support = 0.60):

  • bread → cereal: conf = 0.60/0.80 = 0.75 ✓, lift = 0.75/0.60 = 1.25
  • cereal → bread: conf = 0.60/0.60 = 1.00 ✓, lift = 1.00/0.80 = 1.25

Time Complexity

Apriori must scan the full database once per itemset level k. With large datasets or low min_support, this is slow. The number of database scans = max itemset length. For large datasets, FP-Growth (Module 7.7) is preferred.

7.4 mlxtend Implementation — Full Python Workflow

AN306 AN300

Python's mlxtend library implements Apriori and FP-Growth for us. The workflow has three stages: encode the data, mine frequent itemsets, then generate rules.

Installing mlxtend

pip install mlxtend

Stage 1 — Encode Transactions into One-Hot Format

mlxtend requires a one-hot encoded DataFrame (also called the transaction matrix): each row is a transaction, each column is an item, cell = True/False (item in basket or not).

import pandas as pd
from mlxtend.preprocessing import TransactionEncoder
from mlxtend.frequent_patterns import apriori, association_rules

# Raw transactions — list of lists
transactions = [
    ['milk', 'bread', 'eggs'],
    ['milk', 'diapers', 'beer', 'cereal'],
    ['bread', 'diapers', 'beer', 'cereal'],
    ['milk', 'bread', 'diapers', 'beer'],
    ['bread', 'cereal']
]

# Step 1: Fit TransactionEncoder
te = TransactionEncoder()
te_array = te.fit(transactions).transform(transactions)

# Step 2: Convert to DataFrame
df_te = pd.DataFrame(te_array, columns=te.columns_)
print(df_te)
#        beer  bread  cereal  diapers   eggs   milk
# 0     False   True   False    False   True   True
# 1      True  False    True     True  False   True
# 2      True   True    True     True  False  False
# 3      True   True   False     True  False   True
# 4     False   True    True    False  False  False

Stage 2 — Mine Frequent Itemsets with Apriori

# Step 3: Run Apriori to find frequent itemsets
frequent_itemsets = apriori(
    df_te,
    min_support=0.4,         # at least 40% of transactions
    use_colnames=True,       # use column names, not indices
    max_len=None             # no limit on itemset size
)

# Sort by support descending
frequent_itemsets.sort_values('support', ascending=False, inplace=True)
print(frequent_itemsets.head(10))
#    support         itemsets
# 0      0.8          (bread)
# 1      0.6         (cereal)
# 2      0.6        (diapers)
# ...
# 7      0.6  (bread, cereal)
# 8      0.6  (beer, diapers)

# Add itemset length column for filtering
frequent_itemsets['length'] = frequent_itemsets['itemsets'].apply(len)
print(frequent_itemsets[frequent_itemsets['length'] >= 2])
Important: apriori() returns itemsets as frozensets. You can't index them with [0]. Use list(itemset) to convert when needed. The use_colnames=True argument is essential — without it, columns are integer indices and the output is unreadable.

Stage 3 — Generate Association Rules

# Step 4: Generate rules from frequent itemsets
rules = association_rules(
    frequent_itemsets,
    metric="lift",           # filter by this metric
    min_threshold=1.0        # minimum value for the metric
)

# Key columns returned:
# antecedents, consequents — frozensets
# antecedent support, consequent support — individual support values
# support — joint support of the rule
# confidence — P(consequent | antecedent)
# lift — confidence / consequent support
# leverage — support - (ant_support * cons_support)
# conviction — (1 - cons_support) / (1 - confidence)

print(rules[['antecedents', 'consequents', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']].to_string())
#          antecedents    consequents  support  confidence      lift
# 0          (cereal)        (bread)      0.6        1.00   1.25000
# 1           (bread)       (cereal)      0.6        0.75   1.25000
# 2         (diapers)         (beer)      0.6        1.00   1.66667
# 3            (beer)       (diapers)     0.6        1.00   1.66667

Filtering Rules Programmatically

# Filter by multiple conditions
strong_rules = rules[
    (rules['confidence'] >= 0.7) &
    (rules['lift'] > 1.2) &
    (rules['support'] >= 0.05)
].copy()

# Sort by lift descending
strong_rules.sort_values('lift', ascending=False, inplace=True)
print(strong_rules.head(20))

# Count rules meeting criteria
print(f"Total rules found: {len(rules)}")
print(f"Strong rules (conf≥0.7, lift>1.2): {len(strong_rules)}")

Converting Frozensets to Readable Strings

# Pretty-print rules as "A → B" format
def rule_to_string(row):
    ant = ', '.join(sorted(row['antecedents']))
    cons = ', '.join(sorted(row['consequents']))
    return f"{ant} → {cons}"

strong_rules['rule'] = strong_rules.apply(rule_to_string, axis=1)
print(strong_rules[['rule', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']].to_string())

Visualizing Rule Space

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import matplotlib.cm as cm
import numpy as np

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(14, 5))

# --- Plot 1: Support vs Confidence (colored by lift)
sc = axes[0].scatter(
    rules['support'], rules['confidence'],
    c=rules['lift'], cmap='RdYlGn',
    alpha=0.7, s=80, edgecolors='none'
)
plt.colorbar(sc, ax=axes[0], label='Lift')
axes[0].set_xlabel('Support')
axes[0].set_ylabel('Confidence')
axes[0].set_title('Association Rule Space')
axes[0].axhline(y=0.7, color='red', linestyle='--', alpha=0.5, label='conf=0.7')
axes[0].axvline(x=0.05, color='blue', linestyle='--', alpha=0.5, label='supp=0.05')
axes[0].legend(fontsize=8)

# --- Plot 2: Top N rules by lift (horizontal bar chart)
top = rules.nlargest(15, 'lift').copy()
top['rule'] = top.apply(rule_to_string, axis=1)
top = top.sort_values('lift')

axes[1].barh(range(len(top)), top['lift'], color='#7c8ff5', alpha=0.85)
axes[1].set_yticks(range(len(top)))
axes[1].set_yticklabels(top['rule'], fontsize=8)
axes[1].axvline(x=1, color='red', linestyle='--', alpha=0.5, label='lift=1 (independence)')
axes[1].set_xlabel('Lift')
axes[1].set_title('Top 15 Rules by Lift')
axes[1].legend(fontsize=8)

plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('association_rules.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()
Colab tip: With large datasets, apriori() can produce hundreds of thousands of rules. Always start with high min_support and work down. Use max_len=3 to limit to 3-item rules first, then explore larger combinations selectively.

7.5 Interpreting Association Rules

AN300 AN306

Reading a Rule Completely

When you encounter a rule, report all three numbers, not just one:

# Example rule output:
# antecedents: (whole milk)
# consequents: (other vegetables)
# support:     0.0748   → 7.5% of all baskets contain both
# confidence:  0.4867   → 48.7% of whole-milk buyers also buy vegetables
# lift:        1.5136   → 51% more likely than independent chance

Translate in plain language: "Customers who buy whole milk also buy other vegetables nearly half the time — and they're 51% more likely to do so than a randomly selected customer."

The Confidence Trap

High confidence alone is not enough. If beer has 95% support (bought in almost every transaction), then ANY rule with beer as the consequent will have confidence near 95% — even a rule like {bubble wrap} → {beer} would be 95% confident! Lift corrects for this. A rule with confidence=0.80 and lift=0.9 is WORSE than one with confidence=0.50 and lift=2.1 — the first is weaker than expected given how common the consequent is.

Directionality Matters

Two rules can describe the same relationship but have very different confidence levels:

  • {premium whiskey} → {soda water}: conf = 0.80, lift = 2.3
  • {soda water} → {premium whiskey}: conf = 0.12, lift = 2.3 (same lift, low confidence — soda is very common)

The first rule drives action: "stock soda water next to premium whiskey." The second doesn't — most people buying soda water are not buying premium whiskey.

Actionability Criteria

A rule is actionable if:

  1. It's new information — {tea} → {milk} is obvious in British culture; not actionable
  2. It's large enough to matter — support above a business-meaningful threshold
  3. It's reliable — confidence high enough to act on without excessive misses
  4. The association is real — lift meaningfully above 1
  5. Action is possible — you can actually change shelf placement, offer bundles, trigger a recommendation

Interpreting Lift Values

Lift ValueInterpretationBusiness Implication
< 1Negative association (items avoid each other)Competing products; don't cross-promote
= 1Independence; no associationNo relationship to exploit
1.0 – 1.3Weak positive associationExists but marginally useful
1.3 – 2.0Moderate positive associationWorth testing in recommendation engine
2.0 – 5.0Strong positive associationHigh-value cross-sell opportunity
> 5.0Very strong associationNear-complementary products; bundle deals

7.6 Filtering, Ranking, and Selecting Rules

AN306

The Rule Explosion Problem

With real retail data (50,000+ transactions, 1,000+ products), Apriori with min_support=0.01 can generate millions of rules. You need a systematic approach to surface only the useful ones.

Strategy 1 — Multi-metric Filter

def filter_rules(rules_df, min_support=0.01, min_confidence=0.5,
                 min_lift=1.2, max_len_ant=3, max_len_cons=1):
    """
    Filter association rules by multiple criteria.

    Parameters
    ----------
    min_support    : minimum support of the full rule (A ∪ B)
    min_confidence : minimum confidence
    min_lift       : minimum lift (1.0 = independence; 1.2 = moderate filter)
    max_len_ant    : max number of items in antecedent
    max_len_cons   : max items in consequent (1 = simpler, more interpretable rules)
    """
    rules_df = rules_df.copy()
    rules_df['ant_len'] = rules_df['antecedents'].apply(len)
    rules_df['cons_len'] = rules_df['consequents'].apply(len)

    mask = (
        (rules_df['support']    >= min_support) &
        (rules_df['confidence'] >= min_confidence) &
        (rules_df['lift']       >= min_lift) &
        (rules_df['ant_len']   <= max_len_ant) &
        (rules_df['cons_len']  <= max_len_cons)
    )
    return rules_df[mask].sort_values('lift', ascending=False).reset_index(drop=True)

filtered = filter_rules(rules, min_support=0.01, min_confidence=0.5, min_lift=1.5)
print(f"Filtered from {len(rules)} to {len(filtered)} rules")

Strategy 2 — Target-Item Search

When you want rules about a specific product (e.g., "what leads people to buy Product X?"):

def rules_for_item(rules_df, item, role='consequent'):
    """
    Find all rules where \`item\` appears in the specified role.

    role: 'consequent'  → "what leads to buying X?"
          'antecedent'  → "if someone buys X, what else do they buy?"
    """
    if role == 'consequent':
        mask = rules_df['consequents'].apply(lambda x: item in x)
    else:
        mask = rules_df['antecedents'].apply(lambda x: item in x)

    return rules_df[mask].sort_values('lift', ascending=False)

# "What other items predict beer purchases?"
beer_rules = rules_for_item(rules, 'beer', role='consequent')
print(beer_rules[['antecedents', 'consequents', 'confidence', 'lift']].head(10))

Strategy 3 — Diversity Filter (Deduplication)

Rules like {A} → {B} and {A, C} → {B} with similar lift are often redundant. The simpler rule (fewer antecedent items) is usually preferred for actionability. Sort by lift descending, then optionally deduplicate on consequent.

# Keep only the top rule per consequent (simplest, highest-lift)
top_per_consequent = (
    filtered
    .sort_values(['consequents', 'lift', 'ant_len'],
                 ascending=[True, False, True])
    .drop_duplicates(subset='consequents', keep='first')
)
print(top_per_consequent[['antecedents', 'consequents', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']])

Strategy 4 — Heatmap of Item Co-occurrence

Before running Apriori, a co-occurrence heatmap gives intuition about which items are worth exploring:

import seaborn as sns

# Build co-occurrence matrix from the one-hot DataFrame
# (df_te is the TransactionEncoder output)
cooccurrence = df_te.T.dot(df_te)  # item × item matrix, diagonal = item count
# Zero out diagonal (self-co-occurrence)
for col in cooccurrence.columns:
    cooccurrence.loc[col, col] = 0

# Normalize to co-occurrence rate (fraction of transactions)
n_transactions = len(df_te)
cooccurrence_rate = cooccurrence / n_transactions

# Plot
plt.figure(figsize=(12, 10))
sns.heatmap(
    cooccurrence_rate,
    cmap='Blues', annot=False,
    xticklabels=True, yticklabels=True,
    linewidths=0.3
)
plt.title('Item Co-occurrence Rate (fraction of all transactions)')
plt.xticks(rotation=45, ha='right', fontsize=8)
plt.yticks(rotation=0, fontsize=8)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()

7.7 FP-Growth — The Faster Alternative to Apriori

AN306

Why FP-Growth?

Apriori's weakness is the number of database scans — it scans the full transaction database once per itemset level. With millions of transactions, that's slow. FP-Growth (Frequent Pattern Growth) scans the database only twice and stores the data in a compact in-memory tree structure called the FP-tree.

How It Works (Conceptual)

1
First scan: Count support of all individual items. Discard infrequent ones. Sort remaining items by frequency descending.
2
Second scan: Build the FP-tree. Each transaction is inserted as a path from root to leaf, with shared prefixes merged. Each node stores the item name and its count along that path.
3
Mine the FP-tree: For each frequent item (starting from least frequent), extract all paths leading to it (called the conditional pattern base). Build a conditional FP-tree and mine it recursively. Each level produces frequent itemsets without additional database scans.

The result is identical to Apriori — the same set of frequent itemsets — but typically 10–100× faster on large data.

Using FP-Growth in mlxtend

from mlxtend.frequent_patterns import fpgrowth

# SAME interface as apriori — just swap the function
frequent_itemsets_fp = fpgrowth(
    df_te,
    min_support=0.01,
    use_colnames=True,
    max_len=None
)

# Generate rules identically
rules_fp = association_rules(
    frequent_itemsets_fp,
    metric="lift",
    min_threshold=1.0
)

print(f"Frequent itemsets (FP-Growth): {len(frequent_itemsets_fp)}")
print(f"Rules generated: {len(rules_fp)}")
When to use which:
Apriori — small datasets (≤10,000 transactions), educational settings, when you want to trace the pruning logic step by step.
FP-Growth — real production scenarios, datasets >10,000 transactions, when speed matters. Use fpgrowth by default and fall back to apriori only for debugging or teaching.

7.8 Common Mistakes and Exam Traps

AN300
Trap 1 — Confidence does not equal predictive accuracy. High confidence for A → B means "when A occurs, B occurs often." It says nothing about what happens when A does NOT occur. It is NOT accuracy in the classification sense. Association rules are not classifiers.
Trap 2 — Support is symmetric, confidence is not. support(A → B) = support(B → A) always. confidence(A → B) ≠ confidence(B → A) in general. Lift is symmetric: lift(A → B) = lift(B → A) always.
Trap 3 — Apriori property is anti-monotone for support, not confidence. The anti-monotone pruning rule — "if infrequent, all supersets infrequent" — applies to support only. Confidence does not follow this property. You cannot prune candidate itemsets based on the confidence of their subsets.
Trap 4 — min_support must be set before rules are generated. You cannot retroactively lower min_support after calling apriori() and expect to find more rules. Apriori prunes itemsets during the mining phase. If you lower min_support, you must re-run apriori() from scratch. The frequent_itemsets DataFrame only contains itemsets that met the original threshold.
Trap 5 — lift = 1 does not mean "no co-occurrence." Items with lift = 1 DO co-occur in some transactions. It means they co-occur at EXACTLY the rate you'd expect if they were independent. Items that never co-occur have support(A ∪ B) = 0 and confidence = 0, but lift is undefined (division by zero). mlxtend will return NaN for those.
Trap 6 — Association rules do not imply causation. Diapers and beer co-occurring doesn't mean buying diapers causes beer purchase (or vice versa). Both may be driven by a confounding variable (new parents restocking on a weekend). Association mining finds co-occurrence patterns, not causal mechanisms. Never interpret a rule as "buying A causes buying B."
Trap 7 — Lowering min_support is exponential in cost. Halving min_support can more than double (sometimes 10×) the number of frequent itemsets. The relationship is not linear. Always check item count and expected rule count before running with very low thresholds on large data.

Choosing min_support in Practice

Dataset SizeTypical min_supportRationale
< 100 transactions0.20 – 0.50High threshold needed to avoid noise
100 – 10,0000.05 – 0.20Standard range
10,000 – 1M0.01 – 0.05Even rare rules have enough occurrences
> 1M transactions0.001 – 0.01Very rare co-occurrences still statistically reliable

Lab 7 — Market Basket Analysis Lab

This lab uses the Groceries dataset (from the R arules package), a classic market basket analysis dataset of 9,835 grocery transactions. We'll mine frequent itemsets, generate rules, and interpret the most actionable findings.

Setup — Load and Inspect

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import matplotlib.cm as cm
from mlxtend.preprocessing import TransactionEncoder
from mlxtend.frequent_patterns import fpgrowth, association_rules

# --- Load the Groceries dataset ---
# Option 1: Load from mlxtend's built-in datasets
# (not always available; use the CSV approach below)

# Option 2: Download from GitHub
url = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/stedy/Machine-Learning-with-R-datasets/master/groceries.csv"
raw = pd.read_csv(url, header=None)

# Each row is one transaction (comma-separated items in one string)
# Split into lists
transactions = raw[0].str.split(',').tolist()
print(f"Number of transactions: {len(transactions)}")
print(f"Sample transaction: {transactions[0]}")
print(f"Sample transaction: {transactions[1]}")
# --- Basic statistics ---
lengths = [len(t) for t in transactions]
print(f"Mean basket size:   {np.mean(lengths):.2f}")
print(f"Median basket size: {np.median(lengths):.1f}")
print(f"Max basket size:    {max(lengths)}")
print(f"Min basket size:    {min(lengths)}")

# Item frequency
from collections import Counter
all_items = [item for transaction in transactions for item in transaction]
item_counts = Counter(all_items)
print(f"\\nTotal unique items: {len(item_counts)}")

# Most common items
top20 = pd.DataFrame(item_counts.most_common(20),
                     columns=['item', 'count'])
top20['support'] = top20['count'] / len(transactions)
print(top20)

Cell 2 — One-Hot Encode and Visualize Item Frequency

# --- Encode ---
te = TransactionEncoder()
te_array = te.fit(transactions).transform(transactions)
df_te = pd.DataFrame(te_array, columns=te.columns_)

print(f"One-hot matrix shape: {df_te.shape}")  # (9835, 169)

# --- Plot top 20 items by support ---
item_support = df_te.mean().sort_values(ascending=False).head(20)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 6))
bars = ax.barh(range(len(item_support)), item_support.values[::-1],
               color='#7c8ff5', alpha=0.85, edgecolor='none')
ax.set_yticks(range(len(item_support)))
ax.set_yticklabels(item_support.index[::-1])
ax.set_xlabel('Support (fraction of transactions)')
ax.set_title('Top 20 Items by Support — Groceries Dataset')
ax.axvline(x=0.1, color='red', linestyle='--', alpha=0.6, label='support = 0.10')
ax.legend()
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('item_frequency.png', dpi=150, bbox_inches='tight')
plt.show()

print("Note: 'whole milk' appears in", f"{item_support['whole milk']:.1%}",
      "of all transactions — the most common single item.")

Cell 3 — Mine Frequent Itemsets with FP-Growth

# --- FP-Growth ---
frequent_itemsets = fpgrowth(
    df_te,
    min_support=0.01,    # 1% = at least ~98 of 9,835 transactions
    use_colnames=True,
    max_len=4            # limit to 4-item rules for speed
)

frequent_itemsets['length'] = frequent_itemsets['itemsets'].apply(len)
print(f"Frequent itemsets found: {len(frequent_itemsets)}")
print("\\nBreakdown by itemset length:")
print(frequent_itemsets['length'].value_counts().sort_index())

# Top 15 frequent 2-itemsets (most useful for rules)
top_pairs = (frequent_itemsets
             [frequent_itemsets['length'] == 2]
             .sort_values('support', ascending=False)
             .head(15))
print("\\nTop 15 frequent item pairs:")
print(top_pairs[['support', 'itemsets']].to_string())

Cell 4 — Generate and Filter Association Rules

# --- Generate rules ---
rules = association_rules(
    frequent_itemsets,
    metric="confidence",
    min_threshold=0.3    # start broad, we'll filter
)

print(f"Total rules generated: {len(rules)}")
print(f"Rules with lift > 1:   {(rules['lift'] > 1).sum()}")
print(f"Rules with lift > 2:   {(rules['lift'] > 2).sum()}")
print(f"Rules with lift > 3:   {(rules['lift'] > 3).sum()}")

# --- Apply multi-metric filter ---
strong_rules = rules[
    (rules['confidence'] >= 0.50) &
    (rules['lift']       >= 2.00) &
    (rules['support']    >= 0.01)
].copy()

strong_rules['ant_len'] = strong_rules['antecedents'].apply(len)
strong_rules['rule'] = strong_rules.apply(
    lambda r: ', '.join(sorted(r['antecedents'])) + ' → ' +
              ', '.join(sorted(r['consequents'])), axis=1
)
strong_rules = strong_rules.sort_values('lift', ascending=False)

print(f"\\nStrong rules (conf≥0.50, lift≥2.0, supp≥0.01): {len(strong_rules)}")
print("\\nTop 20 rules by lift:")
print(strong_rules[['rule', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']].head(20).to_string())

Cell 5 — Rule Space Scatter Plot

# --- Scatter: support vs confidence, colored by lift ---
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(14, 5))
fig.patch.set_facecolor('#0f1117')
for ax in axes:
    ax.set_facecolor('#161922')

    cmap='RdYlGn', alpha=0.5, s=20, edgecolors='none'

Cell 6 — Targeted Rule Search + Business Interpretation

# --- What leads customers to buy whole milk? ---
def rules_for_item(rules_df, item, role='consequent'):
    if role == 'consequent':
        mask = rules_df['consequents'].apply(lambda x: item in x)
    else:
        mask = rules_df['antecedents'].apply(lambda x: item in x)
    return rules_df[mask].sort_values('lift', ascending=False)

milk_rules = rules_for_item(rules, 'whole milk', role='consequent')
milk_rules['rule'] = milk_rules.apply(
    lambda r: ', '.join(sorted(r['antecedents'])) + ' → whole milk', axis=1
)

print("=== Top 10 rules predicting whole milk purchase ===")
print(milk_rules[['rule', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']].head(10).to_string())

# --- Mutual strongest rules (both directions high lift) ---
print("\\n=== Top overall rules by lift ===")
print(strong_rules[['rule', 'support', 'confidence', 'lift']].head(10).to_string())

# --- Business interpretation of top rule ---
top_rule = strong_rules.iloc[0]
ant = ', '.join(sorted(top_rule['antecedents']))
cons = ', '.join(sorted(top_rule['consequents']))
print(f"\\n=== Business Interpretation of Top Rule ===")
print(f"Rule:       {ant} → {cons}")
print(f"Support:    {top_rule['support']:.4f}")
print(f"Confidence: {top_rule['confidence']:.4f}")
print(f"Lift:       {top_rule['lift']:.4f}")
print(f"\\nIn plain language:")
print(f"  → {top_rule['support']:.1%} of all grocery baskets contain both '{ant}' and '{cons}'.")
print(f"  → Customers who buy '{ant}' also buy '{cons}' {top_rule['confidence']:.1%} of the time.")
print(f"  → That's {top_rule['lift']:.2f}x more likely than a random basket containing '{cons}'.")
print(f"\\nBusiness action: Place '{cons}' near '{ant}' in the store,")
print(f"or offer a bundle discount on these items together.")
Checklist before submitting Lab 7:
  • ✓ TransactionEncoder used to build one-hot matrix (not manual dummy variables)
  • ✓ fpgrowth used (not apriori) for speed
  • ✓ All three metrics — support, confidence, lift — reported for each rule
  • ✓ Rules filtered by all three metrics, not just one
  • ✓ Rule space scatter plot included
  • ✓ Top rules interpreted in business plain language
  • ✓ No causal language used (avoid "causes," "leads to" — use "associated with," "predicts")
  • ✓ Both FP-Growth result counts and rule count reported in write-up

Common Lab Mistakes to Avoid

  • Skipping TransactionEncoder: Manually looping to create dummy variables is error-prone. Use TransactionEncoder — it handles multi-item baskets and ordering correctly.
  • Using apriori on 10K+ row data: It will run, but slowly. Use fpgrowth.
  • Not setting use_colnames=True: Output shows integer indices instead of product names — unreadable.
  • Reporting only confidence: Always pair with lift. A "high confidence" rule with lift ≈ 1 is useless.
  • Treating rules as causal: Never write "buying diapers causes beer purchase." Write "diapers and beer are frequently co-purchased."
  • Not checking for NaN in lift: If a consequent has 100% support (appears in every transaction), you'll get lift = confidence/1 = confidence (fine), but items that never appear alone can cause NaN conviction values. Filter with rules.dropna() if needed.
