Part 8 — NLP & Text Analytics

From raw strings to insights — tokenization, TF-IDF, cosine similarity, sentiment, and LDA topic modeling from scratch

8.1 Why Text Analytics?

AN306 AN300 docx

Roughly 80% of the world's data is unstructured — emails, reviews, social media posts, job descriptions, support tickets, news articles, contracts. None of it has a column labeled "rating" or "sentiment." Yet it contains enormous signal that numerical tables miss entirely.

Natural Language Processing (NLP) is the set of techniques that converts raw text into numerical representations that machine learning algorithms can understand. The field bridges linguistics and statistics — you need both to interpret the output correctly.

Examples in Data Analytics Practice

  • Customer reviews — extract which product features drive satisfaction or complaints
  • Job postings — identify in-demand skills, cluster roles by required competencies, track trends over time
  • Survey responses — topic-model open-ended responses to find themes without reading 10,000 answers manually
  • Social media — sentiment tracking, brand monitoring, event detection
  • Legal / compliance — clause extraction, contract similarity, anomaly detection in filings
  • Healthcare — ICD code prediction from clinical notes, adverse event detection

The NLP Pipeline — Big Picture

Every NLP project follows the same conceptual flow:

RAW TEXT
Unstructured strings — documents, posts, descriptions. May contain HTML, punctuation, URLs, emojis, inconsistent casing.
CLEAN
Lowercase, strip HTML/URLs/punctuation, remove noise. The goal is a normalized string that represents only meaningful content.
TOKENIZE
Split the string into individual tokens (words or sub-words). Now you have a list of strings instead of one big string.
FILTER
Remove stopwords (common words with no signal: "the", "a", "is"). Optionally filter by part of speech.
NORMALIZE
Stem or lemmatize — reduce words to their root form so "running," "runs," and "ran" all map to the same token.
VECTORIZE
Convert token lists to numbers. Options: Bag of Words (counts), TF-IDF (weighted counts), word embeddings (vectors). Now you have a numeric matrix.
MODEL
Apply ML: classification, clustering, topic modeling, similarity search, sentiment analysis. Standard sklearn/gensim on the numeric matrix.
Key packages in this part: nltk (tokenization, stopwords, stemming, lemmatization), sklearn.feature_extraction.text (CountVectorizer, TfidfVectorizer), sklearn.metrics.pairwise (cosine_similarity), textblob (simple sentiment), vaderSentiment (social media sentiment), gensim (LDA topic modeling), pyLDAvis (LDA visualization)

8.2 Tokenization — Splitting Text into Meaningful Units

AN306 docx

Tokenization is the process of breaking a string into a list of tokens. A token is usually a word, but can be a sentence, sub-word, or character depending on the task.

Why Tokenization Is Not Just str.split()

The naive approach is text.split(' ') (split on spaces). This fails in several cases:

  • "don't" → ["don't"] — is this one token or two ("do" + "n't")?
  • "New York" — two words but one concept
  • "Hello!" → ["Hello!"] — the exclamation mark is attached to the word
  • Multiple spaces, tabs, newlines — split() handles some but not all edge cases cleanly

NLTK's word tokenizer handles contractions, punctuation separation, and other linguistic edge cases:

import nltk
nltk.download('punkt')
nltk.download('punkt_tab')   # required in newer NLTK versions

from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize, sent_tokenize

text = "I don't think New York's prices are fair. It's crazy!"

# Word tokenization
tokens = word_tokenize(text)
print(tokens)
# ['I', 'do', "n't", 'think', 'New', 'York', "'s", 'prices',
#  'are', 'fair', '.', 'It', "'s", 'crazy', '!']

# Sentence tokenization
sentences = sent_tokenize(text)
print(sentences)
# ["I don't think New York's prices are fair.", "It's crazy!"]

Key Observations

  • "don't" becomes ["do", "n't"] — NLTK splits contractions into their components
  • Punctuation (periods, exclamation marks) become separate tokens
  • "New York's" becomes ["New", "York", "'s"] — the possessive is separated
  • Case is preserved; lowercasing is a separate step

Alternative Tokenizers

from nltk.tokenize import TweetTokenizer, RegexpTokenizer

# TweetTokenizer — preserves @mentions, #hashtags, emoticons
tweet_tok = TweetTokenizer()
tweet = "I love #Python! @codemaster Great work :)"
print(tweet_tok.tokenize(tweet))
# ['I', 'love', '#Python', '!', '@codemaster', 'Great', 'work', ':)']

# RegexpTokenizer — tokenize using a custom regex pattern
# Pattern: match sequences of word characters (letters, digits, underscores)
re_tok = RegexpTokenizer(r'\\w+')
print(re_tok.tokenize("Hello, world! This is test-1."))
# ['Hello', 'world', 'This', 'is', 'test', '1']
# Note: hyphens and punctuation are stripped by this pattern
Which tokenizer to choose:
word_tokenize — general English prose (articles, reviews, descriptions)
TweetTokenizer — social media where @mentions and #hashtags are meaningful
RegexpTokenizer(r'\\w+') — simple word extraction when you just want alphanumeric tokens and don't care about contractions
sent_tokenize — whenever you need sentence-level analysis before word tokenization

8.3 Text Cleaning — Normalizing Before Tokenization

AN306 docx

Cleaning always happens before or alongside tokenization. The goal is to remove noise — anything that isn't meaningful signal — so the vocabulary is consistent and compact.

Standard Cleaning Steps

import re
import string

def clean_text(text):
    """
    Comprehensive text cleaning pipeline.
    Returns a cleaned, lowercased string ready for tokenization.
    """
    # 1. Lowercase everything
    text = text.lower()

    # 2. Remove HTML tags (e.g., 
, 
, &)
    text = re.sub(r'<[^>]+>', ' ', text)       # remove HTML tags
    text = re.sub(r'&\\w+;', ' ', text)          # remove HTML entities (&  )

    # 3. Remove URLs
    text = re.sub(r'http\\S+|www\\.\\S+', ' ', text)

    # 4. Remove email addresses
    text = re.sub(r'\\S+@\\S+', ' ', text)

    # 5. Remove numbers (optional — depends on task)
    # Keep: when numbers are meaningful (e.g., "5 years experience")
    # Remove: when numbers add noise (e.g., product codes)
    # text = re.sub(r'\\d+', ' ', text)   # uncomment to remove numbers

    # 6. Remove punctuation
    text = text.translate(str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation))

    # 7. Collapse multiple whitespace into single space
    text = re.sub(r'\\s+', ' ', text).strip()

    return text

# Test
raw = "Check out https://example.com! It's a 5-star product. & more!"
print(clean_text(raw))
# "check out  it's a 5 star product  more"

Using Regex — The Correct Way

Regex patterns in Python are written as raw strings using the r'...' prefix. This tells Python NOT to interpret backslashes as escape sequences before passing the string to the regex engine.

Analytics Vidhya Source Error #1: The Analytics Vidhya NLP article (linked in the course readings) contains this code: 
tokens = re.split('W+', text)
This is WRONG. Without the r prefix, Python treats \\W as a backslash followed by W (a string escape) rather than the regex meta-character "non-word character." The correct code is: 
tokens = re.split(r'\\W+', text)
The r prefix before the quote makes it a raw string — backslashes pass through to the regex engine unchanged, so \\W is correctly interpreted as "any non-word character."

Common Regex Patterns for Text

PatternMatchesUse Case
\\w+One or more word characters (letters, digits, _)Extract words
\\W+One or more NON-word charactersSplit on punctuation/spaces
\\d+One or more digitsFind/remove numbers
\\s+One or more whitespace charactersCollapse whitespace
[^a-zA-Z\\s]Not a letter and not whitespaceKeep only letters
http\\S+URL starting with httpRemove URLs
<[^>]+>Anything inside <> bracketsStrip HTML tags
\\b\\w{1,2}\\bWords of 1–2 charactersRemove very short tokens

8.4 Stopword Removal

AN306 docx

Stopwords are extremely common words that carry little semantic information: "the," "a," "is," "in," "it," "to," "and," "of." Including them bloats the vocabulary, adds noise, and makes TF-IDF scores less discriminative. Removing them is standard practice.

NLTK Stopwords

import nltk
nltk.download('stopwords')
from nltk.corpus import stopwords

# English stopwords
stop_words = set(stopwords.words('english'))
print(f"Number of English stopwords: {len(stop_words)}")  # ~179

# Preview
print(sorted(list(stop_words))[:20])
# ['a', 'about', 'above', 'after', 'again', 'against', 'ain', 'all', 'am',
#  'an', 'and', 'any', 'are', "aren't", 'as', 'at', 'be', 'because', 'been', 'before']

Applying Stopword Removal

from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize

def remove_stopwords(text, stop_words):
    """
    Tokenize text and remove stopwords.
    Returns a list of meaningful tokens.
    """
    tokens = word_tokenize(text.lower())
    # Keep token if it's not a stopword AND it's alphabetic
    filtered = [token for token in tokens
                if token not in stop_words and token.isalpha()]
    return filtered

stop_words = set(stopwords.words('english'))
sample = "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog near the river"
result = remove_stopwords(sample, stop_words)
print(result)
# ['quick', 'brown', 'fox', 'jumps', 'lazy', 'dog', 'near', 'river']
Analytics Vidhya Source Error #2 — Wrong column name: The Analytics Vidhya article uses the column name no_sw_msg throughout after the stopword removal step. This column name is never created in the code shown — the code creates a column called no_stopwords. Any code referencing df['no_sw_msg'] will raise a KeyError. Use df['no_stopwords'] instead.

Customizing the Stopword List

# Add domain-specific words to the stopword list
# Example: for a job-posting dataset, 'job', 'work', 'position' might be uninformative
custom_stops = stop_words.copy()
custom_stops.update(['job', 'work', 'position', 'company', 'role', 'opportunity'])

# Remove a word from stopwords (e.g., keep "not" for sentiment)
# "not" in the default list kills negation — risky for sentiment tasks
sentiment_stops = stop_words.copy()
sentiment_stops.discard('not')
sentiment_stops.discard('no')
sentiment_stops.discard('nor')
sentiment_stops.discard('never')
Domain-aware stopwords matter. For a dataset of technical job postings, removing the word "python" might be appropriate if it appears in 95% of posts. But in a general corpus, "python" is highly discriminative. Always inspect your top-N most frequent tokens after cleaning — if something that should be meaningful is at the top, consider adding it to your custom stopword list.

8.5 Stemming and Lemmatization

AN306 docx

After removing stopwords, you still have many forms of the same root word: "run," "running," "runs," "ran." These are different tokens but represent the same concept. Normalization collapses them to one form.

Stemming

Stemming is a crude rule-based chopping of suffixes. It doesn't consult a dictionary — it just applies suffix-stripping rules. Fast but can produce non-words ("running" → "run", "studies" → "studi").

from nltk.stem import PorterStemmer, SnowballStemmer

porter = PorterStemmer()
snowball = SnowballStemmer('english')

words = ['running', 'runs', 'ran', 'studies', 'studying', 'studied',
         'happiness', 'happy', 'happily', 'better', 'connection', 'connected']

print(f"{'Word':<15} {'Porter':<15} {'Snowball':<15}")
print('-' * 45)
for word in words:
    print(f"{word:<15} {porter.stem(word):<15} {snowball.stem(word):<15}")

# Word            Porter          Snowball
# running         run             run
# runs            run             run
# ran             ran             ran           ← note: doesn't unify ran and run
# studies         studi           studi         ← produces non-word
# studying        studi           studi
# happiness       happi           happi         ← produces non-word
# better          better          better        ← doesn't normalize to 'good'

Lemmatization

Lemmatization uses a vocabulary and morphological analysis to return the dictionary base form (the lemma). It requires knowing the part of speech. Slower but linguistically correct — always produces real words.

import nltk
nltk.download('wordnet')
nltk.download('averaged_perceptron_tagger')
nltk.download('averaged_perceptron_tagger_eng')

from nltk.stem import WordNetLemmatizer
from nltk.corpus import wordnet

lemmatizer = WordNetLemmatizer()

# IMPORTANT: lemmatize requires the POS tag, otherwise it defaults to noun
# and may not lemmatize verbs correctly

def get_wordnet_pos(tag):
    """Convert NLTK POS tag to WordNet POS constant."""
    if tag.startswith('J'):
        return wordnet.ADJ
    elif tag.startswith('V'):
        return wordnet.VERB
    elif tag.startswith('N'):
        return wordnet.NOUN
    elif tag.startswith('R'):
        return wordnet.ADV
    else:
        return wordnet.NOUN   # default to noun

def lemmatize_text(text):
    """
    Tokenize, POS-tag, then lemmatize with correct POS context.
    Returns a list of lemmas.
    """
    tokens = word_tokenize(text.lower())
    # Get POS tags for all tokens at once (more accurate than tagging individually)
    pos_tags = nltk.pos_tag(tokens)

    lemmas = []
    for token, tag in pos_tags:
        if token.isalpha():   # skip punctuation
            wn_pos = get_wordnet_pos(tag)
            lemmas.append(lemmatizer.lemmatize(token, pos=wn_pos))
    return lemmas

text = "The wolves were running faster than the studies suggested they would"
print(lemmatize_text(text))
# ['the', 'wolf', 'be', 'run', 'fast', 'than', 'the', 'study', 'suggest', 'they', 'would']
# Note: 'wolves' → 'wolf', 'studies' → 'study', 'running' → 'run'
Analytics Vidhya Source Error #3 — Indentation errors: The Analytics Vidhya article shows the stemming and lemmatization functions with their body code at the wrong indentation level — the function body lines appear at the same level as the def statement. Python requires the function body to be indented (4 spaces or 1 tab) relative to the def. As written in the article, running the code raises an IndentationError. All code in this course has correct indentation.

Stemming vs. Lemmatization — When to Use Which

FactorStemmingLemmatization
SpeedFast (rule-based, no lookup)Slower (dictionary + POS tagging)
OutputMay produce non-words ("studi")Always produces real words
AccuracyCrude; misses irregular forms ("ran"≠"run")Handles irregular forms correctly
POS needed?NoYes for best results
Use whenSpeed matters, interpretability less critical; large-scale IRInterpretability matters; NLP pipelines needing valid vocabulary
AN306 defaultOK for quick analysisPreferred for final pipelines
Stemming and lemmatization are NOT interchangeable. Stemming is a heuristic suffix-stripper; lemmatization is morphological analysis returning valid dictionary words. The key difference: "ran" → Porter stem is "ran" (unchanged), lemmatization is "run." Stemming can produce non-words; lemmatization never does.

8.6 Bag of Words and CountVectorizer

AN306

After cleaning, tokenizing, filtering, and normalizing, you have a list of tokens per document. The next step is converting these into numbers. The simplest approach is Bag of Words (BoW): represent each document as a vector of word counts, ignoring word order.

The Concept

Given a corpus of 3 documents:

D1: "data science is great"
D2: "science and data analytics"
D3: "great analytics skills needed"

The vocabulary (unique words) = {analytics, and, data, great, is, needed, science, skills}. Each document becomes a vector of counts:

Documentanalyticsanddatagreatisneededscienceskills
D100111010
D211100010
D310010101

This is the document-term matrix (DTM). Rows = documents, columns = vocabulary terms, cells = term frequency in that document.

CountVectorizer in scikit-learn

from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
import pandas as pd

corpus = [
    "data science is great",
    "science and data analytics",
    "great analytics skills needed"
]

# --- Basic CountVectorizer ---
cv = CountVectorizer()
dtm = cv.fit_transform(corpus)   # sparse matrix

# Convert to dense DataFrame for inspection
dtm_df = pd.DataFrame(dtm.toarray(), columns=cv.get_feature_names_out())
print(dtm_df)

# --- With options ---
cv2 = CountVectorizer(
    lowercase=True,          # lowercase (default True)
    stop_words='english',    # built-in English stopwords
    min_df=2,                # ignore terms appearing in fewer than 2 documents
    max_df=0.95,             # ignore terms appearing in more than 95% of docs
    max_features=5000,       # keep only top 5000 terms by frequency
    ngram_range=(1, 2)       # include both unigrams AND bigrams
)
dtm2 = cv2.fit_transform(corpus)
print("Vocabulary:", cv2.vocabulary_)   # {term: column_index}

n-Grams

A unigram is a single word. A bigram is a pair of consecutive words. ngram_range=(1,2) includes both. Bigrams capture phrases: "not good" is very different from "not" and "good" separately.

🔑 Why n-grams matter — the "not good" problem:
With unigrams only, these two reviews look almost identical:
  • Review A: "This product is not good." → {not:1, good:1, product:1, ...}
  • Review B: "This product is very good." → {very:1, good:1, product:1, ...}
Review A is negative. Review B is positive. But their unigram vectors are nearly identical — the word "good" appears once in both. A BoW sentiment classifier trained on unigrams will be confused.

With bigrams, Review A has the feature not_good and Review B has very_good — now the model can tell them apart. Negation ("not good", "never works", "can't recommend") is the most common case where bigrams beat unigrams. 
# Bigrams example
cv_bigram = CountVectorizer(ngram_range=(2, 2))
dtm_bigram = cv_bigram.fit_transform(["machine learning is powerful", "deep learning scales well"])
print(cv_bigram.get_feature_names_out())
# ['deep learning', 'is powerful', 'learning is', 'learning scales', 'scales well']
The sparsity problem: With a vocabulary of 10,000 words and 1,000 documents, the DTM is 1,000 × 10,000 = 10 million cells. Most documents use only a tiny fraction of the vocabulary, so the matrix is 95–99% zeros (sparse). scikit-learn's vectorizers return sparse matrices by default — do not call .toarray() on large corpora unless necessary (it loads the full dense matrix into RAM).

8.7 TF-IDF — Term Frequency–Inverse Document Frequency

AN306 AN300

Bag of Words counts how often a word appears. But a word that appears in every document (like "the" or in a news dataset, "said") is useless for distinguishing documents from each other — even if it has high counts. TF-IDF downweights common words and upweights rare-but-informative words.

TF — Term Frequency

Term Frequency TF(t, d) = count of term t in document d / total terms in document d

TF is just the raw count normalized by document length (so longer documents don't get artificially high scores). Note: scikit-learn's TfidfVectorizer uses raw count for TF by default (unnormalized), not the length-normalized version.

IDF — Inverse Document Frequency

Inverse Document Frequency (scikit-learn formula) IDF(t) = log( (1 + N) / (1 + df(t)) ) + 1
N = total documents; df(t) = number of documents containing term t
The +1s and final +1 are sklearn's smoothing to avoid division by zero

Words appearing in every document → IDF ≈ 1 (low weight). Words appearing in 1 document → IDF is high (high weight).

🔑 One-sentence intuition for IDF:
Imagine 100 job postings. The word "work" appears in all 100 — it tells you nothing about which posting is which (IDF ≈ low). The word "tensorflow" appears in only 3 — it strongly identifies those 3 specific postings (IDF = high).

IDF is literally "penalize common words, reward rare ones." A word that appears in every document is useless for distinguishing documents, so IDF squashes its weight toward 1. A word unique to 3 documents gets a high multiplier.

From your DS jobs project: In 672 Glassdoor postings, the word "data" appeared in nearly every title — IDF near 1. The term "databricks" appeared in ~12 postings — IDF much higher. TF-IDF makes "databricks" a stronger signal for those 12 docs than "data" is for any doc.

TF-IDF Score

TF-IDF Score TF-IDF(t, d) = TF(t, d) × IDF(t)

Then sklearn normalizes each document vector to unit L2 norm (by default), so documents of different lengths are comparable.

Worked Example

Three documents, term "python":

  • D1: "python data python" → TF = 2/3 = 0.667
  • D2: "python machine learning" → TF = 1/3 = 0.333
  • D3: "machine learning deep" → TF = 0

df("python") = 2 (appears in D1 and D2). N = 3.

IDF("python") = log((1+3)/(1+2)) + 1 = log(4/3) + 1 ≈ 0.288 + 1 = 1.288

TF-IDF D1: 0.667 × 1.288 ≈ 0.859 | D2: 0.333 × 1.288 ≈ 0.429 | D3: 0

"python" is weighted highest in D1, moderate in D2, zero in D3.

TfidfVectorizer in scikit-learn

from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer

corpus = [
    "python data science machine learning",
    "python programming data analysis statistics",
    "machine learning deep learning neural networks"
]

tfidf = TfidfVectorizer(
    lowercase=True,
    stop_words='english',
    min_df=1,
    max_features=1000,
    ngram_range=(1, 1),    # unigrams only
    sublinear_tf=True      # use 1 + log(TF) instead of raw TF — reduces impact of very frequent terms
)

tfidf_matrix = tfidf.fit_transform(corpus)   # shape: (3, vocab_size)

# Convert to DataFrame for inspection
vocab = tfidf.get_feature_names_out()
tfidf_df = pd.DataFrame(tfidf_matrix.toarray(), columns=vocab)
print(tfidf_df.round(3))

# Which terms are most important in document 0?
doc0_scores = tfidf_df.iloc[0].sort_values(ascending=False)
print("\\nTop terms in Document 0:")
print(doc0_scores[doc0_scores > 0])

# Top terms globally (mean TF-IDF across corpus)
mean_tfidf = tfidf_df.mean().sort_values(ascending=False)
print("\\nTop 10 terms by average TF-IDF:")
print(mean_tfidf.head(10))
TF-IDF is NOT a probability — TF-IDF values can exceed 1, especially with sublinear_tf=False and multiple occurrences of a term. After L2 normalization by sklearn, individual document vectors have unit norm, but individual cell values depend on the vocabulary size and term frequencies. Do not interpret TF-IDF scores as "the probability that this term defines this document."

8.8 Cosine Similarity — Measuring Document Similarity

AN306 AN300

Once documents are TF-IDF vectors, we can measure how similar two documents are. The standard metric is cosine similarity — it measures the cosine of the angle between two vectors, ignoring their magnitudes.

Why Cosine, Not Euclidean Distance?

Euclidean distance is affected by document length. A 1,000-word article and a 100-word tweet about the same topic will have very different vector magnitudes, making them appear far apart even if they discuss identical concepts. Cosine similarity normalizes for length — it cares about direction (what topics), not magnitude (how long).

Cosine Similarity cos_sim(A, B) = (A · B) / (||A|| × ||B||)
where A · B is the dot product and ||A|| is the L2 norm (magnitude) of vector A

Range: 0 to 1 for TF-IDF vectors (which are non-negative). Value interpretation:

  • 1.0 — identical direction (very similar documents)
  • 0.0 — perpendicular (no shared vocabulary at all)
  • Between 0 and 1 — partial similarity
🔑 Concrete cosine example — vectors in 3D space:
Imagine only 3 words in our vocabulary: [python, sql, finance]. Two job postings:

Doc A: "python sql python" → TF-IDF vector ≈ [0.8, 0.5, 0.0]
Doc B: "python sql" → TF-IDF vector ≈ [0.7, 0.6, 0.0]
Doc C: "finance analyst excel" → TF-IDF vector ≈ [0.0, 0.0, 1.0]

Cosine(A, B): Both point in the "python + sql" direction. Dot product = (0.8×0.7) + (0.5×0.6) + (0×0) = 0.56 + 0.30 = 0.86. Magnitudes ≈ 1 (L2 normalized). Result: 0.86 — very similar.
Cosine(A, C): A points toward python/sql; C points purely at finance. No overlap. Dot product = 0. Result: 0.0 — no similarity.

The key insight: cosine doesn't care that Doc A mentioned "python" twice and Doc B only once (magnitudes differ). It cares that they both point in the same direction. That's why it handles documents of different lengths correctly.

Computing Cosine Similarity in scikit-learn

from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import cosine_similarity
import numpy as np

# Using our tfidf_matrix from above (3 documents)
# Compute all pairwise similarities — returns N×N matrix
sim_matrix = cosine_similarity(tfidf_matrix)
print("Pairwise cosine similarity matrix:")
print(sim_matrix.round(3))
# array([[1.   , 0.52 , 0.082],
#        [0.52 , 1.   , 0.   ],
#        [0.082, 0.   , 1.   ]])
# → D0 and D1 are 52% similar, D0 and D2 barely similar, D1 and D2 unrelated

# Similarity of one query document to all others
query = tfidf.transform(["python data machine"])   # transform (not fit_transform!)
query_sim = cosine_similarity(query, tfidf_matrix)
print("\\nQuery similarities:", query_sim.round(3))
print("Most similar document:", np.argmax(query_sim))
Always use .transform() for new documents, never .fit_transform(). fit_transform would rebuild the vocabulary from the new document alone — the query would have a completely different vector space and comparisons would be meaningless. The vectorizer must be fit on the corpus; new documents are transformed into that existing vocabulary space.

Building a Simple Document Recommendation System

def find_similar_documents(query_text, corpus_texts, vectorizer, top_n=5):
    """
    Given a query and a corpus, return the top N most similar documents.

    Parameters
    ----------
    query_text   : str — the search query
    corpus_texts : list of str — the document corpus
    vectorizer   : fitted TfidfVectorizer
    top_n        : int — number of results to return
    """
    # Vectorize the full corpus and the query
    corpus_matrix = vectorizer.transform(corpus_texts)
    query_vec = vectorizer.transform([query_text])

    # Compute similarity to all corpus documents
    similarities = cosine_similarity(query_vec, corpus_matrix).flatten()

    # Rank by similarity (descending)
    ranked_indices = np.argsort(similarities)[::-1][:top_n]

    results = []
    for idx in ranked_indices:
        results.append({
            'doc_index': idx,
            'similarity': round(similarities[idx], 4),
            'text': corpus_texts[idx][:100]   # first 100 chars preview
        })

    return pd.DataFrame(results)

# Example usage
results = find_similar_documents(
    "machine learning python skills",
    corpus,
    tfidf
)
print(results.to_string())

8.9 Sentiment Analysis

AN306 docx

Sentiment analysis classifies text as positive, negative, or neutral. There are two main approaches: lexicon-based (look up words in a sentiment dictionary) and machine-learning-based (train a classifier on labeled data). We focus on lexicon-based, which requires no labeled training data.

VADER — Valence Aware Dictionary and Sentiment Reasoner

VADER is specifically designed for social media text — short, informal, with capitalization, punctuation, and emojis as sentiment signals. It handles:

  • ALL CAPS intensification ("GREAT" vs. "great")
  • Punctuation intensification ("great!!!" vs. "great")
  • Negation ("not good" → negative)
  • Degree modifiers ("very good," "barely good")
  • Emojis and common slang
pip install vaderSentiment
from vaderSentiment.vaderSentiment import SentimentIntensityAnalyzer

analyzer = SentimentIntensityAnalyzer()

texts = [
    "The product is amazing! Best purchase I've made.",
    "Absolutely terrible. Never buying again.",
    "It's okay. Nothing special.",
    "GREAT QUALITY!! Fast shipping!!!",
    "Not bad at all, actually pretty good.",
    "not good"   # negation
]

for text in texts:
    scores = analyzer.polarity_scores(text)
    print(f"\\nText: {text}")
    print(f"  neg={scores['neg']:.3f}  neu={scores['neu']:.3f}  pos={scores['pos']:.3f}  compound={scores['compound']:.4f}")

    # Classify using compound score
    if scores['compound'] >= 0.05:
        label = 'POSITIVE'
    elif scores['compound'] <= -0.05:
        label = 'NEGATIVE'
    else:
        label = 'NEUTRAL'
    print(f"  → {label}")

VADER Scores Explained

ScoreRangeMeaning
pos0 to 1Proportion of text that is positive sentiment
neg0 to 1Proportion of text that is negative sentiment
neu0 to 1Proportion of text that is neutral
compound−1 to +1Normalized, weighted composite score. The main classification metric.

pos + neg + neu ≈ 1.0 (they sum to 1 within floating point precision).

Applying VADER to a DataFrame

import pandas as pd

# Example review DataFrame
df = pd.DataFrame({
    'review_id': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5],
    'review_text': [
        "Love this product! Works perfectly.",
        "Waste of money. Broke after one day.",
        "Decent quality, but shipping was slow.",
        "AMAZING!!! Will definitely buy again!!!",
        "Not the best, not the worst."
    ]
})

# Apply VADER
def get_sentiment(text, analyzer):
    scores = analyzer.polarity_scores(str(text))
    return pd.Series({
        'vader_neg':      scores['neg'],
        'vader_neu':      scores['neu'],
        'vader_pos':      scores['pos'],
        'vader_compound': scores['compound'],
        'vader_label':    'POSITIVE' if scores['compound'] >= 0.05
                         else 'NEGATIVE' if scores['compound'] <= -0.05
                         else 'NEUTRAL'
    })

sentiment_df = df['review_text'].apply(lambda x: get_sentiment(x, analyzer))
df = pd.concat([df, sentiment_df], axis=1)
print(df[['review_text', 'vader_compound', 'vader_label']].to_string())

# Sentiment distribution
print("\\nSentiment distribution:")
print(df['vader_label'].value_counts())

TextBlob — Simpler Alternative

from textblob import TextBlob

text = "The movie was surprisingly good, though the ending felt rushed."
blob = TextBlob(text)
print(f"Polarity:    {blob.sentiment.polarity:.3f}")    # -1 (negative) to +1 (positive)
print(f"Subjectivity:{blob.sentiment.subjectivity:.3f}") # 0 (objective) to 1 (subjective)

# Polarity > 0 → positive; < 0 → negative; = 0 → neutral
# Subjectivity: 0 = fact-based ("The book has 300 pages"); 1 = opinion-based ("It's wonderful")
VADER's compound score ranges from −1 to +1. The classification thresholds are: compound ≥ 0.05 = POSITIVE; compound ≤ −0.05 = NEGATIVE; between −0.05 and 0.05 = NEUTRAL. These are VADER's recommended thresholds — not 0 and 1. If you use 0 as the threshold, you misclassify weakly negative text.

8.10 LDA Topic Modeling — Discovering Hidden Themes

AN306 AN300

Topic modeling is an unsupervised technique that discovers latent themes in a large document collection. You don't label the topics — the algorithm finds the themes by identifying word co-occurrence patterns. The dominant algorithm is Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA).

The LDA Generative Assumption

LDA treats each document as a mixture of topics, and each topic as a distribution over words:

  • Document = mixture of K topics (e.g., 70% Technology, 20% Business, 10% Sports)
  • Topic = distribution over all vocabulary words (Technology topic: "software" 5%, "data" 4%, "system" 3%...)

Each word in a document is generated by: (1) sample a topic from the document's topic distribution, (2) sample a word from that topic's word distribution. LDA infers the topics and distributions from observed word co-occurrences.

LDA with sklearn

from sklearn.decomposition import LatentDirichletAllocation
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
import numpy as np

# LDA needs COUNT data (not TF-IDF — the probabilistic model assumes raw counts)
cv = CountVectorizer(
    max_features=5000,
    min_df=5,          # ignore very rare terms
    max_df=0.90,       # ignore very common terms (appear in >90% of docs)
    stop_words='english'
)

dtm = cv.fit_transform(corpus_texts)   # corpus_texts = your list of documents

# Fit LDA
n_topics = 5    # you choose this — analogous to k in K-Means

lda = LatentDirichletAllocation(
    n_components=n_topics,
    learning_method='online',   # 'online' for large corpora, 'batch' for small
    random_state=42,
    max_iter=25,
    n_jobs=-1
)
lda.fit(dtm)

# --- Inspect topics: top N words per topic ---
def print_top_words(model, feature_names, n_top_words=10):
    for topic_idx, topic in enumerate(model.components_):
        # topic is a 1D array of shape (n_vocab,)
        # sort word indices by weight descending
        top_word_indices = topic.argsort()[::-1][:n_top_words]
        top_words = [feature_names[i] for i in top_word_indices]
        print(f"Topic {topic_idx}: {', '.join(top_words)}")

vocab = cv.get_feature_names_out()
print_top_words(lda, vocab, n_top_words=10)

Getting Document-Topic Distributions

# Get topic distribution for each document
# Shape: (n_documents, n_topics) — each row sums to 1
doc_topic_matrix = lda.transform(dtm)

print(f"doc_topic_matrix shape: {doc_topic_matrix.shape}")
print("\\nFirst 5 documents — topic distributions:")
print(doc_topic_matrix[:5].round(3))

# Dominant topic for each document
dominant_topics = doc_topic_matrix.argmax(axis=1)
print("\\nDominant topic assignments:")
print(dominant_topics[:20])

# Add to original DataFrame
df_corpus['dominant_topic'] = dominant_topics
df_corpus['topic_confidence'] = doc_topic_matrix.max(axis=1)

# How many documents per topic?
print("\\nDocuments per topic:")
print(df_corpus['dominant_topic'].value_counts().sort_index())

Choosing the Number of Topics K

LDA requires you to specify K (number of topics) upfront — like K-Means. The main metric for choosing K is perplexity (lower is better) or coherence score (higher is better). Coherence measures whether the top words of a topic actually make semantic sense together.

# Compute log-likelihood (proxy for perplexity) for different K values
# Note: sklearn's score() returns log-likelihood (higher is better for model fit)
from sklearn.decomposition import LatentDirichletAllocation
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

k_values = [2, 3, 5, 7, 10, 15, 20]
log_likelihoods = []
perplexities = []

for k in k_values:
    lda_k = LatentDirichletAllocation(
        n_components=k, learning_method='online',
        random_state=42, max_iter=20
    )
    lda_k.fit(dtm)
    log_likelihoods.append(lda_k.score(dtm))
    perplexities.append(lda_k.perplexity(dtm))
    print(f"K={k:2d}  log-likelihood={lda_k.score(dtm):.1f}  perplexity={lda_k.perplexity(dtm):.2f}")

# Plot perplexity vs K
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4))
ax.plot(k_values, perplexities, 'o-', color='#7c8ff5', linewidth=2, markersize=7)
ax.set_xlabel('Number of Topics (K)')
ax.set_ylabel('Perplexity (lower is better)')
ax.set_title('LDA Perplexity vs Number of Topics')
ax.set_facecolor('#161922')
fig.patch.set_facecolor('#0f1117')
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('lda_perplexity.png', dpi=150)
plt.show()

Visualizing LDA with pyLDAvis

pip install pyLDAvis
import pyLDAvis
import pyLDAvis.lda_model   # for sklearn LDA

# Prepare visualization
vis = pyLDAvis.lda_model.prepare(lda, dtm, cv)

# Display in Jupyter/Colab
pyLDAvis.display(vis)

# Or save to HTML file
pyLDAvis.save_html(vis, 'lda_visualization.html')

The pyLDAvis interface shows two plots:

  • Left — topic circles in 2D (using inter-topic distance). Circles sized by prevalence. Well-separated circles = distinct topics. Overlapping circles = topics that share vocabulary.
  • Right — bar chart of top words for the selected topic. Bars show term frequency in the topic (red) vs. in the corpus (blue). The λ slider adjusts between topic-specific terms (λ → 0) and globally frequent terms (λ → 1). λ = 0.6 is recommended for interpretability.
Labeling topics — LDA doesn't name topics for you. After inspecting top words, you assign a human-readable label: "Topic 2: machine learning, neural, model, train, accuracy" → you'd call this the Machine Learning topic. Topic labeling is a manual, interpretive step.

8.11 The Full Text Preprocessing Pipeline

AN306

Putting all the steps together into a reusable pipeline function. This is what you'll adapt for class projects and real datasets.

import re
import string
import nltk
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

nltk.download('punkt', quiet=True)
nltk.download('punkt_tab', quiet=True)
nltk.download('stopwords', quiet=True)
nltk.download('wordnet', quiet=True)
nltk.download('averaged_perceptron_tagger_eng', quiet=True)

from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize
from nltk.corpus import stopwords, wordnet
from nltk.stem import WordNetLemmatizer, PorterStemmer

# ==============================================================
# STEP 1: Text Cleaning
# ==============================================================
def clean_text(text):
    """Remove HTML, URLs, punctuation; lowercase."""
    if pd.isna(text):
        return ''
    text = str(text).lower()
    text = re.sub(r'<[^>]+>', ' ', text)         # HTML tags
    text = re.sub(r'&\\w+;', ' ', text)            # HTML entities
    text = re.sub(r'http\\S+|www\\.\\S+', ' ', text) # URLs
    text = re.sub(r'\\S+@\\S+', ' ', text)           # emails
    text = text.translate(str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation))
    text = re.sub(r'\\s+', ' ', text).strip()
    return text

# ==============================================================
# STEP 2: Tokenize and Remove Stopwords
# ==============================================================
STOP_WORDS = set(stopwords.words('english'))

def tokenize_and_filter(text, custom_stops=None):
    """Tokenize, remove stopwords and non-alpha tokens."""
    stops = STOP_WORDS.copy()
    if custom_stops:
        stops.update(custom_stops)
    tokens = word_tokenize(text)
    return [t for t in tokens if t.isalpha() and t not in stops and len(t) > 2]

# ==============================================================
# STEP 3: Lemmatize
# ==============================================================
lemmatizer = WordNetLemmatizer()

def _get_wn_pos(tag):
    if tag.startswith('J'):   return wordnet.ADJ
    elif tag.startswith('V'): return wordnet.VERB
    elif tag.startswith('N'): return wordnet.NOUN
    elif tag.startswith('R'): return wordnet.ADV
    return wordnet.NOUN

def lemmatize_tokens(tokens):
    """POS-tag and lemmatize a list of tokens."""
    pos_tags = nltk.pos_tag(tokens)
    return [lemmatizer.lemmatize(tok, pos=_get_wn_pos(tag))
            for tok, tag in pos_tags]

# ==============================================================
# FULL PIPELINE
# ==============================================================
def preprocess_text(text, custom_stops=None, normalize='lemma'):
    """
    Full NLP preprocessing pipeline.

    Parameters
    ----------
    text          : str — raw input text
    custom_stops  : set — additional stopwords to remove
    normalize     : 'lemma' or 'stem' or None

    Returns
    -------
    tokens        : list of str — preprocessed tokens
    joined        : str — tokens joined by space (for vectorizers)
    """
    # 1. Clean
    cleaned = clean_text(text)
    # 2. Tokenize and filter stopwords
    tokens = tokenize_and_filter(cleaned, custom_stops)
    # 3. Normalize
    if normalize == 'lemma':
        tokens = lemmatize_tokens(tokens)
    elif normalize == 'stem':
        stemmer = PorterStemmer()
        tokens = [stemmer.stem(t) for t in tokens]
    # 4. Join back for vectorizers
    joined = ' '.join(tokens)
    return tokens, joined

# ==============================================================
# APPLY TO A DATAFRAME
# ==============================================================
def preprocess_corpus(df, text_col, custom_stops=None, normalize='lemma'):
    """
    Apply full preprocessing to a text column in a DataFrame.
    Adds 'clean_text', 'tokens', 'no_stopwords' columns.

    Returns the modified DataFrame.
    """
    df = df.copy()

    # Stage 1: basic cleaning
    df['clean_text'] = df[text_col].apply(clean_text)

    # Stage 2: full pipeline
    results = df['clean_text'].apply(
        lambda x: preprocess_text(x, custom_stops, normalize)
    )
    df['tokens']       = results.apply(lambda x: x[0])
    df['no_stopwords'] = results.apply(lambda x: x[1])  # ← correct column name

    # Stage 3: basic stats
    df['token_count'] = df['tokens'].apply(len)

    return df

# Example usage
sample_data = pd.DataFrame({
    'id': [1, 2, 3],
    'text': [
        "Data scientists are analyzing trends in machine learning models.",
        "Python programming skills are required for most data analysis jobs.",
        "Statistical methods and visualization tools help communicate insights."
    ]
})

processed = preprocess_corpus(sample_data, text_col='text')
print(processed[['text', 'no_stopwords', 'token_count']].to_string())
Column naming convention — from the source error: The correct column after stopword removal is no_stopwords. This is what the pipeline above creates. The Analytics Vidhya article inconsistently refers to it as no_sw_msg in later code cells — a name that doesn't exist in their pipeline. All subsequent code in this course uses df['no_stopwords'].

8.12 Exam Traps and Source Errors — Full NLP Register

AN300
Trap 1 — fit vs. transform on new data. ALWAYS call .fit_transform() on training/corpus data. ALWAYS call .transform() (not fit_transform) on test or query documents. Calling fit_transform on new data builds a new vocabulary — the resulting vectors live in a different space and cannot be compared to the corpus vectors. This is data leakage in the NLP context.
Trap 2 — LDA requires count data, not TF-IDF. LDA is a probabilistic model of word counts. Using a TF-IDF matrix with LDA is technically possible but conceptually wrong — LDA's generative model assumes integer or near-integer counts. Use CountVectorizer (not TfidfVectorizer) as input to LDA. Use TfidfVectorizer for cosine similarity and document classification.
Trap 3 — TF-IDF is not a probability. TF-IDF values can exceed 1, especially without sublinear_tf. After sklearn's L2 normalization, the full document vector has unit norm, but individual cell values are NOT probabilities and do NOT sum to 1. Document-topic distributions from LDA DO sum to 1 (they are probabilities). Don't confuse the two.
Trap 4 — Stemming does not unify all word forms. Porter stemmer does NOT stem across irregular inflections. "ran" stems to "ran" (not "run"). "went" stems to "went" (not "go"). If you need true morphological reduction, use lemmatization with correct POS tags.
Trap 5 — Removing "not" from stopwords matters for sentiment. NLTK's default stopword list includes "not," "no," "nor," "never." If you apply default stopwords before sentiment analysis, the sentence "not good" becomes "good" and VADER/TextBlob will score it as positive. For sentiment tasks, always use a custom stopword list that keeps negation words.
Trap 6 — word_tokenize("can't") → ["ca", "n't"]. NLTK splits contractions: "can't" → ["ca", "n't"], "won't" → ["wo", "n't"], "I'm" → ["I", "'m"]. This is intended behavior. Depending on your task, you may want to normalize contractions before tokenizing: "can't" → "cannot", "won't" → "will not". The module contractions (pip install contractions) handles this automatically.

Documented Source Errors — Analytics Vidhya NLP Article

Error 1 — Missing raw string prefix:
Article code: tokens = re.split('W+', text)
Correct code: tokens = re.split(r'\\W+', text)
Without the r prefix, Python treats \\W as a backslash escape rather than a regex meta-character. The pattern 'W+' matches the literal string "W+" — entirely wrong behavior. Always use raw strings for regex: r'\\W+'.
Error 2 — Wrong column name after stopword removal:
Article creates column: df['no_stopwords'] = ...
Then later references: df['no_sw_msg'] (raises KeyError)
Correct reference: df['no_stopwords']
The column is never renamed in the article's code. Use no_stopwords throughout.
Error 3 — Indentation errors in function bodies:
The stemming and lemmatization functions in the article have their body code at the same indentation level as the def statement. Python requires function bodies to be indented (conventionally 4 spaces). The article's code will raise IndentationError: expected an indented block. All functions in this course use correct indentation.

Cell 3 — Full Text Preprocessing Pipeline

# --- Preprocessing functions (paste from Module 8.11) ---
def clean_text(text):
    if pd.isna(text): return ''
    text = str(text).lower()
    text = re.sub(r'<[^>]+>', ' ', text)
    text = re.sub(r'http\\S+|www\\.\\S+', ' ', text)
    text = text.translate(str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation))
    text = re.sub(r'\\s+', ' ', text).strip()
    return text

def _get_wn_pos(tag):
    if tag.startswith('J'):   return wordnet.ADJ
    elif tag.startswith('V'): return wordnet.VERB
    elif tag.startswith('N'): return wordnet.NOUN
    elif tag.startswith('R'): return wordnet.ADV
    return wordnet.NOUN

def preprocess(text, custom_stops=None):
    stops = STOP_WORDS.copy()
    if custom_stops: stops.update(custom_stops)
    cleaned = clean_text(text)
    tokens  = word_tokenize(cleaned)
    tokens  = [t for t in tokens if t.isalpha() and t not in stops and len(t) > 2]
    pos_tags = nltk.pos_tag(tokens)
    lemmas  = [lemmatizer.lemmatize(t, pos=_get_wn_pos(p)) for t, p in pos_tags]
    return lemmas, ' '.join(lemmas)

# Apply to descriptions
# Custom stops: words universal to job ads but not discriminative
job_stops = {'experience', 'required', 'skill', 'ability', 'must', 'will', 'work',
             'team', 'strong', 'excellent', 'year', 'role', 'position', 'seeking'}

results = df['description'].apply(lambda x: preprocess(x, job_stops))
df['tokens']       = results.apply(lambda x: x[0])
df['no_stopwords'] = results.apply(lambda x: x[1])
df['token_count']  = df['tokens'].apply(len)

print("Preprocessing complete.")
print(df[['title', 'no_stopwords', 'token_count']].to_string())

# Top 20 most common tokens across all job postings
from collections import Counter
all_tokens = [t for tokens in df['tokens'] for t in tokens]
top20_tokens = Counter(all_tokens).most_common(20)
print("\\nTop 20 tokens across all postings:")
for tok, cnt in top20_tokens:
    print(f"  {tok:<20} {cnt}")

Cell 4 — TF-IDF Vectorization and Most Important Terms per Role

# --- TF-IDF ---
tfidf = TfidfVectorizer(
    max_features=200,
    min_df=1,
    ngram_range=(1, 2),    # unigrams and bigrams
    sublinear_tf=True
)

tfidf_matrix = tfidf.fit_transform(df['no_stopwords'])
vocab = tfidf.get_feature_names_out()
tfidf_df = pd.DataFrame(tfidf_matrix.toarray(), columns=vocab, index=df['title'])

print(f"TF-IDF matrix: {tfidf_df.shape}")

# Most important terms per job title
print("\\n=== Top 5 TF-IDF terms per role ===")
for title in df['title']:
    top_terms = tfidf_df.loc[title].sort_values(ascending=False).head(5)
    print(f"\\n{title}:")
    for term, score in top_terms.items():
        if score > 0:
            print(f"  {term:<30} {score:.4f}")

# --- Pairwise cosine similarity heatmap ---
import seaborn as sns

sim_matrix = cosine_similarity(tfidf_matrix)
sim_df = pd.DataFrame(sim_matrix, index=df['title'], columns=df['title'])

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 8))
sns.heatmap(sim_df, annot=True, fmt='.2f', cmap='Blues',
            xticklabels=True, yticklabels=True, ax=ax,
            linewidths=0.5, linecolor='#2d3348')
ax.set_title('Job Posting Cosine Similarity (TF-IDF)', fontsize=13)
ax.tick_params(axis='x', rotation=45)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('job_similarity_heatmap.png', dpi=150)
plt.show()

# Most similar pair
np.fill_diagonal(sim_matrix, 0)   # zero diagonal to find off-diagonal max
flat_idx = np.argmax(sim_matrix)
row, col = np.unravel_index(flat_idx, sim_matrix.shape)
print(f"\\nMost similar pair: '{df['title'].iloc[row]}' and '{df['title'].iloc[col]}'")
print(f"Cosine similarity: {sim_matrix[row, col]:.4f}")

Cell 5 — Sentiment Analysis on Job Descriptions

# --- VADER sentiment on raw (not cleaned) descriptions ---
# Use original text — VADER needs capitalization and punctuation signals

def score_sentiment(text):
    scores = vader.polarity_scores(str(text))
    label = ('POSITIVE' if scores['compound'] >= 0.05
             else 'NEGATIVE' if scores['compound'] <= -0.05
             else 'NEUTRAL')
    return pd.Series({
        'vader_pos':      scores['pos'],
        'vader_neg':      scores['neg'],
        'vader_compound': scores['compound'],
        'vader_label':    label
    })

sentiment = df['description'].apply(score_sentiment)
df = pd.concat([df, sentiment], axis=1)

print("=== Sentiment Scores by Job Title ===")
print(df[['title', 'vader_compound', 'vader_label']].to_string())

# Note: job descriptions tend to be neutral-to-positive
# (they're aspirational, not emotional)
print("\\n\\nSentiment distribution:")
print(df['vader_label'].value_counts())

# Most and least positively worded postings
print(f"\\nMost positive: {df.loc[df['vader_compound'].idxmax(), 'title']}")
print(f"              compound={df['vader_compound'].max():.4f}")
print(f"\\nLeast positive: {df.loc[df['vader_compound'].idxmin(), 'title']}")
print(f"               compound={df['vader_compound'].min():.4f}")

Cell 6 — LDA Topic Modeling

# --- LDA: discover latent themes in job descriptions ---
# Use CountVectorizer (count data, not TF-IDF)
cv = CountVectorizer(
    max_features=150,
    min_df=1,
    stop_words='english'
)
dtm = cv.fit_transform(df['no_stopwords'])
cv_vocab = cv.get_feature_names_out()

# With only 8 documents, 3 topics is reasonable
n_topics = 3

lda = LatentDirichletAllocation(
    n_components=n_topics,
    learning_method='batch',  # 'batch' better for small datasets
    random_state=42,
    max_iter=50
)
lda.fit(dtm)

# --- Print top words per topic ---
print("=== LDA Topics ===")
for topic_idx, topic_weights in enumerate(lda.components_):
    top_indices = topic_weights.argsort()[::-1][:8]
    top_words = [cv_vocab[i] for i in top_indices]
    print(f"Topic {topic_idx}: {', '.join(top_words)}")

# --- Document-topic distribution ---
doc_topics = lda.transform(dtm)
df['dominant_topic'] = doc_topics.argmax(axis=1)
df['topic_confidence'] = doc_topics.max(axis=1).round(3)

print("\\n=== Document-Topic Assignments ===")
print(df[['title', 'dominant_topic', 'topic_confidence']].to_string())

# --- Visualize topic distributions as stacked bar ---
topic_df = pd.DataFrame(doc_topics.round(3),
                         columns=[f'Topic {i}' for i in range(n_topics)],
                         index=df['title'])
topic_colors = ['#7c8ff5', '#56d9b0', '#f59e45']

ax = topic_df.plot(
    kind='barh', stacked=True, figsize=(10, 6),
    color=topic_colors, alpha=0.85
)
ax.set_xlabel('Topic Proportion')
ax.set_title('Document-Topic Distributions (LDA)')
ax.set_facecolor('#161922')
ax.figure.patch.set_facecolor('#0f1117')
ax.tick_params(colors='#94a3b8')
ax.legend(loc='lower right', facecolor='#161922', labelcolor='#e2e8f0')
plt.tight_layout()
plt.savefig('lda_topics.png', dpi=150)
plt.show()

print("\\nInterpretation: assign human labels to each topic based on its top words.")
print("Example: Topic 0 (python, machine, learning) → 'ML Engineering'")
print("         Topic 1 (sql, data, analyst) → 'Data Analysis'")
print("         Topic 2 (nlp, language, model) → 'AI/NLP Research'")

  • ✓ Function bodies correctly indented (4 spaces relative to def)

NLP Deep Dive

Entities, embeddings, the rules→transformers evolution, predictive vs. generative AI, and the hard problems NLP still hasn't fully solved.

This supplement picks up from Part 8. Part 8 covered the core pipeline (clean → tokenize → lemmatize → vectorize → model), Bag of Words, TF-IDF, VADER sentiment, and LDA topic modeling. NLP Evolution: From Hand-Written Rules to Foundation Models

Source: TechTarget + IBM This is the conceptual backbone — understanding the evolution helps you understand WHY modern tools work, not just HOW to call them.

NLP has gone through three major paradigm shifts since the 1950s, each one fundamentally changing how machines understand language. Knowing this history is essential for understanding which tool to reach for and why.

Era 1 — 1950s–1980s: Rules-Based NLP

The first wave of NLP was entirely hand-crafted. Linguists wrote if-then rules describing language patterns. If a sentence contained "not + adjective," flip the sentiment. If a word matched a dictionary entry, it was categorized.

  • What worked: Controlled vocabularies, simple spell-checkers, grammar correction in closed domains
  • What failed: Human language is inconsistent. Rules couldn't scale. "Bank" means a financial institution or the side of a river — the same word, two entirely different rules needed
  • Key limitation: Every new language, domain, or exception required a programmer to write more rules by hand. Zero generalization
Historical marker: The Georgetown-IBM experiment in 1954 automatically translated 60 Russian sentences into English using 250 vocabulary items and 6 grammar rules. Researchers predicted machine translation would be fully solved in 3–5 years. It took 60+ more years.
Era 2 — 1990s–2010s: Statistical NLP

Instead of writing rules, researchers started training models on data. The key insight: patterns in large corpora can teach machines about language better than humans can explicitly describe those patterns.

  • Markov models: Predict the next word based on the previous N words. The core behind early spell-checkers and T9 predictive text on feature phones. "I want to…" — the model assigns probabilities to what comes next based on how often those sequences appeared in training text
  • Naive Bayes classifiers: Spam filters. Given the words in this email, what's the probability it's spam? Train on labeled examples
  • Regression-based models: Linear and logistic regression on text features. This is where your AN306 regression skills directly apply to NLP
  • Hidden Markov Models (HMMs): Used for POS tagging and speech recognition
Key insight that unlocked statistical NLP: You don't need to understand language. You just need to have seen enough of it. A system that has read a billion sentences "knows" that "the cat sat" is more probable than "the cat barked" without understanding anything about cats or grammar.
Era 3 — 2010s: Deep Learning NLP (RNNs / LSTMs)

Neural networks changed NLP dramatically. Instead of hand-engineering features (word count, TF-IDF scores), models learned their own representations of language automatically.

  • Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs): Process text sequentially — each token's representation is influenced by everything that came before it. Good for modeling sequence, but short memory — context from many steps back fades
  • Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM): Solved the fading problem with "gates" that selectively remember or forget information. LSTMs powered machine translation systems (Google Translate used them before transformers)
  • Encoder-decoder seq2seq architecture: Two LSTMs — one encodes the source sentence into a fixed-length vector ("meaning"), the other decodes it into the target language. The key architecture behind machine translation
  • Word embeddings (Word2Vec, GloVe): Represent words as dense vectors where semantically similar words cluster together in high-dimensional space. Covered fully in Module 8b.4
# Seq2seq conceptual overview (not full implementation)
# Encoder: "Je veux du café" → [0.3, -0.7, 0.9, ...] (fixed vector = "meaning")
# Decoder: [0.3, -0.7, 0.9, ...] → "I want coffee"

# LSTM cell structure (conceptual):
# forget_gate = σ(Wf · [h_prev, x] + bf)   # what to drop from memory
# input_gate  = σ(Wi · [h_prev, x] + bi)   # what new info to add
# cell_state  = forget_gate * prev_cell + input_gate * tanh(Wc · [h_prev, x] + bc)
# output      = output_gate * tanh(cell_state)
Era 4 — 2017–Present: Transformers & Self-Attention

The transformer architecture (from the Google paper "Attention Is All You Need", 2017) replaced LSTMs almost entirely. The core mechanism: self-attention.

Self-Attention: The Key Mechanism

Instead of processing tokens sequentially (which limits parallelization and hurts long-range dependencies), transformers process all tokens simultaneously and learn which tokens are most relevant to each other.

# Self-attention — conceptual example
sentence = "The animal didn't cross the street because it was too tired"

# For the token "it":
# Self-attention computes a weighted relevance score to EVERY other token
# "it" should focus heavily on "animal" (not "street") to resolve the reference
# The attention weight for "animal" becomes high; for "street" it becomes low

# The model learns these weights FROM DATA — not from hand-written rules
# This is what "attention" means: which parts of the input to pay attention to
  • BERT (2018, Google): Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers. Trained on two tasks: predict masked words, predict if sentence B follows sentence A. Result: a general-purpose language encoder. Fine-tune BERT for sentiment analysis, NER, QA with far fewer labeled examples than training from scratch
  • GPT family (OpenAI): Autoregressive — trained to predict the next token. Much better at generation. GPT-1 (2018), GPT-2 (2019, too dangerous to release, then released), GPT-3 (175B parameters, 2020), GPT-4 (2023), GPT-5 (2025)
  • Open models: Llama (Meta), Mistral, Claude (Anthropic) — same transformer architecture
Attention Formula
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(QKT / √dk) · V

Q = queries (what am I looking for?)
K = keys (what do I contain?)
V = values (what do I actually return?)
dk = dimension of key vectors (scaling factor to prevent gradient vanishing)
Era 5 — 2022–Present: Foundation Models / LLMs

Models trained at scale (100B+ parameters, trillions of tokens) exhibit emergent capabilities — behaviors not present in smaller models that just appear at scale. Few-shot and zero-shot learning, reasoning, code generation, complex instruction following.

  • Key milestone: ChatGPT launched November 30, 2022 — 1 million users in 5 days, 100 million in 2 months. Fastest-adopted product in history
  • Self-supervised learning (SSL): LLMs train without human-labeled data at the pre-training stage. The labels ARE the data: "predict the next token" on the entire internet is self-supervised. This is what makes scale possible — no bottleneck on labeling
  • Fine-tuning: Start from a pre-trained foundation model, then train on your specific task with far fewer examples. BERT for sentiment: 10K labeled reviews → production model. Without pre-training, you'd need millions

NLP Timeline Cheat Sheet

YearEventSignificance
1950Turing proposes the Turing TestFirst formal definition of machine intelligence
1954Georgetown-IBM experimentFirst machine translation; wildly overoptimistic forecast
1990sStatistical NLP emergesData + probability replaces hand-written rules
2013Word2Vec (Google)Semantic word vectors; "king − man + woman ≈ queen"
2014GloVe (Stanford)Global co-occurrence matrix for embeddings
2017"Attention Is All You Need"Transformer architecture — changed everything
2018BERT (Google)First bidirectional pre-trained transformer
2019GPT-2 (OpenAI)Autoregressive generation at quality that surprised researchers
2020GPT-3 (175B params)Few-shot learning, emergent capabilities
Nov 2022ChatGPT launchFastest consumer product adoption in history
Aug 2025GPT-5Current frontier model (as of this course)
⚠ Exam / Interview Trap
Q: "What's the difference between BERT and GPT?"
A: BERT is an encoder — it reads text bidirectionally to build rich representations, best for classification and understanding tasks. GPT is a decoder — it generates text left-to-right, best for generation. BERT reads the whole sentence at once; GPT only uses what came before. Both are transformers; the architecture difference is encoder-only vs. decoder-only.

Part-of-Speech Tagging — Grammatical Role Analysis

Source: Built In + TechTarget

Part-of-speech (POS) tagging assigns a grammatical role to each token in a sentence: noun, verb, adjective, adverb, pronoun, preposition, conjunction, determiner, punctuation. This sounds simple but it's the foundation for almost every advanced NLP task.

Why POS Tagging Is Harder Than It Looks

The same word can be multiple parts of speech depending on context. POS tagging requires modeling context, not just looking words up in a dictionary.

import spacy
nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")

doc = nlp("I need to make a move before they make their move")
for token in doc:
    print(f"{token.text:<15} {token.pos_:<8} {token.tag_:<6} {token.dep_}")

# Output:
# I               PRON     PRP    nsubj
# need            VERB     VBP    ROOT
# to              PART     TO     aux
# make            VERB     VB     xcomp     ← "make" = VERB
# a               DET      DT     det
# move            NOUN     NN     dobj      ← "move" = NOUN here
# before          SCONJ    IN     mark
# they            PRON     PRP    nsubj
# make            VERB     VBP    advcl     ← "make" = VERB again
# their           PRON     PRP$   poss
# move            NOUN     NN     dobj      ← "move" = NOUN again
The word "make" appears twice, both times as a verb — but notice the tags differ: VB (base form verb, used with "to make") and VBP (non-3rd-person-singular present). spaCy uses Penn Treebank tags (accessed via token.tag_) for fine-grained tagging and Universal POS tags (via token.pos_) for coarser, language-agnostic tagging.

spaCy's Full POS Tag Set

Universal POS Tags (token.pos_)

  • NOUN — noun (pipeline, gas, pressure)
  • VERB — verb (monitor, detect, flow)
  • ADJ — adjective (critical, high-pressure)
  • ADV — adverb (rapidly, unexpectedly)
  • PRON — pronoun (he, she, it, they)
  • DET — determiner (a, an, the)
  • ADP — adposition / preposition (in, on, at)
  • CONJ — conjunction (and, but, or)
  • NUM — numeral (100, three)
  • PUNCT — punctuation (., ,, :)
  • PROPN — proper noun (Southern Star, Texas)

What POS Tagging Unlocks

  • Lemmatization: Correctly lemmatize "running" as "run" (verb) but "running" as "running" (noun, as in "a running") — same surface form, different base depending on POS
  • NER: Named entities are almost always noun phrases — POS narrows the candidates dramatically
  • Dependency parsing: Relationships between words are based on grammatical roles
  • Information extraction: "What verbs follow 'pipeline'?" — extract actions related to pipelines in regulatory docs
  • Chunking: Group consecutive tokens with compatible POS tags into phrases (noun chunks, verb phrases)
import spacy
nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")

# Noun chunk extraction — useful for pipeline operations
text = "The natural gas pressure in the main transmission pipeline exceeded safe operating limits"
doc = nlp(text)

print("Noun chunks:")
for chunk in doc.noun_chunks:
    print(f"  '{chunk.text}' — root: '{chunk.root.text}' (dep: {chunk.root.dep_})")

# Output:
# 'The natural gas pressure' — root: 'pressure' (dep: nsubj)
# 'the main transmission pipeline' — root: 'pipeline' (dep: pobj)
# 'safe operating limits' — root: 'limits' (dep: pobj)

print("\\nAll verbs:")
for token in doc:
    if token.pos_ == "VERB":
        print(f"  '{token.text}' (lemma: '{token.lemma_}')")
⚠ Interview Question
"Why do you do lemmatization AFTER POS tagging?"
Because the correct lemma depends on the part of speech. "saw" as a VERB lemmatizes to "see." "saw" as a NOUN lemmatizes to "saw" (the tool). Without POS, you can't know which lemma to return.

Named Entity Recognition (NER) — Extracting Key Information

Source: Built In

Named Entity Recognition is the NLP task of identifying and classifying named entities in text — proper nouns, dates, money, locations, organizations, percentages, and more — into predefined categories. It's one of the most commercially valuable NLP capabilities because it converts unstructured text into structured data.

What NER Looks Like

Given a sentence, NER annotates spans of text with their entity type:

Martin ⟨PERSON⟩ bought 300 shares of SAP ⟨ORG⟩ in 2016 ⟨DATE⟩

Southern Star ⟨ORG⟩ operates over 6,000 miles ⟨QUANTITY⟩ of pipeline in the Central United States ⟨GPE⟩

The Q3 2024 ⟨DATE⟩ maintenance budget was $2.4M ⟨MONEY⟩, up 12% ⟨PERCENT⟩ YoY

NER in spaCy

import spacy
nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")

text = """Southern Star Central Corp filed its Q3 2024 operations report with the
FERC on November 15, 2024. The Midcontinent pipeline system transported
3.2 billion cubic feet of natural gas at an average rate of $0.42 per MMBtu."""

doc = nlp(text)

print("Named Entities:")
for ent in doc.ents:
    print(f"  {ent.text:<40} {ent.label_:<12} {spacy.explain(ent.label_)}")

# Output:
# Southern Star Central Corp              ORG          Companies, agencies, institutions
# Q3 2024                                 DATE         Absolute or relative dates
# FERC                                    ORG          Companies, agencies, institutions
# November 15, 2024                       DATE         Absolute or relative dates
# Midcontinent                            GPE          Countries, cities, states
# 3.2 billion cubic feet                  QUANTITY     Measurements (not money)
# $0.42                                   MONEY        Monetary values
# MMBtu                                   ORG          (misclassified — sm model limitation)

# Visualize in notebook:
from spacy import displacy
displacy.render(doc, style="ent", jupyter=True)

spaCy's Built-In Entity Types

People & Organizations

  • PERSON — real or fictional people
  • ORG — companies, agencies, institutions
  • NORP — nationalities, religious or political groups

Locations

  • GPE — countries, cities, states
  • LOC — non-GPE locations (mountains, rivers)
  • FAC — buildings, airports, highways

Numeric / Temporal

  • DATE — absolute or relative dates
  • TIME — times smaller than a day
  • MONEY — monetary values
  • QUANTITY — measurements
  • PERCENT — percentages
  • CARDINAL — numerals not covered above

Custom NER: Training Your Own Entity Types

spaCy's pre-trained models work for general entities. For specialized domains (say, pipeline component types: VALVE, COMPRESSOR, METER), you train a custom NER model.

import spacy
from spacy.training import Example

# Training data format: (text, {"entities": [(start, end, label), ...]})
TRAIN_DATA = [
    ("The gate valve on Station 14 showed pressure loss",
     {"entities": [(4, 14, "EQUIPMENT"), (19, 29, "LOCATION")]}),
    ("Compressor Unit 3 at Elkhart failed during peak demand",
     {"entities": [(0, 15, "EQUIPMENT"), (19, 26, "LOCATION")]}),
    ("The mainline check valve near Chanute was replaced",
     {"entities": [(4, 22, "EQUIPMENT"), (28, 35, "LOCATION")]}),
]

# Start from blank English model
nlp = spacy.blank("en")
ner = nlp.add_pipe("ner")
ner.add_label("EQUIPMENT")
ner.add_label("LOCATION")

# Training loop
optimizer = nlp.begin_training()
for epoch in range(30):
    losses = {}
    for text, annotations in TRAIN_DATA:
        example = Example.from_dict(nlp.make_doc(text), annotations)
        nlp.update([example], sgd=optimizer, losses=losses)

# Production pipeline typically needs 500–10,000 training examples
# In practice: use Prodigy (explosion.ai's annotation tool) or Label Studio

NER as Information Extraction — The Business Case

NER converts unstructured text into structured data that can be queried, aggregated, and analyzed. Think about what this unlocks:

Regulatory Document Processing

Pipeline companies receive thousands of pages of regulatory filings, incident reports, and inspection documents. NER can automatically extract: locations mentioned, equipment types cited, dates of incidents, regulators involved, monetary penalties. What took analysts hours becomes a database query.

Contract / Agreement Analysis

Extract parties (ORG), effective dates (DATE), dollar amounts (MONEY), and jurisdiction (GPE) from contracts automatically. Flag contracts where the MONEY amount exceeds a threshold or where the DATE falls within a certain window.

Southern Star Scenario: You're handed 1,200 maintenance work orders from the past 3 years. Manual analysis would take weeks. NER on work order descriptions → structured table with columns: Equipment Type, Station Location, Incident Date, Repair Cost, Lead Technician. Then you can query: "Show me all valve failures at Texas stations in Q3 that cost more than $50K."
