Word Embeddings — From BoW to Semantic Vectors

Source: Built In + IBM + TechTarget

Part 8 covered Bag of Words (CountVectorizer) and TF-IDF — these represent documents as sparse, high-dimensional vectors where each dimension is one vocabulary word. They're effective and interpretable, but they have a critical flaw: they treat all words as equally unrelated.

The BoW / TF-IDF limitation: "happy" and "joyful" are completely different dimensions in a BoW model. Cosine similarity between a document containing "happy" and one containing "joyful" would be 0 — no overlap. But a human knows these words are semantically nearly identical. Word embeddings solve this.

The Core Idea: Words as Dense Vectors

Word embeddings map each word to a dense vector in N-dimensional space (typically 100–300 dimensions), where semantically similar words are close together in that space.

EXAMPLE: 4-dimensional embedding (real embeddings are 100–300d)
"king" = [0.71, -0.21, 0.98, 0.52]
"queen" = [0.70, -0.19, 0.97, -0.51]
"man" = [0.69, -0.22, 0.07, 0.51]
"woman" = [0.68, -0.20, 0.08, -0.50]
"puppy" = [-0.3, 0.85, -0.12, 0.11]
king and queen are close → royalty cluster
man and woman are close → gender cluster
puppy is far from both → different conceptual space
🔑 What do these numbers actually mean?

The individual numbers in a word vector have no direct human-readable meaning. You can't look at [0.71, -0.21, 0.98, 0.52] and say "0.71 means royalty." The model learned these values automatically — they're just weights that happened to cluster similar words together during training.

What does matter is the distance and direction between vectors. Notice in the example above:

  • Dimensions 1–2 (positions 0 and 1): king, queen, man, woman all have similar values (~0.70, ~-0.21). "Puppy" is very different (-0.3, 0.85). The model learned that dimension 1 captures something like "human-ness."
  • Dimension 3 (position 2): king=0.98, queen=0.97 (high), man=0.07, woman=0.08 (low). This dimension likely captures "social status/royalty."
  • Dimension 4 (position 3): king=0.52, man=0.51 (positive) vs. queen=-0.51, woman=-0.50 (negative). This dimension captures gender.

The king − man + woman ≈ queen trick works because flipping dimension 4 (gender) while keeping dimensions 1–3 (human, royal) gives you queen's vector. The math of meaning falls out of training — you never program in "gender is dimension 4."

The Famous Analogy Property

Word embeddings capture analogical relationships as vector arithmetic — one of the most surprising and famous properties:

Vector Arithmetic in Embedding Space
vec("king") − vec("man") + vec("woman") ≈ vec("queen")

vec("Paris") − vec("France") + vec("Germany") ≈ vec("Berlin")

vec("walking") − vec("walk") + vec("swim") ≈ vec("swimming")

Interpretation: the MODEL learned that "royalty" and "gender" and "conjugation" are geometrically separable dimensions in embedding space — without being told anything about grammar or semantics.

Word2Vec — How It Works

Word2Vec (Google, Mikolov et al., 2013) trains a shallow neural network on a self-supervised task: given a word, predict its context words (or vice versa). The word vectors are the learned weights of that network.

CBOW (Continuous Bag of Words)

Given the surrounding context words, predict the center word. Input: ["The", "___", "sat", "on"] → predict: "cat"

Better for frequent words, faster to train.

Skip-Gram

Given the center word, predict the surrounding context words. Input: "cat" → predict: ["The", "sat", "on"]

Better for rare words, works better with smaller datasets.

from gensim.models import Word2Vec
import pandas as pd

# Example: train on pipeline maintenance work orders
work_orders = [
    "valve pressure drop detected at compression station",
    "meter calibration required at measurement point",
    "compressor shutdown high temperature fault",
    "pipeline pig run scheduled for corrosion inspection",
    "cathodic protection system test at milepost 42",
    "emergency valve closure manual override activated",
    "leak detection alarm compressor station pressure loss",
]
sentences = [order.split() for order in work_orders]

# Train Word2Vec
model = Word2Vec(
    sentences=sentences,
    vector_size=50,    # embedding dimensions (use 100-300 in production)
    window=3,          # context window size (words before/after)
    min_count=1,       # minimum word frequency to include
    workers=4,
    epochs=100
)

# Find similar words
print("Words similar to 'valve':")
for word, similarity in model.wv.most_similar("valve", topn=5):
    print(f"  {word:<20} similarity: {similarity:.3f}")

# Word arithmetic
print("\\n'compressor' - 'station' + 'meter' ≈ ?")
result = model.wv.most_similar(
    positive=["compressor", "meter"],
    negative=["station"]
)
print(f"  → {result[0][0]}")

GloVe — Global Vectors for Word Representation

GloVe (Stanford, Pennington et al., 2014) takes a different approach: instead of training on local context windows, it trains on the global co-occurrence matrix — how often does word i appear in the context of word j across the entire corpus?

Word2Vec

  • Local context window (5 words before/after)
  • Faster to train, works well on small corpora
  • Can train from scratch on your domain data
  • Better for analogical reasoning tasks

GloVe

  • Global co-occurrence statistics
  • Pre-trained vectors on massive corpora (Wikipedia + Gigaword, 6B tokens)
  • Download and use directly — no training needed
  • Often better on similarity benchmarks
import numpy as np

# Using pre-trained GloVe embeddings (download: nlp.stanford.edu/data/glove.6B.zip)
def load_glove(filepath):
    """Load GloVe embeddings from file into a dict."""
    embeddings = {}
    with open(filepath, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        for line in f:
            parts = line.split()
            word = parts[0]
            vector = np.array(parts[1:], dtype='float32')
            embeddings[word] = vector
    return embeddings

# glove = load_glove('glove.6B.100d.txt')  # 100-dim, 6B token trained

# --- spaCy includes GloVe-style vectors in the medium/large models ---
import spacy
nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_md")  # medium model has 300-dim GloVe vectors

# Word similarity via embeddings
print(nlp("valve").similarity(nlp("compressor")))   # ~0.4 (related pipeline equipment)
print(nlp("valve").similarity(nlp("invoice")))       # ~0.1 (unrelated)
print(nlp("pressure").similarity(nlp("stress")))     # ~0.6 (related concept)

# Document similarity (average of token vectors)
doc1 = nlp("high pressure detected in mainline pipeline")
doc2 = nlp("anomalous flow rate at compression station")
doc3 = nlp("quarterly financial report results")
print(doc1.similarity(doc2))  # ~0.7 (both about pipeline operations)
print(doc1.similarity(doc3))  # ~0.3 (different domain)

Static vs. Contextual Embeddings

Critical limitation of Word2Vec/GloVe: They produce static embeddings — one vector per word, regardless of context. "bank" (financial) and "bank" (river) get the SAME vector. This is called the polysemy problem.

BERT solves this with contextual embeddings — the vector for "bank" in "I went to the bank to deposit money" is different from "bank" in "the river bank eroded." Each token's representation is computed in context of the entire sentence.
RepresentationDimensionalityPolysemyWhen to Use
Bag of Words|vocabulary| (sparse, 10K–100K+)No contextBaselines, interpretable models
TF-IDF|vocabulary| (sparse)No contextSearch, document retrieval
Word2Vec / GloVe50–300 (dense)One vector per wordSimilarity, clustering, feature input to DNNs
BERT / transformers768–1024 (contextual)Different vector per contextProduction NLP, fine-tuning for tasks
8b
5

Parse Trees — Syntactic Structure of Sentences

Source: Built In + TechTarget

Parsing reveals the grammatical structure of a sentence — how words group into phrases and how phrases relate to each other. There are two main approaches, each representing a different theory of grammar.

Constituency Parsing

Breaks a sentence into hierarchically nested phrases (constituents). A sentence is a tree where leaves are words and internal nodes are phrase types (NP = noun phrase, VP = verb phrase, PP = prepositional phrase).

S
├── NP: "The valve"
│ ├── DET: "The"
│ └── NN: "valve"
└── VP: "failed during inspection"
    ├── VBD: "failed"
    └── PP: "during inspection"
        ├── IN: "during"
        └── NP: "inspection"

Used by: NLTK's CFG parsers; common in classical NLP

Dependency Parsing

Represents grammatical relationships between individual words as directed arcs. One word is the head; others are dependents. No phrase nodes — just pairwise relationships.

"failed" ← ROOT
"valve" ← nsubj of "failed"
"The" ← det of "valve"
"during" ← prep of "failed"
"inspection" ← pobj of "during"

Used by: spaCy (default parser); better for information extraction

import spacy
nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")

doc = nlp("The high-pressure valve at Station 14 failed during routine inspection")

print("Dependency parse:")
for token in doc:
    print(f"  {token.text:<20} → dep: {token.dep_:<12} → head: '{token.head.text}'")

# Output:
# The                  → dep: det         → head: 'valve'
# high-pressure        → dep: amod        → head: 'valve'
# valve                → dep: nsubj       → head: 'failed'
# at                   → dep: prep        → head: 'valve'
# Station              → dep: pobj        → head: 'at'
# 14                   → dep: nummod      → head: 'Station'
# failed               → dep: ROOT        → head: 'failed'
# during               → dep: prep        → head: 'failed'
# routine              → dep: amod        → head: 'inspection'
# inspection           → dep: pobj        → head: 'during'

# Visualize the dependency parse
from spacy import displacy
displacy.render(doc, style="dep", jupyter=True)

# Extract subject-verb-object triples
for token in doc:
    if token.dep_ == "nsubj":
        subj = token.text
        verb = token.head.text
        # Find direct object of verb
        obj = [t.text for t in token.head.children if t.dep_ == "dobj"]
        print(f"\\nSVO: {subj} → {verb} → {obj}")
Practical use: Relationship extraction. From "The compressor at Elkhart failed due to bearing wear," dependency parsing lets you extract: subject="compressor", verb="failed", cause="bearing wear", location="Elkhart." This is how NLP converts incident reports into structured failure-mode databases.
8b
6

Coreference Resolution & Word Sense Disambiguation

Source: TechTarget + IBM + Built In

These two tasks represent some of the hardest unsolved problems in NLP — where machines still lag significantly behind humans.

Coreference Resolution

Coreference resolution identifies when different expressions in a text refer to the same entity. The pronouns "he," "she," "it," "they," and "this" only have meaning when you can trace what they refer back to.

# The challenge:
text1 = "Dr. Chen reviewed the incident report. She flagged the pressure anomaly."
# "She" refers to "Dr. Chen" — same entity, different surface forms

text2 = """Southern Star submitted its Q3 report to the FERC. The agency reviewed it
and requested additional documentation. The company complied within 30 days."""
# "The agency" = "FERC"
# "it" = "Q3 report"
# "The company" = "Southern Star"
# Without resolving these, you can't track what happened to whom

# spaCy does NOT include coreference resolution by default
# neuralcoref is a popular add-on (but unmaintained)
# Best current option: use a large language model

# Example with spaCy + experimental coref (spaCy v3.5+):
# import spacy
# nlp = spacy.load("en_coreference_web_trf")  # requires transformer model
# doc = nlp(text2)
# for cluster in doc.spans:
#     print(f"Cluster: {list(doc.spans[cluster])}")
Why coreference resolution is hard:
  • "The city council refused the demonstrators a permit because they feared violence." — does "they" refer to the city council or the demonstrators? Humans disagree! This classic example (Winograd Schema) requires real-world reasoning about who typically fears whom
  • "The trophy would not fit in the suitcase because it was too big." — "it" refers to the trophy. Change "big" to "small" and "it" refers to the suitcase. Same syntactic structure, different referent, based purely on world knowledge about size
  • These examples require common sense reasoning about the physical world — far beyond pattern matching

Word Sense Disambiguation (WSD)

Many words have multiple meanings. WSD is the task of identifying which sense a word is used in based on context.

Examples from IBM's NLP documentation:
  • "pen" in "He used a pen to sign the document" → writing instrument
  • "pen" in "The livestock were secured in the pen" → enclosure
  • "bank" in "She deposited $500 at the bank" → financial institution
  • "bank" in "They picnicked on the river bank" → shoreline
  • "bass" in "He plays bass guitar" → musical register
  • "bass" in "We caught a bass at the lake" → fish species
WSD matters for: search relevance (don't return fishing results when user searches "bass guitar"), translation (the German word varies by sense), NER (is "Apple" the company or the fruit?), sentiment (did the reviewer say the movie was "sick" = bad or "sick" = great?) 
from nltk.wsd import lesk
from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize
import nltk
# nltk.download('wordnet')
# nltk.download('punkt')

# Lesk algorithm — overlap between word definition and context
sentence1 = word_tokenize("I went to the bank to deposit money")
sentence2 = word_tokenize("We fished on the bank of the river")

sense1 = lesk(sentence1, 'bank')
sense2 = lesk(sentence2, 'bank')

print(f"Sense in sentence 1: {sense1.name()} — {sense1.definition()}")
# bank.n.01 — sloping land especially the slope beside a body of water
# (Note: Lesk algorithm is simple and often gets this wrong!)
# The accuracy is ~50–60% on standard benchmarks for simple Lesk

print(f"Sense in sentence 2: {sense2.name()} — {sense2.definition()}")

# Modern approach: use BERT contextual embeddings
# The embeddings for "bank" in context 1 vs context 2 will be measurably different
# and you can cluster those embeddings to identify senses
WSD + NER interaction: "Apple released its Q4 earnings" — "Apple" is an ORG (company). "An apple fell from the tree" — "apple" is not a named entity. Standard NER models handle this reasonably well through context, but fail on edge cases: "Amazon is drowning in returns this quarter" — ORG or the river? Context usually makes it clear but automated systems still make these errors.
8b
7

NLP vs. GenAI — Predictive vs. Generative Thinking

Source: Explosion.ai (makers of spaCy)

This module covers one of the most practically important conceptual frameworks for anyone building NLP systems today — a framework developed by the team that makes spaCy, the library you've been using throughout this course.

The key insight from explosion.ai: "When all you have is ChatGPT, all problems look like LLM problems." There's a critical difference between using AI for communication vs. using it for computation.

The Generative vs. Predictive Distinction

🗣 Generative AI (Computers ↔ Humans)

ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini. The output is text meant to be read and evaluated by a human. The human is the final judge of correctness.

Examples:

  • Write a summary of this document
  • Answer this customer question
  • Draft a reply to this email
  • Explain why this code is failing

The human reads the output and decides if it's good. Quality is subjective.

🔧 Predictive AI / Classical NLP (Computers ↔ Computers)

NER, classification, extraction, clustering. The output is a structured prediction fed directly into another system. No human reads it — it goes straight into a database or triggers an action.

Examples:

  • Classify this work order as URGENT / ROUTINE
  • Extract all entity names from this contract
  • Route this customer complaint to the right department
  • Flag this sensor reading as ANOMALY or NORMAL

The output is a label, a list, a score. Quality is objective and measurable (precision/recall/F1).

Why This Distinction Matters for Production Systems

# Scenario: Classify pipeline work orders as URGENT or ROUTINE

# Option A: Use an LLM (generative approach)
import openai
def classify_llm(work_order: str) -> str:
    response = openai.chat.completions.create(
        model="gpt-4",
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": f"Is this work order URGENT or ROUTINE?\\n\\n{work_order}"}]
    )
    return response.choices[0].message.content
# Problems:
# - $0.03/classification × 10,000 work orders/month = $300/month vs. $0 for a local model
# - Response: "Based on the description, this appears to be URGENT..." (need to parse!)
# - 2-3 second latency per call; not suitable for real-time systems
# - Text is sent to OpenAI's servers (data privacy concern for regulated industries)
# - Non-deterministic: same input can get different outputs on different runs

# Option B: Train a text classifier (predictive approach)
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
import pandas as pd

# After labeling ~200 work orders (1-2 hours of annotation work):
work_orders = [
    ("gas smell reported by homeowner near milepost 42", "URGENT"),
    ("routine valve inspection due per 6-month schedule", "ROUTINE"),
    ("pressure drop 15% below normal at station 7", "URGENT"),
    ("update station logbook with monthly readings", "ROUTINE"),
    # ... 196 more labeled examples
]
df = pd.DataFrame(work_orders, columns=["text", "label"])
X, y = df["text"], df["label"]

pipeline = Pipeline([
    ("tfidf", TfidfVectorizer(ngram_range=(1,2))),
    ("clf", LogisticRegression(C=1.0))
])
pipeline.fit(X, y)

# Production characteristics:
# - $0 per inference (runs locally)
# - <1ms latency (1000x faster)
# - Deterministic (same input → same output every time)
# - No data leaves your servers
# - 94% accuracy on held-out test set (measurable, auditable)
The explosion.ai framework in practice: Use LLMs to build NLP systems, not only as NLP systems.
  • Use an LLM to generate training data: "Here are 10 URGENT work orders and 10 ROUTINE ones. Generate 50 more examples of each in the same style." Label 200 examples in minutes, not days
  • Use an LLM to annotate examples: GPT-4 can label training data at 85–90% human accuracy. Use it to bootstrap a dataset, then train a cheap local classifier
  • Use a small trained model in production: A DistilBERT fine-tuned on 1,000 examples often matches GPT-4 accuracy at 1/1000th the cost and 100x the speed

The Six Qualities of Production NLP (Explosion.ai)

🧩 Modular

Each component does one thing well and can be replaced independently. The NER model and the sentiment model are separate units, not one monolithic LLM call.

🔍 Transparent

You can inspect what the system is doing at every step. Black-box LLMs fail here — you can't audit why they made a particular prediction.

📊 Explainable

You can explain predictions to stakeholders. "It was classified URGENT because it matched these keywords at these TF-IDF weights" vs. "the neural network said so."

🔒 Data-Private

Proprietary text never leaves your infrastructure. Critical for regulated industries (FERC-regulated natural gas pipelines have strict data handling rules).

✅ Reliable

Deterministic output. Same input → same output. No "hallucinations." You can write unit tests for your NLP pipeline.

💰 Affordable

Runs on a CPU. Free to infer. A logistics company processing 1M documents/day can't pay $30K/day in API fees.

"Working prototypes are the best arguments." — Explosion.ai

Don't debate whether an LLM or a classical model is better in the abstract. Build both. Measure them. The one with better F1 on your test set wins, regardless of its provenance.
8b
8

NLP Challenges — Why Language Is Hard for Machines

Source: TechTarget + IBM + Built In

Understanding where NLP fails is as important as understanding where it succeeds. These are the documented challenges from your course's reading list — each one explains a category of real production failures.

⚠ Bias in Training Data

NLP models learn from human-generated text, and human language reflects human biases. If resumes from the past 50 years associate software engineering roles with male pronouns, a word embedding will encode "programmer" as closer to "he" than "she." These biases propagate into hiring tools, credit scoring, content moderation.

Example: Word2Vec trained on Google News: "man is to doctor as woman is to nurse" — the model learned this from the data, not because it's true. Amazon's internal recruiting AI was scrapped after it learned to downrank resumes from women's colleges.

🔄 Evolving Language

Language changes constantly. Words acquire new meanings ("sick," "fire," "ghost"), new words emerge ("doomscrolling," "vibe check," "deinfluencing"), and old words fall out of use. A model trained in 2020 may not understand slang from 2024.

Technical response: Regular re-training on fresh corpora; domain-specific fine-tuning after major vocabulary shifts; monitoring model confidence scores on new inputs to detect distribution shift.

🎭 Sarcasm & Tone

Sentiment analysis fails badly on sarcasm. "Oh great, the pipeline is leaking again" — the words "great" should be positive, but the sentence is clearly negative. Irony, sarcasm, and indirect expression require pragmatic understanding that goes beyond surface-level word meanings.

VADER handles some sarcasm via punctuation (!!!), capitalization (GREAT), and contrast words ("but"). Transformer models handle it much better by modeling broader context.

🗣 Accents, Dialects, Code-Switching

NLP models trained primarily on formal written English degrade on regional dialects, non-native speaker patterns, and code-switching (mixing languages in one sentence). African American Vernacular English (AAVE) is systematically misclassified by sentiment analyzers trained on standard English.

Practical implication: If your training data doesn't represent the population your model serves, your model's performance is not what your benchmark says it is.

❓ Ambiguity at Every Level

Language is ambiguous at every linguistic level:

  • Lexical: "bank" (financial? river?) — WSD problem
  • Syntactic: "I saw the man with the telescope" — did I use the telescope to see him, or did he have the telescope? Same words, two parse trees
  • Semantic: "Every child loves some movie" — does every child love the same movie, or each child loves some different movie? Quantifier scope ambiguity
  • Pragmatic: "Can you pass the salt?" is a question syntactically but a request pragmatically

🧠 World Knowledge Gaps

Resolving "The city council refused the demonstrators a permit because they feared violence" requires knowing who typically fears whom in that kind of confrontation. This requires world knowledge and common sense — not just pattern matching over text.

Current state: Large language models are surprisingly good at this but still fail on adversarial examples. They can't be reliably tested to exhaustion because there are infinite such examples.

Domain Vocabulary — The Challenge for Specialized Applications

# Problem: pipeline industry vocabulary is completely absent from general NLP models
# "MMBtu", "compression ratio", "MAOP", "cathodic protection", "pigging",
# "meter calibration", "SCADA", "blowdown" — none of these are in standard word vectors

# spaCy's small model has never seen "MAOP" (Maximum Allowable Operating Pressure)
import spacy
nlp_sm = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")
doc = nlp_sm("The MAOP for this segment was exceeded during the blowdown operation")
for token in doc:
    if not token.has_vector:
        print(f"'{token.text}' — no vector (OOV word)")
# 'MAOP' — no vector (OOV word)
# 'blowdown' — no vector (OOV word)

# Solution options:
# 1. Add custom vectors manually:
nlp_sm.vocab["MAOP"].vector = nlp_sm.vocab["pressure"].vector  # approximate
nlp_sm.vocab["blowdown"].vector = nlp_sm.vocab["depressurization"].vector

# 2. Use a larger pre-trained model that has more vocabulary
nlp_lg = spacy.load("en_core_web_lg")  # 684,000 word vectors

# 3. Train domain-specific Word2Vec on internal documents
# (best long-term solution for a specialized domain like natural gas infrastructure)
Southern Star Practical Note: Any NLP work at a natural gas pipeline company will immediately hit domain vocabulary challenges. Standard pre-trained models have never seen "blowdown," "compressor knock," "MAOP violations," or "EFM data." Your first task on any NLP project would be: gather a corpus of internal documents (work orders, incident reports, maintenance logs) and train domain-adapted word vectors, or fine-tune a base transformer model on that corpus. This is where the real analytics value creation happens.

NLP Industry Applications — Real-World Use Cases

Source: Built In + IBM

NLP is now one of the most commercially impactful AI fields. This module maps NLP capabilities to specific business functions across industries — giving you the vocabulary to describe how techniques connect to business value.

💼 Finance & Banking

  • Sentiment analysis on earnings calls: Classify executive language as confident vs. cautious; predict short-term stock movement from tone
  • Fraud detection: NLP on transaction descriptions; flag anomalous language patterns
  • Contract review: NER extracts parties, dates, obligations; clause classification finds problematic terms
  • Regulatory compliance: Parse FERC/SEC filings; flag deviations from expected language in required disclosures
  • Credit scoring: Text features from loan applications improve default prediction beyond numeric features alone

🏥 Healthcare

  • Clinical note extraction: NER on physician notes → structured data: diagnoses, medications, dosages, allergies
  • Medical coding: Map free-text symptoms to ICD-10 billing codes automatically
  • Drug interaction detection: Parse prescription databases; flag combinations contraindicated in literature
  • Patient sentiment: Analyze post-visit survey text; identify satisfaction drivers beyond the star rating
  • Literature mining: Extract clinical trial results from thousands of papers at scale

⚙ Energy & Utilities

  • Maintenance work order analysis: Classify urgency; cluster by failure mode; predict which equipment types fail in certain seasons
  • Regulatory document processing: Extract compliance deadlines, violation language, penalty amounts from FERC/PHMSA filings
  • Incident report mining: NER on historical incident reports → structured failure database; identify patterns humans missed
  • SCADA alarm analysis: Classify and route alarm text; reduce alarm fatigue through NLP-based deduplication
  • Contract management: Extract terms, renewal dates, pricing from supplier agreements

📦 E-Commerce / Retail

  • Product classification: Map merchant-submitted product descriptions to standardized category taxonomy
  • Review analysis: Aspect-based sentiment — not just "positive/negative" but "positive about delivery, negative about packaging"
  • Query understanding: Semantic search — "cheap running shoes under $50 for wide feet" → structured filter
  • Fake review detection: Linguistic patterns differ between genuine and incentivized reviews
  • Chatbot / customer service: Intent classification → route to right handler

⚖ Legal

  • Contract due diligence: Flag unusual clauses, non-standard risk provisions, missing standard protections
  • Legal research: Semantic search over case law; find precedents similar in substance to a new case
  • Deposition analysis: Identify contradictions in witness testimony across multiple documents
  • Regulatory compliance monitoring: Track new regulations; map them to affected business processes

🎯 HR & Recruiting

  • Resume parsing: NER extracts skills, job titles, companies, education, dates → structured candidate profile
  • Job matching: Semantic similarity between resume text and job description (embedding cosine similarity)
  • Bias auditing: Flag job descriptions with language statistically correlated with demographic skew in applications
  • Employee survey analysis: Topic modeling on open-text responses; identify emerging concerns before attrition
  • Exit interview mining: Cluster reasons for departure; quantify attrition drivers

The NLP Value Chain — From Text to Decision

Input
Raw Text
Work orders, emails, reports, contracts
Preprocess
Clean & Tokenize
Lowercase, regex, spaCy tokenizer
Structure
NER / POS / Parse
Extract entities, relationships
Classify
Model Prediction
Sentiment, urgency, topic, intent
Output
Structured Data
Database, dashboard, alert, API
Action
Business Decision
Route, escalate, report, automate

From spaCy to Transformers — Your Next Step in NLP

Source: IBM + Explosion.ai

You now know the full classical NLP stack. This module maps the path from where you are to production-grade transformer NLP — the skills that separate junior from mid-level NLP practitioners.

The Skill Progression

LevelSkillsToolsCan Do
Foundations
(Part 8)		 Tokenization, TF-IDF, VADER, LDA NLTK, sklearn, spaCy sm Basic text analysis, bag-of-words classification, topic modeling
Intermediate
(Part 8b)		 NER, POS, dependency parsing, word embeddings, coreference spaCy md/lg, gensim Information extraction, semantic similarity, pipeline-ready classifiers
Advanced
(next course)		 Fine-tuning BERT/DistilBERT, transfer learning, custom pipelines 🤗 Transformers, spaCy + transformers Production-quality NLP, domain-adapted models, 90%+ accuracy on custom tasks
Applied AI
(practitioner)		 Prompt engineering, RAG, LLM-assisted annotation LangChain, LlamaIndex, OpenAI API Build NLP products that use LLMs wisely alongside classical models

Quick Start: Fine-Tune BERT for Text Classification

from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForSequenceClassification
from transformers import TrainingArguments, Trainer
import torch
from datasets import Dataset

# Task: classify pipeline work orders as URGENT / ROUTINE
# After gathering ~1,000 labeled examples:

MODEL_NAME = "distilbert-base-uncased"  # 66M params, 97% of BERT's accuracy, 2x faster
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL_NAME)
model = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained(MODEL_NAME, num_labels=2)

# Tokenize your data
def tokenize(batch):
    return tokenizer(batch["text"], padding=True, truncation=True, max_length=128)

train_data = {
    "text": ["pressure anomaly detected", "routine valve greasing scheduled", ...],
    "label": [1, 0, ...]  # 1=URGENT, 0=ROUTINE
}
dataset = Dataset.from_dict(train_data).map(tokenize, batched=True)

# Training — just 3 epochs is usually enough with pre-trained weights
training_args = TrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./work-order-classifier",
    num_train_epochs=3,
    per_device_train_batch_size=16,
    evaluation_strategy="epoch",
    save_strategy="epoch",
    load_best_model_at_end=True,
    metric_for_best_model="f1",
)

trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=dataset,
)
trainer.train()

# After training: 92–96% accuracy is typical with 1,000 examples
# Cost: runs on a free Colab GPU, takes ~20 minutes
# Production inference: <10ms per document on CPU

spaCy + Transformers — The Best of Both Worlds

# spaCy v3+ integrates transformers directly into the pipeline
# Install: pip install spacy-transformers

# Using a transformer-based spaCy model (much more accurate than sm/md/lg)
import spacy

# Download: python -m spacy download en_core_web_trf
nlp_trf = spacy.load("en_core_web_trf")  # based on RoBERTa

# Same spaCy API, but backed by a transformer encoder
doc = nlp_trf("FERC reported that Southern Star's pipeline pressure exceeded safe limits")
for ent in doc.ents:
    print(f"{ent.text} → {ent.label_}")
# FERC → ORG
# Southern Star → ORG
# (much better accuracy than sm model for domain-specific text)
Transfer learning is what makes NLP tractable for data analysts. Pre-trained models have already learned the structure of language from billions of sentences. You only need to teach them your specific task with a few hundred to a few thousand examples. The explosion.ai team's key insight: "Transfer learning has made annotation take hours instead of months." This is why NLP is now accessible to data analysts, not just ML researchers.
⚠ Interview / Portfolio Advice
"What would you do to improve the work order NLP system after getting 80% accuracy?"
Walk them through the ML improvement loop:
  1. Inspect misclassified examples — is there a pattern? (Domain vocabulary? Short/ambiguous text?)
  2. Add training data, specifically targeting the error categories identified
  3. Upgrade from TF-IDF + LogReg to DistilBERT fine-tuned — usually +8–15 F1 points
  4. Consider the costs: labeling more data vs. switching to a bigger model vs. engineering features specifically for failure cases
  5. Monitor precision vs. recall tradeoff — for URGENT classification, false negatives (calling URGENT → ROUTINE) are much worse than false positives; adjust threshold accordingly

Part 8b — Summary & Integration With Part 8

What you now know (Part 8 + Part 8b combined):
  • The full NLP pipeline: clean → tokenize → remove stopwords → lemmatize → vectorize → model → evaluate
  • Text representations: BoW, TF-IDF (sparse), Word2Vec/GloVe (dense static), BERT (dense contextual)
  • Core NLP tasks: Sentiment (VADER), Topic modeling (LDA), NER, POS tagging, Dependency parsing, WSD, Coreference resolution
  • The evolution: Rules → Statistical → Deep Learning → Transformers → Foundation Models
  • When to use what: Classical ML (cheap, fast, explainable, deterministic) vs. LLMs (better accuracy, expensive, non-deterministic, privacy concerns)
  • Industry applications: Energy, finance, healthcare, legal, HR — and how text becomes structured data becomes business value
  • What NLP still can't do reliably: Coreference in ambiguous cases, sarcasm, domain vocabulary without fine-tuning, true common-sense reasoning

Quick Reference: Which Tool for Which Task?

TaskStarting PointWhen to Upgrade
Sentiment on social media / short textVADER (lexicon-based)Switch to fine-tuned transformer if <80% accuracy on your domain
Sentiment on structured reviewsTF-IDF + Logistic RegressionDistilBERT if reviews are complex/ironic
Topic discovery (unsupervised)LDABERTopic (transformer-based clustering) for sharper topics
Named entity extractionspaCy en_core_web_md NERTrain custom NER for domain-specific entities
Text classification (custom labels)TF-IDF + Logistic Regression baselineFine-tune DistilBERT for >90% accuracy with 500+ examples
Semantic similarity / searchTF-IDF cosine similaritySentence-BERT (sentence-transformers library) for semantic search
Information extraction (relationships)spaCy dependency parse + rule matchingFine-tune for precision tasks
Open-ended text generation / summarizationUse an LLM API (GPT-4, Claude)Fine-tune open-source model if data privacy required
